## 📚 Paper List

- **[DexRemo: A Force-Sensing Hand Platform and Task Suite for Contact-Rich Removal Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15981)**  `arXiv:2611.15981`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Liman Wang, Hanyang Zhong, Sun Zhaole, Wenting Cao, Babar Jamil, Andy Tyrrell, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many manipulation tasks require separating adhered, embedded, contaminated, or mechanically constrained targets. We study this setting as removal manipulation, where progress depends on evolving contact states that are only partially observable from vision and kinematics. We introduce DexRemo, a three-finger robotic hand with high-force actuation, modular fingertips, and three-axis fingertip force sensing, together with a nine-scenario real-robot task suite spanning adhesive detachment, surface cleaning and self-cleaning, and occluded retrieval. To make multi-finger force observations easier to fuse, we express fingertip forces in a shared wrist-centered frame, giving force components consistent geometric meaning across fingers and time. Using an Action Chunking Transformer backbone, policies fuse multi-view vision, proprioception, fingertip pose, and force observations to predict closed-loop action chunks from demonstrations. Evaluation uses six core benchmark tasks for controlled fingertip-input and force-frame ablations, plus three extension scenarios for additional held-out coverage. Across these evaluations, the learned policies achieve consistent removal performance, and ablations show that wrist-frame forces, especially with fingertip pose, improve stability and generalization over local-frame force inputs and no-force baselines. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[BiGym 2.0: Benchmarking Learned and Agent-Developed Policies for Humanoid Household Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07594)**  `arXiv:2610.07594`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Zexi Zhang, Zecheng Zhu, Zidong Chen, Zulkhuu Tuya, Stephen James_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid household manipulation requires the arms to act while the body balances, steps and changes posture. We present BiGym 2.0, an adaptation of BiGym for the Unitree G1 across 20 household tasks using a unified whole-body controller for demonstration and evaluation. The suite provides 60 native human virtual-reality demonstrations per task with synchronised multi-camera views and full-body execution records. We benchmark vision-language-action fine-tuning, imitation learning, demo-driven reinforcement learning, and cold-start coding agents given the interaction budget of online reinforcement learning. With the same onboard views, proprioception and whole-body controller for every method, vision-language-action fine-tuning has the highest nine-task mean, and agent-developed programs outperform every demo-driven reinforcement learning baseline on this mean and lead on bimanual reaching. Cross-workspace stacking remains open, $\pi_{0.5}$ stays low on pick-box, and multi-object transport is hard for imitation learning, demo-driven reinforcement learning and coding agents. All environments, human demonstrations, and evaluation traces are open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Makes a VLA Reactive? A Study of Execution Strategies for Dynamic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15491)**  `arXiv:2611.15491`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuoyue Li, Zhendong Han, Pankhuri Vanjani, Wenting Li, Rudolf Lioutikov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models predict chunks of future actions, and when objects move, those chunks go stale before they are executed. We study what makes a pretrained VLA reactive, using \pihalf{} on two dynamic simulation benchmarks and three real-robot tasks. We vary the prediction and execution horizons, compare synchronous and asynchronous inference, test methods that deliver actions sooner or keep successive chunks continuous, and adapt the policy with motion cues and latent-space reinforcement learning. The best prediction horizon is about one second, and the best execution horizon depends on the checkpoint. Short horizons help more with asynchronous execution, and methods that keep the trajectory continuous outperform those that only reduce latency. Adapting the policy repairs targeted failures, but on the conveyor task changing the execution horizon gained as much. Reactivity depends on the execution pipeline as much as on the policy, so both should be evaluated together.
  </details>

- **[UAV-Flow++: A Unified Suite for Benchmarking and Learning Language-Guided UAV Flight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17163)**  `arXiv:2611.17163`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Donglin Yang, Xiangyu Wang, Yantong Zhong, Wenhao Zheng, Zihan Ding, Yue Liao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-guided UAV navigation has largely focused on long-horizon route planning, leaving short-range, object-relative flight maneuvers underexplored. To address this gap, we formulate Flying-on-a-Word (Flow), a task requiring a UAV to convert concise natural-language instruction, egocentric visual observation, and current state dynamics into continuous flight actions. Flow encompasses both primitive motion commands and complex object-interactive behaviors, assessing motion intent comprehension and spatial context grounding. To enable policy learning and closed-loop evaluation, we introduce UAV-Flow, a comprehensive dataset comprising 30,692 expert-piloted real-world trajectories across eight distinct motion categories, alongside 10,109 simulation trajectories in the UAV-Flow-Sim environment equipped with task-specific completion criteria. Furthermore, we develop FlowVLA, which undergoes imitation pretraining on mixed real-sim demonstrations and online post-training via Tail-Aware GRPO (TGRPO). By leveraging group reward distributions, TGRPO effectively reinforces high-reward action sequences. Evaluated on 273 held-out UAV-Flow-Sim episodes, pretrained FlowVLA achieves a mean success rate of 75.58%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 6.72 percentage points, which TGRPO post-training further elevates to 93.16% with superior sample efficiency. Physical UAV experiments demonstrate robust execution of object-relative commands in both indoor and outdoor real-world scenes. Together, Flow establishes a novel benchmark and learning framework for language-conditioned flight maneuvers.
  </details>

- **[MOSAIC: Modular Obstacle and Spatial-Aware Framework for Manipulation In Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15937)**  `arXiv:2611.15937`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Richard Shi, Mohammad Mahdavian, Mehrdad Momeni Zadeh, Mohammed Elmahgiubi, Amir Rasouli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance in robotic manipulation, yet most rely primarily on 2D visual observations and language instructions, leaving scene geometry and obstacle relationships to be inferred implicitly. This limitation is particularly problematic in cluttered environments, where small spatial errors can lead to unintended contacts or collisions. We propose MOSAIC, a modular spatial- and obstacle-aware framework that can be integrated with pretrained VLA policies to improve object-level scene understanding and action generation. Our method augments the VLA backbone with an auxiliary semantic grounding task that predicts role-specific masks for the target object, destination region, and surrounding obstacles. We further combine the target and destination masks with depth estimated from 3D scene reconstruction to construct role-specific geometric tokens that are injected directly into the action expert. In addition, we propose obstacle-aware attention supervision that emphasizes obstacles near the robot body during task execution. The resulting attention features are subsequently injected into later action-expert layers to further guide action generation. Together, these components provide explicit semantic, geometric, and obstacle-aware cues while preserving the underlying pretrained VLA architecture. We evaluate our framework on pretrained VLA model using the RoboPRO benchmark, including visual perturbation tests, and further validate it on real-world manipulation tasks. MOSAIC improves the success rate by 5.1% in cluttered scenes, and by 9.7% under visual perturbations, with gains of up to 25.0% in the most challenging environment. We will release our code and dataset upon acceptance at the following Github page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TinyAstral: Transferring Self-Evolving Harnesses to Tiny Generalist Manipulation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41216)**  `arXiv:2610.41216`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Kehong Gong, Xiaoxu Zheng, Guanli Hou, Seunghun Lee, Mengqi Guo, Runjia Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot agents built on vision-language models depend on both the planner and its harness: the tools, feedback, memory, and recovery logic around it. Can improvements to a large teacher's harness be carried into a compact agent with lower inference compute cost? We present TinyAstral, a two-stage approach. We first freeze an open-weight teacher and improve its harness through recursive self-improvement (RSI): frontier LLM agents read the teacher's trajectories and propose harness edits, and each edit is accepted only if it passes a paired statistical test. We then freeze the final harness, collect teacher trajectories with reasoning and failure recovery, and distill a small student that replaces the teacher in the same harness. On RoboTwin, harness evolution raises the teacher's success from 33.4% to 63.6%; the final 4B student reaches 62.8%. Across four harness versions, distilled 4B students score close to their corresponding teachers, and later versions yield stronger students, showing that harness evolution gains carry into compact models. The harness gains also transfer to other frozen planners without retraining. On identical episodes, the final 4B student outperforms open 4B agents, the open 27B harness-RSI model SenseNova-RoboRSI, and our own teacher model placed in three competitors' harnesses. It stays close to its teacher on all three training benchmarks, and on three benchmarks never used for RSI or training it scores close to a 552B planner running the same harness. On the measured deployment episodes, it uses about one fifth of its teacher's accelerator time. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TEMPO: Semantic-Action Decoupled RL Post-Training for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07314)**  `arXiv:2608.07314`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Ziheng Liu, Quantao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models are commonly adapted to downstream manipulation tasks via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or online reinforcement learning(RL) post-training. SFT is prone to distribution mismatch, and existing RL approaches typically optimize all trainable components within a single RL loop at a uniform update rate, although these components play distinct functional roles. We propose TEMPO, a semantic-action decoupled, two-timescale RL post-training framework for VLA models. TEMPO freezes the pretrained vision-language backbone and adapts two components with dedicated RL loops: the semantic projection layer, which produces the latent action conditioning the action expert, and the low-level action expert itself. The semantic projection layer is updated infrequently and the action expert frequently, which aims to limit latent-action drift while allowing the action expert to learn from online feedback. Our analyses show that, compared with equal update frequencies, less frequent semantic updates reduce latent-action drift and improve policy performance. Experiments on the CALVIN benchmark and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that TEMPO outperforms both pretrained state-of-the-art VLA models and the RL post-training baseline, while reaching and maintaining higher evaluation rewards on three real-world tasks.
  </details>

- **[AgriSense: Vision-Language Decision Support and Robotic Intervention in a Virtual Greenhouse Copy title](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16752)**  `arXiv:2611.16752`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Lyes Saad Saoud, Oualid Doukhi, Reza Ghorbani, Moussa Ayyash_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous greenhouse management increasingly requires robotic systems that integrate perception, navigation, environmental regulation, and plant-level intervention. This paper presents AgriSense, a framework combining an Isaac Sim virtual greenhouse, a quadruped mobile manipulator, RGB and LiDAR-inertial sensing, environmental control, and supervised vision-language decision support. Frozen DINOv2 and BERT encoders, together with structured environmental inputs, support joint prediction of twelve environmental references, a target zone, and an intervention flag. Checkpoint replay reproduces 41/43 zone decisions. Across five seeds on a source-grouped test set, the full, image-removed, and language-removed configurations each achieve 43/44 correct zone decisions, providing no evidence of incremental multimodal zone-accuracy improvement. The full model achieves mean absolute errors of 0.249 degrees C for temperature, 2.030 percentage points for humidity, and 0.997 percentage points for soil moisture. Sensor-only inputs match full-model zone accuracy, while language-only inputs achieve 68.64%. External real-image benchmarks reveal distribution-shift limitations. Five offline Point-LIO routes yield rigidly aligned trajectory errors of 5.22-17.32 mm. Of 40 virtual-arm targets, 32 satisfy geometric acceptance criteria and eight are rejected at boundaries. Six long-horizon climate cases and fault protocols characterize integration limits. Navigation and integrated treatment rely on simulator-state inputs; physical greenhouse performance and agronomic treatment efficacy remain unvalidated. The contribution is an evaluated decision-support and robotic-intervention framework for a virtual greenhouse.
  </details>

- **[JustRUA: A Controlled Study of GPT-6 Robot-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16009)**  `arXiv:2611.16009`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zefan Wang, Yufan Dang, Xuanwei Shi, Mingqi Gao, Cheng Yin, SikYuen Tam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GPT-6 Astra can drive a robot arm directly through a fixed harness, and scores of such robot-use agents on RoboDojo have risen quickly since. Reported systems differ in harness and test subset and often change several designs at once, so it is hard to tell which choices produce the gains. We take these designs apart and test them in one framework, along the harness around the model, the action interface, the reuse of experience and the scene geometry given to the model. A lean harness that keeps the full episode history and stops each motion early goes a long way. Reusing experience changes how the model acts but is not a free lunch, and scene geometry helps most, even when depth is predicted from RGB alone. JustRUA starts from the official L3 harness and keeps only what matters most. Even with no learned policy, self-improvement loop or task-specific tuning, this simple harness raises the score on the RoboDojo simulation benchmark substantially, from 29.7 to 50.3.
  </details>

- **[Decoupled Asynchronous System for Humanoids (DASH): Latency-Agnostic Scaling of Vision-Language-Action Models for Humanoid Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11854)**  `arXiv:2611.11854`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Bingzhang Wang, Nathan Zhao, Jialeng Ni, Jiayin Zheng, Yin Zhang, Kehua Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models pair a vision-language backbone with an action head, but in closed-loop control the backbone sits on the control-critical path: while it computes, the robot keeps moving, so each executed action is conditioned on an increasingly stale observation. A larger backbone therefore directly blocks reactivity, especially in humanoid dexterous manipulation. We present DASH, a Decoupled Asynchronous System for Humanoids that removes this coupling. DASH splits the policy into a slow vision-language model (System 2) and a fast flow-matching action head (System 1) that run as concurrent processes over the LCM communication protocol, so the control rate is set by the action head alone, independent of backbone size. The action head fuses stale semantics from a frozen backbone with fresh vision from its own ResNet encoder, desynchronized training exposes it to the staleness it meets at deployment, and an adaptive temporal aggregator aligns overlapping action chunks under variable inference latency. On an asynchronous RoboTwin benchmark that charges inference latency in simulated time, DASH holds 20 Hz control rate from 0.8B to 8B backbones while the synchronous rate falls from 9.7 to 4.1 Hz, with higher success at every size. On a real humanoid with dexterous hands, DASH achieves clearly higher pick-and-place success than the synchronous pipeline.
  </details>

- **[XBot-Omni: An Omni-Modal Model for Embodied Understanding and Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17160)**  `arXiv:2611.17160`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kerui Gu, Xing Fu, Shuoyang Sun, Cheng Peng, Jingru Duan, Huayu Gu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied interaction requires an agent to integrate multimodal perception with verbal and nonverbal behaviors to effectively communicate its intentions, including speech, body motion, and facial expressions. We present XBot-Omni, a Thinker--Talker--Actor architecture that extends the Omni paradigm to robotic modalities, including body motion and facial expressions, with ``X'' representing the extensibility to robotic-specific modalities. The Thinker processes text, images, audio, and video to formulate contextually appropriate responses, which are then realized by the Talker and Actor through speech and coordinated bodily expressions, respectively. In this report, we innovatively introduce the concept of ``Actor'' as a dedicated component for embodied agents: a coordinated, streaming module that leverages the native representations of the Thinker and Talker within an end-to-end pipeline, introducing only modest latency overhead. This unified architecture enables multimodal generation conditioned on omni-modal inputs directly to prevent information loss in staged designs. We comprehensively evaluate XBot-Omni across seven benchmarks, demonstrating strong multimodal understanding capabilities on both general-domain and robot-centric videos, as well as high-quality, natural, and well-coordinated motion generation. Furthermore, our results validate its capability to generate embodied multimodal responses directly from omni inputs, meeting the diverse requirements of embodied interaction. XBot-Omni enables robots not only to perceive the omni world but also to express themselves through speech and bodily behaviors, extending Omni models from conversational interaction to embodied communication in physical environments.
  </details>

- **[CT-SGAL-LIO: Causal Continuous Time Refinement of Discrete LiDAR Inertial Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16918)**  `arXiv:2611.16918`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nam Tran, Thu Tran, Toan Nguyen, William J Beksi, Tuan Dang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State-of-the-art LiDAR-inertial odometry methods, including those on richer state manifolds, achieve strong global accuracy, yet their pose stream alternates between propagation and correction and can oscillate over short horizons even when globally accurate. Most existing methods follow these approaches: they either modify the filter to enable faster updates or estimate a continuous-time trajectory from raw LiDAR and inertial data. The latter involves extra optimization, delaying state updates and becoming less robust during rapid rotations or accelerations. We propose CT-SGAL-LIO, a continuous-time refinement module that smooths the posterior pose stream of a discrete SGal(3) estimator without modifying the estimator itself. The key novelty is to perform continuous-time trajectory refinement directly on the projected SGal(3) posterior: we fit a cubic trajectory over a bounded window of recent poses and evaluate it at a small fixed lag. This places the query time within the observed interval, so the refined pose is obtained by interpolation rather than extrapolation, while preserving causal operation. CT-SGAL-LIO improves relative trajectory accuracy on 17 sequences from three public benchmarks. Across 17 sequences from GRAND-TOUR, MCD, and GEODE, CT-SGAL-LIO improves short-term motion accuracy while largely preserving global accuracy. RTE improves by up to 70% on GRAND-TOUR and on all seven GEODE sequences, with 9.9-51.1% reductions. A 19.5-min R-Campus run also reduces posterior jumps by 85.4% with only a 4.7 mm change in the end-to-start gap. We plan to release our source code and results to support community validation and reproducibility.
  </details>

- **[Informative Path Planning with Gaussian Processes: Error Bounds, Algorithms, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16877)**  `arXiv:2611.16877`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kalvik Jakkala, Jennifer Wakulicz, Donald G. Dansereau, Srinivas Akella, Lantao Liu, Jason M. O'Kane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate environmental monitoring relies on Informative Path Planning (IPP) to deploy autonomous systems that maximize information gain. While Gaussian Processes (GPs) provide a robust mathematical foundation for modeling the environment, contemporary IPP approaches remain hindered by heuristic operational constraints and the lack of simultaneous theoretical guarantees for worst-case estimation error and approximation quality. Rather than introducing yet another heuristic, this paper presents a rigorous theoretical analysis of existing error bounds and algorithms to further develop the mathematical foundation for IPP.By evaluating probabilistic and deterministic bounds on reconstruction error, we establish that predictive variance is the theoretically ideal optimization objective for GP-based IPP. Furthermore, we analyze GreedyCover, a recent approach for optimizing predictive variance, and demonstrate that its near-optimal approximation guarantee matches the fundamental hardness-of-approximation limit for the problem. Comprehensive benchmarking confirms that this approach delivers superior estimation accuracy and computational efficiency compared to prominent information-theoretic objectives and optimization methods used in IPP. Finally, this theoretical analysis establishes a rigorous, near-optimal foundation for discrete offline IPP under bounded uncertainty constraints, offering clear and principled guidance for future work in continuous-space and online formulations.
  </details>

- **[NSDL: Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor for Multi-Robot Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16671)**  `arXiv:2611.16671`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xincheng Tan, Ke Wang, Yining Di, Minfeng Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning for a heterogeneous robot team from a scene image and an instruction involves two kinds of decisions. Some must be judged from the scene: which goals are already met, how the task splits, and which robot does what. Others follow from these and can be computed: prerequisite actions, handoffs between robots, and timing. We argue that each kind should go to the component suited to it: a small vision-language model learns the first, and a deterministic solver computes the second. In NSDL (Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor), the trained model writes its judgments as a plan sketch, which lists the goals that still need action and the intended steps in order, each with a robot. The solver expands the sketch into an executable joint plan. The model calls two tools, a detector for evidence and the solver, and is trained with the solver in the loop. We evaluate on the public VIKI-L2 benchmark and on LOCUS-Bench, a new benchmark that grades the two kinds of difficulty separately. NSDL outperforms end-to-end planners and formalize-then-plan baselines on both benchmarks and with three backbones from 3B to 8B; the 7B model reaches 97.0% out-of-distribution accuracy on VIKI-L2. Ordering and scheduling errors nearly disappear once the solver writes the structure; the remaining failures are misjudged states, and most goals the model misses are ones it never lists. Code, model weights, and LOCUS-Bench are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[STVLA: Spatial-aware and Temporal-memory forVision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16589)**  `arXiv:2611.16589`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhuo Han, Yeqiang Wang, Xinran Wang, Hongwei Han, Yi Xiong, Zunian Wan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation often requires both spatial scene understanding and task history. However, many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely primarily on current observations, without explicitly integrating spatial structure and task history. Appearance-based memories can be sensitive to occlusion and viewpoint changes, while spatial representations of the current scene alone do not preserve how the scene has changed during task execution. We introduce STVLA, a framework that incorporates features from a pretrained visual geometry foundation model into temporal memory alongside visual representations. This allows the policy to recall past scene structure as context for current action decisions. To select informative history, STVLA uses a mathematically grounded scoring rule that balances relevance to the current observation with complementary evidence from memory. Across evaluations on LIBERO, LIBERO-Mem, MIKASA-Robo, and SimplerEnv Bridge, STVLA achieves the highest average success rate among the compared methods on each benchmark. On real-world block stacking, object organization, and phone packaging, it improves success over $\pi_{0.5}$ by $58.33$, $30.00$, and $35.00$ percentage points, respectively. These results support integrating scene geometry into temporal memory to improve manipulation requiring both spatial precision and task history. Project website: \href{this https URL}{this https URL}
  </details>

- **[RGB-D SLAM on the Edge: Benchmarking and Profiling RTAB-Map and ORB-SLAM3 on the Raspberry Pi 5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16472)**  `arXiv:2611.16472`  `cs.RO`  
  _Himanshu Patil, Jaideep Joshi, Rohit Saluja, Ganesh Ramakrishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Micro aerial vehicles that operate without GPS often run SLAM on ARM-based edge boards, yet there is little published data on how standard RGB-D SLAM systems behave on such hardware. We benchmark RTAB-Map and ORB-SLAM3 on the Raspberry Pi 5 using six TUM RGB-D sequences at three image resolutions, recording tracking coverage, trajectory error, time per frame, memory, CPU load and temperature for every run, and we profile both systems at the stage and function level. RTAB-Map was configured to process and keep every frame, so its back end runs on every frame rather than at its default rate of 1 Hz. On the sequences that every configuration tracked, its front end takes 38-74 ms per frame and its full pipeline 150-462 ms, compared with 13-45 ms and 26-62 ms for ORB-SLAM3. RTAB-Map's main cost is optimizing a pose graph that keeps growing, because its working memory is unbounded by default; ORB-SLAM3's local bundle adjustment costs about as much as its tracking but runs in a parallel thread. At quarter resolution, both systems lose tracking on most sequences, and runs that lose tracking finish sooner and report lower error on the poses they keep, so time and accuracy must be read alongside coverage. Timing is repeatable to within 1-5%, but trajectory error varies by up to 27% between identical runs, so the accuracy differences between the two systems are inconclusive. No thermal throttling occurred.
  </details>

- **[ManiLadder: Benchmarking Robot Manipulation Through a Categorized and Multi-Difficulty Level Task Ladder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16374)**  `arXiv:2611.16374`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chongkai Gao, Jieao Shi, Zhaoyu Mu, Yudi Lin, Xuanwei Liu, Wei Ken Ng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in robotic manipulation have enabled increasingly complex and dexterous behaviors, yet existing benchmarks still focus on simple pick-and-place tasks and lack a systematic framework for measuring progress in robot learning along the axis of task difficulty. We present ManiLadder, a large-scale and organized simulation benchmark for robotic manipulation. ManiLadder contains 112 carefully designed tasks stratified into 4 difficulty levels, with 8 categories in each level, spanning rigid-body and deformable-object manipulation, single-arm and dual-arm settings, and gripper and dexterous-hand end-effectors. To calibrate difficulty, we fix the data amount the same for every task and use a unified flow- matching imitation learning policy under the same training and evaluation protocols, then use its evaluation success rate as an operational empirical difficulty signal. All tasks are iteratively refined until they fall into target difficulty ranges, which are then verified across different random seeds, policies, and agentic learning systems. We further analyze the task properties, compare different policy performances and training settings, analyze task transfer and data scaling behaviors, and conduct real-world experiments showing that the designed tasks preserve their difficulty ordering beyond simulation. ManiLad- der provides a calibrated ladder for measuring, comparing, and advancing robotic manipulation capabilities. More results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Video-Action Models for Data-Efficient Industrial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15974)**  `arXiv:2611.15974`  `cs.RO`  
  _Eugenio Chisari, Stefanos Charalambous, Oliver Sanchez, Thomas Umbach, Michael Gentner, Fabio Quattrini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial manipulation requires precise alignment and controlled handling of rigid, deformable, and flexible components. Yet, the benefits of pretrained visuomotor policies remain underexplored in settings where limited access to specialized hardware, components, and fixtures constrains demonstration collection and evaluation. Understanding task-specific data requirements is important for assessing their scalability across diverse processes on the factory floor. We investigate whether video pretraining can support reliable industrial manipulation with limited action data. We propose FLUX-mimic, a video-action model, motivated by the potential of video pretraining to capture motion and interaction patterns that enable sample-efficient training. FLUX-mimic is built on a backbone pretrained on large-scale web and industrial task data, and an action expert to generate robot trajectories. We train our policy by joint video-action training. Across four automotive manufacturing benchmark tasks and two bimanual robot platforms, FLUX-mimic outperforms all considered baselines on the dexterous robot platform and matches pi_{0.5}'s average success rate on the gripper-based robot platform. On a deformable part kitting task, FLUX-mimic reaches 77.5% success with as little as two hours of task-specific action-labeled demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[ActionCodec2: A Streaming Action Tokenizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15490)**  `arXiv:2611.15490`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zibin Dong, Yicheng Liu, Shiduo Zhang, Tianyuan Yuan, Baijun Ye, Jinqiu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most action tokenizers for autoregressive vision language action (VLA) models compress motion into chunks that must be decoded in full before their actions become executable. We introduce ActionCodec2, a non-neural streaming tokenizer that releases actions incrementally while keeping trajectory reconstruction error within a prescribed tolerance. Its encoder quantizes each step relative to the decoder's reconstructed state, so the selected motion primitive corrects residual error rather than allowing errors from independently quantized increments to accumulate. Finding the smallest such primitive set is a geometric covering problem; we derive the exact minimum for translation and constant-factor-tight bounds for rotation. Set-BPE then losslessly merges co-occurring primitive symbols across coordinates and neighboring steps, without a fixed coordinate ordering. A universal version fitted on 20k hours of data from 24 embodiments averages 8.8 tokens per second for 15 Hz end-effector motion on this corpus. With the same VLA backbone, ActionCodec2 outperforms the compared tokenizers on three simulation benchmarks and in zero-shot real-robot evaluation. Its streaming representation allows policies to incorporate state feedback within an action chunk, substantially improving performance over open-loop execution under randomized dynamics, external disturbances, and moving objects. The same tokens also can serve as a direct control interface for VLM agents, which compose successful action sequences into reusable macros. These agent-built macros reduce control decisions and thinking-token usage across attempts and transfer to a held-out task. More results are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[SPARC: RoboCup Standard Platform for Autonomous Rescue Competition with Initial Datasets, Software, and Simulation Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15443)**  `arXiv:2611.15443`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuezhong Wang, Bichi Zhang, Zhongli Yin, Pingcong Li, Raimund Edlinger, Sören Schwertfeger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The RoboCup Standard Platform for Autonomous Rescue Competition (SPARC) is a new competition under development of the RoboCup Rescue Robot League that uses the Unitree A2-W as a common robot platform. SPARC aims to make research on artificial intelligence for autonomous rescue robots accessible to a broader research community, including groups without expertise in developing and maintaining custom robot hardware. By providing a capable standard platform and established benchmark environments, researchers can focus on autonomous navigation, perception, manipulation, and embodied AI for challenging disaster-response scenarios. This paper introduces the SPARC competition, its benchmark environment, and the Unitree A2-W standard platform. Furthermore, we present the first open SPARC datasets collected during the IEEE ICRA 2026 Legged Robot Challenges and RoboCup Rescue 2026, release an open-source software stack for autonomous navigation, and provide initial simulation assets representing RoboCup Rescue test environments. Together, these resources aim to lower the barrier to entry for autonomous rescue robotics and facilitate reproducible research and collaborative development on a common hardware platform.
  </details>

- **[Decide Before You Move: Diffusion-Generated Search Reference for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation in Semi-Static Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15093)**  `arXiv:2611.15093`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuxuan Feng, Fujing Xie, Sören Schwertfeger, Hermann Blum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-world navigation requires a robot to locate a user-specified target in an indoor scene under open-vocabulary queries. Realistic indoor environments are semi-static: walls and furniture largely persist while smaller objects move, relocate, or vanish between visits. Prior-map systems answer queries from object poses recorded at reconstruction time, which expire as soon as objects move, whereas online methods re-derive where to look from current observations every few steps, often through expensive calls to a vision--language model. Assuming that walls and large furniture do not move, we keep their map to plan paths, but use a target--furniture co-occurrence prior to decide where to search. We present PreNav, an ObjectNav framework on semi-static environments. Rather than invoking a vision-language model at each step, PreNav generates a single global search reference before the robot moves: an LLM converts target--furniture co-occurrence into a spatial prior, a conditional diffusion model turns that prior and the persistent structure into an ordered centerline with a band around it, and the robot approaches the target once it is detected online. Experiments on a Gibson ObjectNav benchmark show that PreNav outperforms a strong online baseline under matched structural priors, using a generator trained only on room-level ScanNet++ scenes and applied to floor-level Gibson without fine-tuning. Real-world experiments under object relocation and open-vocabulary queries further validate the approach.
  </details>

- **[Genie Sim Benchmark: Real-to-Sim Fidelity and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15020)**  `arXiv:2611.15020`  `cs.RO`  
  _Di Yang, Da Huang, Yang Lv, Fan Yang, Yi Lu, Zheyuan Xing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We evaluate Genie Sim Benchmark through paired real-simulation tasks and complementary system-identification studies. Across 22 robot-task pairs spanning 16 task names, task-specific pi0.5 policies fine-tuned on real-robot data achieve a descriptive Score correlation of 0.9686, MAE 0.0405, RMSE 0.0520, with simulation-real differences from -0.110 to +0.080. G2 crsB experiments assess long-horizon closed-loop position and joint-side torque agreement, while five representative object cases examine identification and cross-engine contact transfer. A separate 32.6503 s teleoperation trajectory, excluded from mechanical identification and servo-gain search, evaluates external position targets through simulated motion-controller command shaping and a calibrated position servo. Across both arms, per-joint position RMSE spans 0.00614-0.03522 rad and current-derived torque RMSE spans 0.73545-4.00099 N m. Native PhysX adaptations recover three recorded interaction tasks, with case-dependent alignment gains. These results support task-specific simulation evaluation through joint assessment of task outcomes and physical-response consistency.
  </details>

- **[ActPrune: Action-Grounded Visual Token Pruning for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08827)**  `arXiv:2611.08827`  `cs.RO`  
  _Sungwon Moon, Minji Kim, Hyemin Boo, Daeun Song, Jiyoung Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models perform versatile manipulation tasks from language instructions, but their inference cost limits how fast a robot can respond in closed-loop control. Visual token pruning reduces this cost; however, prior methods estimate token redundancy from encoded visual embeddings or attention, only after the visual encoder has processed the entire observation. We propose ActPrune, a training-free token pruning method that moves the pruning decision ahead of the vision encoder by grounding it in a geometric prior derived from the robot's own action. The action chunk predicted at the previous control step is already available before the current observation is encoded, and it specifies the end-effector and camera motion between consecutive observations. The same action affects the two standard camera views asymmetrically: in the fixed third-person view, ActPrune retains patches along the projected end-effector trajectory together with semantic anchors, while in the wrist view, it prunes patches that remain unchanged after compensating for the action-induced camera motion. Extensive experiments on two simulation environments (LIBERO and VLABench) and four real-robot tasks demonstrate that ActPrune achieves up to 1.61$\times$ speedup and 59.5% FLOPs reduction with negligible degradation in success rates. The project site isthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[AnyCamVLA: Zero-Shot Camera Adaptation for Viewpoint Robust Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05868)**  `arXiv:2603.05868`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hyeongjun Heo, Seungyeon Woo, Sang Min Kim, Junho Kim, Junho Lee, Yonghyeon Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite remarkable progress in Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) for robot manipulation, these large pre-trained models require fine-tuning to be deployed in specific environments. These fine-tuned models are highly sensitive to camera viewpoint changes that frequently occur in unstructured environments. In this paper, we propose a zero-shot camera adaptation framework without additional demonstration data, policy fine-tuning, or architectural modification. Our key idea is to virtually adjust test-time camera observations to match the training camera configuration in real-time. For that, we use a recent feed-forward novel view synthesis model which outputs high-quality target view images, handling both extrinsic and intrinsic parameters. This plug-and-play approach preserves the pre-trained capabilities of VLAs and applies to any RGB-based policy. Through extensive experiments on the LIBERO benchmark, our method consistently outperforms baselines that use data augmentation for policy fine-tuning or additional 3D-aware features for visual input. We further validate that our approach constantly enhances viewpoint robustness in real-world robotic manipulation scenarios, including settings with varying camera extrinsics, intrinsics, and freely moving handheld cameras. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[COSMO-Bench: A Benchmark for Collaborative SLAM Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16731)**  `arXiv:2508.16731`  `cs.RO`  
  _Daniel McGann, Easton R. Potokar, Michael Kaess_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent years have seen a focus on research into distributed optimization algorithms for multi-robot Collaborative Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (C-SLAM). Research in this domain, however, is made difficult by a lack of standard benchmark datasets. Such datasets have been used to great effect in the field of single-robot SLAM, and researchers focused on multi-robot problems would benefit greatly from dedicated benchmark datasets. To address this gap, we design and release the Collaborative Open-Source Multi-robot Optimization Benchmark (COSMO-Bench) -- a suite of 24 datasets derived from a baseline C-SLAM front-end and real-world LiDAR data. Data DOI:this https URL
  </details>

- **[DecArena: Evaluating Large-Scale Decentralized Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15521)**  `arXiv:2611.15521`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiali Cheng, Oleg Rokhlenko, Joo-Kyung Kim, Hadi Amiri, Xingzhi Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emerging personal AI agents raise fundamental scalability and privacy concerns for centralized multi-agent systems (MASs), which rely on a global orchestrator with broad access to agents, resources, and communication. In decentralized environments, global visibility is unavailable. Agents need to proactively discover non-local resources (e.g. tools, skills), and collaborate with other agents to solve tasks. We study decentralized multi-agent collaboration under graph-constrained communication, a natural reflection of human structures. Each agent observes only its local state and can interact only with neighboring agents. We introduce a benchmark with synthetic scalable distributed tool calling with 100K+ tools. Our experiments reveal that recent LLM agents struggle to discover non-local relevant resources and collaborate in decentralized setting. Moreover, standard reinforcement learning fails to reliably overcome this limitation, suggesting that decentralized MAS needs more explicit harness mechanisms for effective collaboration, even using SOTA LLMs. We then present a strong baseline \textbf{GraphHarness}, combining topology-aware warmup with self-improving LLM-based routing. Results show that GraphHarness substantially improves task success (+48.5) and produces higher quality collaboration (+30.6), while using fewer costs (+34.0) than baselines.
  </details>

- **[From Delegation to Coordination: Learning Efficient Multi-Agent Deep Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17041)**  `arXiv:2611.17041`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuyang Wu, Yang Liu, Zequn Xie, Junjie Wang, Dan Yang, Zehao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent deep-research systems use delegation to parallelize search and keep long tool traces out of the main agent's context. Yet delegation helps only when task allocation and subtask execution are coordinated: the main agent must issue useful subtasks, while subagents must return reliable evidence. Existing training either optimizes only the main agent or broadcasts the same team advantage to all agents, which can reward misleading subagent reports and penalize useful ones. We introduce LEAD-RL (Learning Effective Agent Delegation with Reinforcement Learning), a joint main-agent-subagent training framework with contribution-aware credit assignment. A training-time rubric evaluates the research utility of each subagent report and gates its inherited team advantage, while final-answer correctness remains the only team reward; no token, step, or speedup reward is used. Across five benchmarks and two model families, LEAD-RL improves answer quality. On BrowseComp, it improves accuracy by 2.49 percentage points and reduces critical-path cost by 24.5% over joint training with shared team credit. During validation, accuracy increases by 6.78 points at approximately constant critical-path cost. These results show that contribution-aware credit assignment improves the quality-efficiency trade-off of multi-agent deep research.
  </details>

- **[Shared Item-Level Error Limits the Aggregation of Small Language Models in Fermi Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16315)**  `arXiv:2611.16315`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Carolina Fortuna, Blaz Bertalanic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems of language models are often motivated by an averaging argument: if the agents' errors are unbiased and independent, the error of an aggregate of N answers falls as 1/sqrt(N). Fermi estimation, whose continuous answers are aggregated on the logarithmic scale, permits a direct test. We decompose the log10 error of each answer into the mean error of its model on the item, common to all its draws on the item, and a residual, and estimate both from 2.51M answers given before any communication by 15 open-weight models (3B-72B) on realFP, with replication under a single prompt pair on realFP, Science Olympiad Fermi questions and a guesstimation set. Given the item, teammates' errors are uncorrelated and teams behave as independent draws, but the item-level component accounts for a median 0.906 of the error variance over items. Teams of thirty therefore reduce a single agent's error by a median 1.7% (at most 5.4%), whereas draws that were also unbiased on each item would reduce it by 74-79%. All models compress their estimates toward about 10^6; this trend explains a median 17% of the item-level component, and a cross-validated linear recalibration on labelled items removes the overall bias but not that component. Three rounds of debate reduce within-team dispersion by 93% and leave the item-level component unchanged; revision without peers attains the same small gain. Mixing five models of similar accuracy reduces error by 6.6% relative to their own homogeneous teams, but no mixture improves on the most accurate model. Higher sampling temperature changes team error by about 1% at most, and contrastive activation steering not detectably; per-agent scaffold prompts (not significant after multiplicity correction) and skipping one layer in a single-model screen improve it. The results are conditional on the models, benchmarks, prompts and settings tested.
  </details>

- **[RE-PRESENT: Evidence Triangulation across Alternative Candidate-Space Views for Efficient LLM Ensemble Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14780)**  `arXiv:2611.14780`  `cs.MA`  
  _Juming Xiong, Weixin Liu, Kevin Guo, Congning Ni, Chongyu Qu, Junchao Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heterogeneous large language models (LLMs) exhibit complementary strengths, motivating routing and multi-model inference methods that seek to identify reliable predictions for each input. However, exploiting this complementarity remains challenging even when a correct prediction is available within the ensemble. We investigate this challenge across heterogeneous LLM ensembles and find that a simple single-round aggregation rule is highly competitive with learned routing methods, yet substantial oracle headroom remains. Further analysis shows that the evaluated single-round response signals provide limited discriminative power for identifying reliable predictions. In contrast, controlled re-presentations of the same question elicit complementary evidence about prediction reliability. Motivated by these findings, we introduce RE-PRESENT, a training-free framework that selectively re-presents disputed questions and directly aggregates candidate-level evidence across alternative views. Across seven multiple-choice benchmarks and two heterogeneous model ensembles, RE-PRESENT improves over strong single-round aggregation while offering a favorable accuracy--cost trade-off compared with self-consistency and multi-model debate.
  </details>

- **[Imitating the Imperfect: Offline-to-Online Robust Imitation Learning from Heterogeneous Demonstrators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16020)**  `arXiv:2611.16020`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Cheng Pan, Nan Lu, Kai Arulkumaran, Josie Hughes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imitation learning (IL) typically relies on large-scale offline demonstrations collected from multiple human or algorithmic demonstrators. However, most existing approaches assume these demonstrators are homogeneous or near-optimal experts, a convenient but unrealistic assumption in real-world applications. In practice, demonstrations are often collected from heterogeneous demonstrators with unknown and varying levels of expertise, resulting in highly mixed-quality data. In this work, we study a challenging yet practical setting of imitation learning from such heterogeneous imperfect demonstrators. We propose Latent Expertise and Optimality Scoring based Imitation Learning (LEOS-IL), a principled framework that jointly learns (i) a state-action optimality scoring model and (ii) latent expertise levels for each demonstrator from unlabeled suboptimal offline demonstrations. The learned scoring model is then integrated into an online policy optimization procedure, where the agent is trained to maximize estimated optimality scores while its online rollouts are iteratively leveraged to refine the scoring model, thereby mitigating distributional shift during online adaptation. We further provide theoretical guarantees for optimality score identification and conditional optimal occupancy and policy recovery under explicit sufficient conditions. Experiments on continuous control benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing baselines across highly heterogeneous and low-quality demonstration regimes.
  </details>

- **[Asymmetric Multimodal Learning with Decomposed JEPAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16639)**  `arXiv:2611.16639`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Yusuf Shihata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-modal alignment is the standard objective for building multimodal representations. We show that alignment as a \emph{sole} objective is lossy wherever modalities are informationally asymmetric---where neither can express what the other carries. Visual question answering is an obvious case: a question is a selective relational operator probing a dense visual field whose fine-grained features no query recovers, so what alignment preserves is not what the task requires. We prove an \textbf{Alignment Redundancy Bound}: alignment-only Dirichlet energy minimization retains at most the redundant information atom $\Red(Z; X_1, X_2)$, annihilating private modality-specific structure $\Unq(Z; X_m)$ and cross-modal synergy $\Syn(Z; X_1, X_2)$.We introduce \textbf{Decomposed JEPA (D-JEPA)}, which factorizes latents into an aligned shared core and private modality-specific fibers. The shared core retains most of the structure alignment targets; the private fibers recover what alignment discards, via contextual masked prediction and asymmetric cross-subspace independence. Under information asymmetry, alignment-only training undergoes catastrophic semantic collapse on compositional reasoning benchmarks, surviving only on binary queries. D-JEPA averts this collapse, substantially improving downstream accuracy over both alignment-only and alignment-with-reconstruction baselines, and restoring a positive multimodal synergy gap in place of the baseline's inverted gap. Information-theoretic audits confirm that D-JEPA recovers most of the mutual information available from a frozen backbone, and causal fiber-swap interventions demonstrate that the recovered information is causally localized in the private fibers.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Floorplan Generation with Space Syntax: From Design Theory to Model Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22507)**  `arXiv:2602.22507`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuoyang Jiang, Dongqing Zhang, Qingxiang Liu, Yuxuan Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Architectural design increasingly adopts generative AI (GenAI) for floorplan generation, an early-stage design task exploring how spaces should be organized for use. Yet fidelity objectives inherited from native visual and geometric generators do not automatically capture this design purpose. This organization remains largely implicit in generated representations, limiting direct evaluation and feedback. Drawing on space syntax, an established architectural design theory, the Space Syntax Integration Operator (SSIO) recovers convex-space access graphs across heterogeneous formats. This theory-grounded shared intermediate representation, which we call a design container, supports evaluation and model understanding of spatial design. Applying SSIO to four residential corpora spanning 15 countries reveals public spaces' integration levels are highest, with corpus-dependent distributions. These patterns underpin the RPLAN-SSI benchmark, an empirical design reference system assessing population-level functional-profile alignment (IPD) and distributional alignment of within-plan public-space advantage (PIAD). Comparison with this residential reference reveals a design gap: public spaces in generated floorplans have a smaller integration advantage over other spaces. To address this gap, Space Syntax-Guided Post-Training (SSPT) provides a plug-and-play design-learning interface: SSIO-derived RL feedback reduces IPD and PIAD across five heterogeneous diffusion backbones while preserving native mechanisms. We further discuss how this approach may inform GenAI applications in other design domains.
  </details>

- **[Negative-Only Policy Optimization for One-Sided Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17287)**  `arXiv:2611.17287`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Xu, Shuo He, Fuxiang Zhang, Chaojie Wang, Bo An_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically relies on a verifier that can reliably judge whether sampled solutions are correct. However, in many realistic settings, complete verification is generally unavailable, while cheap checks can only falsify failures. We formalize this regime as one-sided verifiability (OSV): verified negatives are reliable counterexamples, but verified positives are merely non-falsified candidates and may contain many false positives. This asymmetry makes standard RLVR unreliable, because reinforcing verified positives can directly reward shortcuts that pass partial checks. To address this issue, we propose Negative-Only Policy Optimization (NOPO), which treats verified positives as unlabeled and updates the policy only by suppressing trusted verified negatives. Specifically, NOPO combines a reference-relative negative suppression loss with a group-level adapter that scales each update by the proportion of non-falsified responses. Across unit-test, constraint-check, and LLM self-verifiers, NOPO improves pass@k over baselines on code, logic, and math benchmarks when oracle rewards are unavailable. These gains persist under inference-time scaling and cross-benchmark transfer.
  </details>

- **[Experience-Handoff Reinforcement Learning for Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16409)**  `arXiv:2611.16409`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Doyoung Kim, Byeongguk Jeon, Kailin Zhang, Sherry Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  It is common to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for agentic tasks with Reinforcement Learning (RL) from scalar rewards. However, this throws away the fine-grained information about the environment that the agent experienced during the trial (e.g., error logs). To utilize this rich information, recent works wrap the agent in a harness that refines its own inputs through reflection or compaction and hands past experience to future rollouts, but the construction of the handed-off artifacts (e.g., a playbook) is itself not learned. We want the best of both worlds: gradient updates from a scalar reward, while also utilizing environment information to hand off experience. As a concrete testbed, we fix the Agentic Context Engineering (\ace{}) harness and train one model to act, reflect, and curate task-local playbooks. Our method, \acerl{}, evaluates candidate experience-handoff artifacts by their downstream effect on future actor rollouts, and co-optimizes the role-specific behaviors (action generator, insight reflector, and playbook curator) under a fixed scaffold. Across agentic benchmarks, \acerl{} achieves higher held-out performance than baseline methods. The results suggest that agent training should optimize not only the actions in the current rollout, but also the model-written experience that future rollouts inherit.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Compositional Generalization in Transformers: The Role of Composition Equivalence and Module Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13498)**  `arXiv:2611.13498`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Purva Pruthi, Andrew Yuan, Alexander D'Amour, David Jensen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compositional generalization is an important form of out-of-distribution generalization, defined as the ability to train on some combinations of modules (e.g., functions such as sort, filter) and then generalize to unseen combinations. Despite substantial empirical work evaluating compositional generalization in transformers, findings have been inconsistent because evaluations were often conducted on a limited set of train-test splits, defined in an ad hoc manner. Through a systematic evaluation, we identify two key aspects of the train-test distribution that strongly affect performance: composition equivalence and module coverage. We show that the apparent performance of direct models (trained only on final outputs) results primarily from exploiting composition equivalences, meaning different sequences of modules that reduce to identical end-to-end functions. When benchmarks eliminate these equivalences, performance drops significantly, showing their inability to generalize to compositions of known modules that produce novel end-to-end functions. We further demonstrate that performance degrades monotonically as module coverage shifts increase (when modules appear in different contexts between train and test), affecting both direct and step-by-step models (trained on intermediate and final outputs). These findings have concrete implications for benchmark design: benchmarks that include compositional equivalences can lead to significant overestimation of the generalization abilities of direct models, and benchmarks that include both module coverage violations and compositional equivalences can exacerbate shortcut learning. As a case study, we demonstrate the role of compositional equivalence on the generalization performance of the pre-trained model Llama3-8B on the code-output prediction task in the coding benchmark LiveCodeBench.
  </details>

- **[Domain-Routed Delta Optimization for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13249)**  `arXiv:2611.13249`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Lin Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation can yield uneven domain capabilities. We propose Domain-Routed Delta Optimization (DRD-MOPD), composing a frozen base with expert-initialized trainable deltas. Domains update their deltas with independent optimizer states and clipping. After joint training, Post-separate exports the base plus one trained delta per domain, selected by known task domain without retraining. Using AdamW, the unweighted full student reaches 32.83% Macro across six benchmarks after 20 updates, versus 32.21% for a matched shared control. Post-separate raises Macro with Open-MOPD weighting.
  </details>

- **[How Much Is Left Matters: False Positives and False Negatives in Partial-Prompt Contamination Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10307)**  `arXiv:2611.10307`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Abhishek Anand_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial-prompt completion, where a model receives the first part of a benchmark problem and is checked for whether it reproduces the rest, is a widely used test for benchmark contamination. We audit this test on seven open-weight models using MATH-500 and recent AIME 2025/2026 problems, which serve as true negatives. Memorization flags depend strongly on how much text remains: for every model, problems with at most 8 leftover words are flagged several times more often than problems with at least 25 (31.5% vs. 5.5% for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and half to three-quarters of all ROUGE-based flags come from short leftovers. On true negatives, a ROUGE-L >= 0.75 threshold flags up to 6.7% of problems at a 60% prefix, and at an 80% prefix even exact match flags 8.6% (pooled over models; 6.2% on AIME 2026, which postdates every model), against 0% at 40-60%. The test also misses contamination upstream: models distilled from Qwen2.5-Math, whose base is heavily flagged in prior work, show almost no model-specific recall. Calibrating each model against models from other families removes most flags while isolating a consistent Qwen2.5-specific signal at every prefix length. We recommend length-stratified reporting, prefixes of at most 60%, style-matched post-cutoff controls, and cross-family calibration.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Linear Activation Steering: Invertible Latent Transformations for Controlling LLM Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08454)**  `arXiv:2606.08454`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Tuc Nguyen, Thai Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering provides a lightweight inference-time mechanism for controlling large language models (LLMs) by modifying their internal activation vectors toward desired behaviors. Most existing methods compute a fixed steering direction in the original activation space, typically from pairs of contrastive examples using mean differences, linear probes, or arbitrary separability criteria. While effective to a certain extent, these methods treat behavioral control as a global, linear, additive offset: the same direction is applied across inputs, and behaviors are linearly separable. This can be restrictive when behavioral features vary nonlinearly across the activation space or lie on curved and anisotropic manifolds, where the optimal intervention may be input-dependent. To address this limitation, we propose INNSteer, a nonlinear activation steering framework based on invertible latent transformations. Rather than searching for a better steering vector in the original representation space, INNSteer learns a lightweight invertible neural network $\phi$ that maps an LLM's activations into a latent space where behavioral classes are more amenable to linear control. At inference time, activations are mapped through $\phi$, steered in the latent space, and mapped back through the exact inverse transformation $\phi^{-1}$. This makes a simple latent-space translation become a nonlinear, input-dependent intervention in the original activation space. Across experiment settings on multiple LLM families, scales, behavioral traits, and safety benchmarks, INNSteer consistently improves model control over linear, transport-based, and nonlinear steering baselines while largely preserving generation fluency.
  </details>

- **[MEME: Multi-entity & Evolving Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12477)**  `arXiv:2605.12477`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Seokwon Jung, Alexander Rubinstein, Arnas Uselis, Sangdoo Yun, Seong Joon Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents increasingly operate in persistent environments where they must store, update, and reason over information across many sessions. While prior benchmarks evaluate only single-entity updates, MEME defines six tasks spanning the full space defined by the multi-entity and evolving axes, including three not scored by prior work: Cascade and Absence (dependency reasoning) and Deletion (post-removal state). Evaluating six memory systems spanning three memory paradigms on 100 controlled episodes, we find that all systems collapse on dependency reasoning under the default configuration (Cascade: 3%, Absence: 1% in average accuracy) despite adequate static retrieval performance. Prompt optimization, deeper retrieval, reduced filler noise, and most stronger LLMs fail to close this gap. The largest gain comes from a file-based agent paired with Claude Opus 4.7 as its internal LLM, which partially closes the gap at roughly 70x the baseline cost. Code and data are available on the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Winfree Oscillatory Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20922)**  `arXiv:2605.20922`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Jiawen Dai, Yue Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Oscillations and synchronization are widely believed to play a fundamental role in representation and computation. However, existing machine learning approaches based on synchronization dynamics have largely been confined to specialized settings such as object discovery, with limited evidence of scalability to standard vision benchmarks or logic reasoning tasks. We propose the Winfree Oscillatory Neural Network (WONN), a dynamical neural architecture based on generalized Winfree dynamics. WONN evolves representations on the torus $(S^1)^d$ through structured oscillatory interactions, combining phase-based inductive biases with flexible and hierarchical interaction mechanisms instantiated as either fixed trigonometric mappings or learnable neural networks. We evaluate WONN on image recognition and complex reasoning tasks, including CIFAR, ImageNet, Maze-hard, and Sudoku. Across these domains, WONN achieves competitive or superior performance with strong parameter efficiency. In particular, WONN is, to our knowledge, the first synchronization-based oscillatory architecture to scale competitively to ImageNet-1K. Furthermore, on Maze-hard, WONN achieves 80.1% accuracy using only 1% of the parameters of prior state-of-the-art models. These results suggest that structured oscillatory dynamics provide a scalable and parameter-efficient alternative to conventional neural architectures.
  </details>

- **[JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174)**  `arXiv:2611.16174`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.
  </details>

- **[Coding Agent Memory Post-Training II: Unlocking the Memory Potential of Pre-trained File Operations for Long-Horizon Tasks via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14009)**  `arXiv:2611.14009`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Lirui Luo, Kelong Mao, Xinwei Yang, Rongqing Li, Luyu Chen, Kieran Wong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks whose interaction histories exceed the model's active context. They must therefore decide what to write down and what to read back, and when to do either. Recent work has begun to use on-policy distillation (OPD) to make memory control part of the policy, relying on memory tools and compaction protocols that lie outside either model's pre-training. However, this weakens the distillation on both sides: the teacher cannot give useful guidance on an interface it has never used, and the student samples too few meaningful memory actions for that guidance to take effect. To study this problem, we introduce Coding Agent Memory Gym II (CAMG-II), a suite of long-horizon agentic-RL environments spanning Shop, Coding, AutoResearch, General, and Visual. Alongside each environment's native task interface, CAMG-II provides ordinary Bash access and an episode-persistent workspace, enabling the policy to create, revise, search, and reuse files as memory across context replacements. We also introduce Coding Agent Memory Post-Training II (CAMP-II), which trains a single policy jointly across all five environments with fully asynchronous OPD to learn this file-based memory behavior directly from a frozen teacher with a prior for file operations. On CAMG-II, CAMP-II obtains 2.97 times the gain of the strongest OPD baseline. The memory behavior CAMP-II learns generalizes to two external benchmarks, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SWE-Bench Pro V2, with improved success rates.
  </details>

- **[Patching the Blind Spots: Reversible LLM Rule Overlay for Frozen Fraud Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08714)**  `arXiv:2611.08714`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bo Qu, Zhurong Wang, Frank Zahradnik, Ming Ouyang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient-boosted trees dominate industrial tabular fraud classification, but even a well-trained model has blind spots, and retraining a deployed model is not always safe. We bring LLM reasoning to patching such a model without modifying it: LLMs read its tree digest and SHAP attributions, reason about the fraud it misses, and propose rules anchored on features it underweights, screened and calibrated into a reversible log-lift overlay at the production cut-off. Single-LLM benefit is high-variance and inverts between datasets, so we pool three diverse backbones by logit averaging, trading peak gain for a bounded worst case. On an experimental detector trained on sampled, selection-biased real-world e-commerce data, tuned continued training regresses, whereas the overlay gains on average +2.26% recall at equal precision ($\Delta$R@P) and +1.60% precision at equal recall ($\Delta$P@R). Replayed offline on a legacy production detector, the overlay, calibrated on one month and frozen, stays more precise at equal recall for seven months (+5.64% $\Delta$P@R). On seven bias-shift variants of a public benchmark it raises $\Delta$P@R on all and keeps $\Delta$R@P near neutral. LLM reasoning over a model's diagnostics can improve a deployed tabular classifier through readable predicates naming the fraud they cover.
  </details>

- **[Quantifying Subliminal Behavioral Transfer Ratios in Language Model Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11270)**  `arXiv:2606.11270`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Uwe König, Hamza Kazmi, Ruizhe Li, Maheep Chaudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distillation of a language model intended to transfer benign behavior to a student model may also transfer undesirable characteristics, if they are present in the teacher model, a phenomenon known as subliminal learning. While qualitative evidence supports the existence of this effect, its magnitude has not been systematically characterized. This study quantifies subliminal behavioral transfer ratios by steering two teacher models (Llama-2-7B-Chat and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct) at varying steering strengths and distilling student models using only benign data. Evaluation on 100 JailbreakBench prompts with GPT-4.1, serving as the evaluator, indicates that transfer is robust but exhibits distinct scaling behaviors. Llama-2 demonstrates a sharp threshold ($\tau = {0.25,0.32} \ \text{beyond} \ \alpha = -0.15$), whereas Qwen2.5 displays continuous and higher levels of transfer ($\tau$ up to $0.61$).
  </details>

- **[Control Reinforcement Learning: Interpretable Token-Level Steering of LLMs via Sparse Autoencoder Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10437)**  `arXiv:2602.10437`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Seonglae Cho, Zekun Wu, Adriano Koshiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into interpretable features, but existing methods reveal only which features activate, not which change model outputs when amplified. We introduce Control Reinforcement Learning (CRL), which trains a policy to select SAE features for steering at each token, producing interpretable intervention logs: the learned policy identifies features that change model outputs when amplified. Adaptive Feature Masking encourages diverse feature discovery while preserving singlefeature interpretability. The framework yields new analysis capabilities: branch point tracking locates tokens where feature choice determines output correctness; critic trajectory analysis separates policy limitations from value estimation errors; layer-wise comparison reveals syntactic features in early layers and semantic features in later layers. On Gemma 2 2B across MMLU, BBQ, GSM8K, HarmBench, and XSTest, CRL achieves improvements while providing per-token intervention logs. These results establish learned feature steering as a mechanistic interpretability tool that complements static feature analysis with dynamic intervention probes
  </details>

- **[DPA: Decentralized Primal Averaging with Quasi-Global Momentum for Highly Heterogeneous Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16871)**  `arXiv:2611.16871`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Diyako Ghaderyan, Stefan Werner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized training of deep learning models keeps training data local and supports on-device learning over networks. In realistic scenarios, heterogeneity across agents' local data poses an optimization challenge and can severely degrade test accuracy, especially on sparse communication graphs. Building on recent advances in centralized primal averaging, we propose Decentralized Primal Averaging (DPA), a decentralized learning algorithm that combines primal averaging with a local quasi-global momentum buffer. DPA maintains three coupled sequences for optimization, gradient evaluation, and output averaging, while using consecutive mixed iterates to construct a network-aware momentum direction without transmitting an auxiliary tracking variable. For smooth nonconvex objectives, we establish a convergence bound for DPA and identify conditions for linear speedup in the number of agents for sufficiently many iterations. We also introduce DPA-1G, a variant that uses one communication round per iteration and matches the per-iteration communication cost of DSGD-style baselines. Experiments following the GUT decentralized image-classification benchmark show that DPA compares favorably with DSGD, gradient tracking, QG-DSGDm, momentum tracking, and QG-GUTm across datasets, architectures, topologies, and heterogeneity levels, with the largest gains under high heterogeneity on sparse rings. At the same total communication cost, both DPA variants achieve higher mean test accuracy than the best baseline on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with ResNet-20. Our code is publicly available.
  </details>

- **[DRAMA: Dissecting Attention Redundancy for Accelerating Multimodal Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16345)**  `arXiv:2611.16345`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Hsin Hung, Fangfu Liu, Shao-Yuan Lo, Chu-Song Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text through iterative masked denoising with bidirectional attention, offering a compelling alternative to autoregressive vision-language modeling. However, each denoising step requires a full forward pass over long multimodal sequences in which visual tokens dominate the sequence length, and a fixed step budget further compounds this quadratic per-step cost. Existing inference acceleration approaches for multimodal dLLMs characterize attention redundancy implicitly, motivating designs from isolated attention visualizations rather than from measurement. We close this gap with DRAMA, a training-free acceleration framework grounded in a systematic dissection of attention redundancy in multimodal dLLMs. By probing attention tensors along spatial and temporal axes, we reveal two persistent redundancy structures: (i) attention mass consistently concentrates on a sparse subset of visual tokens, and (ii) attention distributions rapidly stabilize across denoising steps after an initial formation phase. These findings motivate two complementary mechanisms: Spatial-Redundancy-Aware Visual Token Pruning (SVTP) and Sufficiency-Guided Adaptive Stopping (SGAS). Together, DRAMA achieves an average 18.67 times inference speedup across six vision-language benchmarks without training, architectural modification, or attention caching, while maintaining competitive accuracy. Our results establish a strong, measurement-grounded acceleration baseline for multimodal dLLM inference.
  </details>

- **[Wanda-Guided Sparse Perturbations for Efficient Zeroth-Order Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16317)**  `arXiv:2611.16317`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqing Wen, Xiaohui Song, Jiahuan Wang, Ping Luo, Junlin Zeng, Dongsheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
