  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient fine-tuning of large language models through forward-only evaluations, but high-dimensional random perturbations can limit optimization efficiency. Sparse perturbations reduce the search dimension, making the selection of informative coordinates critical. We propose Wanda-Importance Sparsity-MeZO (WIS-MeZO), which uses Wanda weight--activation saliency to construct task-conditioned sparse perturbation masks while keeping the pretrained model dense. We analyze support quality through gradient energy capture and relate it to gradient estimation and convergence. Across 16 benchmarks with LLaMA and Qwen models ranging from 3B to 32B parameters, WIS-MeZO achieves higher aggregate scores than MeZO and reaches comparable validation losses in fewer optimization steps. Comparisons with random and magnitude-based sparse perturbations further highlight the importance of informative support selection.
  </details>

- **[Rectified Looped Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265)**  `arXiv:2611.16265`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Musheng Li, Jiawei Zhang, Ziyuan Liu, Yuantao Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.
  </details>

- **[Latent Imagination for Parameterized Action Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15744)**  `arXiv:2611.15744`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Atahan Cilan, İhsan Mert Muhacıroğlu, Ümit Can Bekar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameterized-action MDPs (PAMDPs) ask an agent to choose a discrete action together with continuous parameters whose meaning depends on that choice. Model-free PAMDP methods act fast but need many interactions; model-based methods are sample-efficient but plan at every decision, which makes them slow to act. We introduce HyLI (Hybrid Latent Imagination), a model-based agent that learns a hybrid policy entirely in the latent imagination of a world model and acts with a single forward pass. To address learning problems that the PAMDP structure creates, HyLI adds counterfactual mode evaluation, which gives every discrete action a learning signal at every imagined state, and success-window replay, which keeps rare successes in training. On seven standard PAMDP benchmarks, HyLI is within one published standard deviation of, or above, the published score of the strongest model-free baseline in our comparison on all 7 tasks, reaches 90% of that score with 2.4x fewer environment steps than that baseline (5.0x when its reward-free pre-training is counted), and decides 67x faster than the model-based planner DLPA at its published planner settings. It is the only method in our comparison that is both more sample-efficient than that baseline and fast enough for real-time control.
  </details>

- **[Breath-Dx: A Time Series Classification Benchmark for Disease Screening with eNose](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15348)**  `arXiv:2611.15348`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ivan Poddiakov, Ivan Kruzhilov, Galina Zubkova, Svetlana Erofeeva, Andrey Savchenko, Pavel Blinov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electronic nose (eNose) technology offers a portable, non-invasive route for disease screening via exhaled breath analysis. However, progress in the field is hindered by the lack of systematic benchmarks that compare machine learning methods under realistic conditions, particularly sensor drift, a known but rarely quantified failure mode. Most existing studies rely on small, private datasets with inconsistent protocols, rendering results non-comparable and slowing progress. We introduce Breath-Dx, the first comprehensive benchmark of time series classification methods for breath-based disease screening. Our work leverages the Scent of Health (S-OH) dataset, the largest public clinical eNose collection to date, comprising 1,234 patients across nine diagnostic groups, each providing a 17-channel multivariate time series with explicit temporal train/test splits that account for sensor drift. We systematically evaluate 10 methods spanning four families: feature-based tabular learners, convolution-based models, graph-based, and time series architectures. All methods degrade significantly under temporal validation (0.05-0.12 AUC drop compared to random cross-validation), confirming that drift embeds a strong temporal signal. Under this realistic evaluation, the best methods achieve ROC AUC of 0.74-0.76 for some lung conditions, though others remain near chance. Recurrent architectures show the most consistent performance across diseases, while specialized time series methods achieve the highest peak results. Demographic confounders matter: breath signals provide independent diagnostic value for some conditions but add little beyond age for others. We release the S-OH dataset, all temporal splits, evaluation protocols, and code to enable reproducible research and accelerate progress in clinical breathomics.
  </details>

- **[CastWorld: Benchmarking Context-Aware Agentic Time Series Forecasting Beyond Predictive Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15262)**  `arXiv:2611.15262`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bokai Pan, Ruijie Yang, Mingyue Cheng, Jiahao Wang, Xiaoyu Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic time series forecasting extends conventional approaches beyond one-shot numerical prediction toward autonomous, context-aware interaction. However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.
  </details>

- **[Dual Representation Enhanced Diffusion Policy for Flexible Job Shop Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15246)**  `arXiv:2611.15246`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunyi Zhao, Wei Zhang, Cheng Xiang, Vadakkepat Prahlad, Zuming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) is a fundamental and challenging optimization problem in smart manufacturing. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches can learn effective scheduling policies through interaction with the environment, their performance often degrades as problem scale and processing-time uncertainty increase. In this paper, we propose DredRL, a dual representation enhanced diffusion-policy reinforcement learning scheduler. DredRL first constructs enhanced dual representations of operations and machines through self- and cross-attention mechanisms to capture both intra-entity and inter-entity dependencies. A diffusion model is further employed as the policy network, generating scheduling decisions through iterative denoising conditioned on the encoded state to enhance policy expressiveness and robustness. Extensive experiments on synthetic, public, large-scale, and stochastic FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that DredRL outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based schedulers across most configurations. Compared with SOTA learning-based methods, DredRL reduces makespan by up to 5.44% on synthetic instances. It also achieves the best learning-based performance on three of four public benchmarks and outperforms the stochasticity-aware algorithms on stochastic benchmarks. These results demonstrate that combining enhanced dual representations with diffusion-based policies provides an effective and robust approach for large scale scalable FJSP scheduling in complicated environment.
  </details>

- **[CoLaX: Context-Aware Local Explanations for Time Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15067)**  `arXiv:2611.15067`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kexuan Zhang, Paolo Frazzetto, Xiaobei Zou, Gustau Camps-Valls, Yang Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-hoc explanations for time series classifiers usually score local patterns, such as subsequences or shapelets, without regard to the rest of the sequence. Yet the same motif can carry different predictive meaning under different global temporal conditions, such as trends or regimes. We propose CoLaX, a context-aware post-hoc explanation framework that builds on shapelet-driven Shapley attribution and conditions local-pattern relevance on a learned representation of the full sequence. Global context acts at two levels: it produces sample-specific prototype weights and a temporally resolved signal that reweights the local activation map. The resulting context-conditioned segments are the players of a Shapley attribution game whose values come only from the frozen black-box classifier. On synthetic benchmarks where identical motifs change relevance across contexts, CoLaX improves AUPRC over the strongest baseline by 30\% on average. On real-world data, it achieves the highest AUPRC on ECG. Additional ablations indicate that the gains come from input-dependent conditioning rather than from additional learnable weights. CoLaX is therefore most useful when recurring local motifs change relevance across sequence-level contexts.
  </details>

- **[Graph Label Alignment: A Diagnostic Atlas for Graph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14760)**  `arXiv:2611.14760`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Neelam Akula, Varun Shiralkar, Murat Kantarcioglu, Baris Coskunuzer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph classification benchmarks vary widely in difficulty: some are solved by simple pooled baselines, while others remain challenging for GNNs and graph transformers. We take a data-centric view and ask whether benchmark success is driven by genuine structure--feature reasoning or by label signal already accessible from restricted marginal views. We introduce graph label alignment, a lightweight diagnostic that measures label accessibility through two supervised probes computed without training a graph encoder: structural label alignment (SLA), based on permutation-invariant topological and spectral summaries, and feature label alignment (FLA), based on pooled node features with edges removed. Across real graph classification benchmarks and multiple pooled backbones, the resulting SLA--FLA plane strongly organizes test accuracy, with low-alignment datasets forming a consistent regime where standard pooled pipelines struggle. To probe what lies beyond marginal accessibility, we introduce controlled interaction-dominated stress tests, including heterophilic ego-graphs and synthetic Twins tasks whose structural and feature marginals are matched across classes but whose role--feature couplings differ. These results show that high alignment explains much of easy benchmark performance, while low alignment reveals regimes where success requires preserving structure--feature correspondence beyond standard pooled representations.
  </details>

- **[From Nodes to Pixels: Topological and Structural Two-View Graph Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14750)**  `arXiv:2611.14750`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Md Joshem Uddin, Soham Changani, Sai Karthik Navuluru, Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora, Baris Coskunuzer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph learning still lacks a broadly reusable input interface comparable to patches in vision or tokens in language. Unlike images or text, graphs are irregular, vary in size and density, and are invariant under many equivalent node labelings, making it difficult to standardize inputs across tasks and architectures. We introduce G2Image, a fixed-budget graph imaging framework that converts each graph into two complementary image-like views. TopoGrid provides a stable topological view by encoding an intrinsic bifiltration as a compact multipersistence surface grid, while GraphGrid provides a higher-bandwidth structural view by aggregating edge densities between groups defined by intrinsic node scores. Both views are permutation-invariant and come with resolution-controlled stability guarantees. Because the resulting representations are fixed-size tensors, they can be processed by lightweight 2D encoders and aligned through a supervised cross-view contrastive objective. Across graph classification and molecular prediction benchmarks, G2Image achieves strong average performance among the compared methods, improves over single-view and standard fusion variants, and extends to attribute-rich molecular graphs without changing the overall architecture. These results suggest that fixed-budget graph images offer a practical and reusable interface for graph representation learning.
  </details>

- **[KernelMosaic: An Open Dataset and Agentic Framework for CUDA Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14604)**  `arXiv:2611.14604`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ningyuan Xi, Yuhang Lai, Ning Miao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models to generate efficient CUDA kernels requires supervision spanning diverse computational requirements and iterative code optimization. Constructing this supervision is challenging because of heterogeneous specifications, and useful implementations emerge through iterative complicated testing and refinement. We introduce KernelMosaic, an open dataset pairing diverse CUDA tasks with verified solutions and trajectories. Human-guided review and diversity-aware selection curate tasks expressed as natural-language descriptions, reference programs, mathematical specifications, and their intersections. To build the solutions and trajectories, our agentic framework guides a strong LLM through implementation, correctness repair, and performance optimization with reusable CUDA guidance and execution feedback. Supervised fine-tuning of a small open-source model on KernelMosaic raises correctness from 4%, 0%, and 0% to 88%, 82%, and 16% on KernelBench L1, L2, and L3, respectively, while increasing the fraction of tasks with kernels faster than PyTorch eager at every level. Further training experiments show that supervision from the curated trajectories substantially outperforms direct responses and promotes sustained testing and revision. These results demonstrate that KernelMosaic enables a general-purpose model to achieve substantial gains in CUDA kernel generation.
  </details>

- **[Visible Tails: Characterizing Adaptation to Unknown Moments in Heavy-Tailed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12140)**  `arXiv:2611.12140`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tuan Ngoc Do, Hai Duc Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heavy-tailed bandit learners must tell a poor arm from an arm whose favorable rewards have not yet appeared. We characterize this obstruction through a scale of one-sided visibility conditions on the optimal reward law: the mean hidden in a rare event is at most a constant multiple of a retained central moment. A quantile-trimmed UCB index that is given the visibility constants attains arm-specific moment rates at every exponent, with no lower bound on the exponent. Conversely, every strategy with a fixed exploration budget has polynomial regret on each law that violates the corresponding condition. For the variance benchmark we determine the order of both budget constants in the visibility level. The frontier depends on the largest exponent the benchmark promises, and the resulting classes are strictly nested. The same index has logarithmic expected regret when rewards are integrable, with a coefficient that can be exponential in the inverse gap.
  </details>

- **[CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12125)**  `arXiv:2611.12125`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Ye, Xiao Yao, Hairui Zhu, Yiying Yang, Bei Gan, Jinliang Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. In a step-level diagnosis of Qwen3.5 trajectories at two model sizes on GAIA, a long-horizon tool-use benchmark, we find that most contain removable calls, at a similar rate at both sizes. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.
  </details>

- **[Generating Critic-Guided Synthetic Experience in Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39100)**  `arXiv:2610.39100`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jorge de Freitas, Christos Ziakas, Alessandra Russo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from offline datasets is limited in scalability, as collecting new experience is often costly or unsafe in the real world. World models offer an alternative way to generate new experience through a learned dynamics model, yet learning policies in latent space is limited due to compounding errors in imagined rollouts. To this end, we propose using the world model as a local transition generator, leveraging critic guidance to choose actions inside that model to generate synthetic experiences. We study action refinement methodologies, introducing Critic-Guided Synthetic Experience (CGSE) generation in world models. Specifically, CGSE augments an existing offline dataset by sampling candidate actions using an offline policy and refining each to maximize a first-order approximation of the $Q$-function within a trust region. We then use a frozen latent world model to imagine the future state given each updated action, and use the critic to choose the best one, appending this synthetic transition to the replay buffer. Our method requires no additional real-environment interaction or test-time planner, as it generates synthetic experience offline using the world model. On three OGBench manipulation tasks, CGSE improves an offline policy by a relative improvement of $7.1\%$ on the average success rate.
  </details>

- **[BatchBench: Benchmarking AI Robustness to Batch Effects and Domain Shift in Biomedical Images - A JUMP Cell Painting Leaderboard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30104)**  `arXiv:2610.30104`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sonja Topf, Ana Sanchez-Fernandez, Günter Klambauer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite recent advances, current AI systems remain highly susceptible to domain shifts, particularly in biomedical imaging, where images are usually acquired under substantially different experimental conditions. Although recent domain adaptation methods have made substantial progress, significant performance gaps under domain shift persist. To measure progress in this regard, we introduce BatchBench, a leaderboard that evaluates and compares the robustness of AI systems on biomedical images from unseen experimental batches and data sources. BatchBench is built on JUMP-CP, one of the largest publicly available biomedical imaging datasets. Generated using the Cell Painting assay, JUMP-CP exhibits pronounced batch effects and domain shifts, making it a challenging testbed for evaluating robustness to distributional changes. The leaderboard is open for submissions onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CENDRe: Concept Extraction with Natural Domain Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29621)**  `arXiv:2607.29621`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Antonia Holzapfel, Andres Felipe Posada Moreno, Sebastian Trimpe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are widely used for time-series classification, but their deployment in critical contexts requires understanding the patterns across signal domains that drive their predictions (e.g., temporal or spectral). Concept extraction (CE) methods identify such patterns by analyzing representations within the models' latent space. However, existing time-series CE methods have three limitations: they operate only in the time domain and cannot locate features in other domains, they depend on a predefined number of concepts that biases them, and they produce localizations misaligned with the regions the model uses. We address these limitations with CENDRe, a concept extraction method for CNNs. For concept discovery, we propose a procedure that clusters per-timestep latent representations in two stages, with a silhouette-guided aggregation that selects the number of concepts automatically. For localization, we define a presence score that contrasts the latent representations with their prototypes, and use its gradients to produce masks that concentrate on the regions driving the concept. These gradients, propagated through a differentiable invertible mapping of the input such as a Fourier transform, yield localizations for the same concepts in different domains. Finally, each concept receives a relevance score that quantifies its contribution to each class. On synthetic benchmarks, CENDRe achieves representation correctness comparable to state-of-the-art CE methods and higher importance correctness, while producing visualizations in different domains. On real bearing-fault data, CENDRe extracts the frequency bands driving the model's predictions, located in regions commonly inspected for fault diagnosis, producing evidence to assess the model that time-domain CE methods cannot.
  </details>

- **[MiniOpt: Reasoning to Model and Solve General Optimization Problems with Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25832)**  `arXiv:2606.25832`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Zhao, Zixiang Di, Hong Qian, Xiang Shu, Yaolin Wen, Qitao Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving strong optimization generalization across diverse optimization problems while requiring limited training resources remains a challenging problem for optimization-oriented large language models (LLMs). Existing learning-based methods, however, typically require large-scale supervised data, expensive intermediate step verification, and extensive computational resources, while exhibiting limited optimization generalization across diverse optimization structures. In this paper, we propose \textsc{MiniOpt}, a lightweight optimization-specialized LLM trained through a reasoning-to-model-and-solve paradigm and a two-stage training framework with reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). MiniOpt encourages structure-oriented optimization learning by reinforcing the completeness of optimization formulations and optimization outcomes. Extensive experiments on eight optimization benchmarks demonstrate that MiniOpt consistently outperforms existing learning-based optimization models while requiring substantially fewer parameters and training data. To further understand optimization generalization, we introduce a complementary parameter-decoupled evaluation protocol that disentangles optimization structure understanding from numerical parameter processing. The results reveal that MiniOpt preserves optimization capability under parameter-decoupled settings significantly better than previous optimization-specialized models, suggesting that its performance gains primarily originate from stronger optimization structure understanding. These findings demonstrate an effective pathway toward efficient and generalizable optimization-specialized LLMs. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TIDE: Token-Level Credit Assignment via Entropy-Gated Bounded Deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03327)**  `arXiv:2605.03327`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiaoman Zhang, Shunhua Min, Xiang He, Zhenghan Zhang, Yuting Zhang, Guangxian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is central for aligning large language models (LLMs) with complex reasoning, yet the leading critic-free algorithm, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), broadcasts a single sequence-level advantage uniformly across long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generations and constrains exploration with an unbounded Kullback-Leibler (KL) penalty, which dilutes token-level supervision and induces gradient instability and mode-seeking conservatism. We propose TIDE (Token-level Importance via Deviation and Entropy), a critic-free RL framework that reinterprets distribution deviation as a guiding signal rather than a rigid penalty. TIDE replaces the volatile reverse KL divergence with the bounded Hellinger distance, safely quantifying token-level exploration without risk of gradient explosion. To separate genuine reasoning breakthroughs from confident hallucinations, an entropy gating mechanism scales this deviation by the policy's predictive uncertainty, and the gated scores redistribute the sequence-level advantage across tokens. The resulting updates amplify exploratory steps while suppressing low-entropy deviations, without a value network and with negligible overhead. Across in-domain, held-out, and out-of-domain benchmarks, TIDE outperforms strong critic-free baselines: on the Qwen2.5-32B backbone it reaches 60.0% and 46.0% Avg@32 accuracy on AIME 2024 and AIME 2025, respectively, above competitive baselines such as DAPO. Token-level attribution, causal perturbation, and exploration analyses further support the interpretation that the entropy-gated deviation identifies pivotal reasoning steps rather than merely perturbing optimization dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Delayed homomorphic reinforcement learning for environments with delayed feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03641)**  `arXiv:2604.03641`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jongsoo Lee, Jangwon Kim, Soohee Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning in real-world systems often involves delayed feedback, which violates the Markov assumption and impedes both learning and control. Canonical augmentation-based approaches address this issue by augmenting the state with action histories, but suffer from state-space explosion and the resulting sample-complexity burden. Despite recent progress, state-of-the-art augmentation-based methods either primarily alleviate this burden for the critic or employ separate mechanisms for the actor and critic. To address this limitation, we propose delayed homomorphic reinforcement learning (DHRL), a framework grounded in MDP homomorphisms that introduces a belief-equivalence relation over the augmented state space to collapse control-redundant augmented states into a compact abstract state space. This framework yields exact abstraction under deterministic dynamics and approximate abstraction under stochastic dynamics, providing a unified abstraction mechanism that benefits both the actor and critic. In finite domains, exact abstraction preserves optimality and recovers the delay-free sample-complexity order, while approximate abstraction admits a bound on the value loss of the resulting policy. For continuous domains, we introduce deep delayed homomorphic policy gradient (D$^2$HPG), a deep actor-critic instantiation of the DHRL framework. Experiments on MuJoCo continuous-control benchmarks demonstrate that D$^2$HPG outperforms strong augmentation-based baselines, especially in long-delay regimes. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MAC: A Conversion Rate Prediction Benchmark Featuring Labels Under Multiple Attribution Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02184)**  `arXiv:2603.02184`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinqi Wu, Sishuo Chen, Zhangming Chan, Yong Bai, Lei Zhang, Sheng Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-attribution learning (MAL), which enhances model performance by learning from conversion labels yielded by multiple attribution mechanisms, has emerged as a promising learning paradigm for conversion rate (CVR) prediction. However, the conversion labels in public CVR datasets are generated by a single attribution mechanism, hindering the development of MAL approaches.To address this data gap, we establish the Multi-Attribution Benchmark (MAC), the first public CVR dataset featuring labels from multiple attribution mechanisms. Besides, to promote reproducible research on MAL, we develop PyMAL, an open-source library covering a wide array of baseline methods. We conduct comprehensive experimental analyses on MAC and reveal three key insights: (1) MAL brings consistent performance gains across different attribution settings, especially for users featuring long conversion paths. (2) The performance growth scales up with objective complexity in most settings; however, when predicting first-click conversion targets, simply adding auxiliary objectives is counterproductive, underscoring the necessity of careful selection of auxiliary objectives. (3) Two architectural design principles are paramount: first, to fully learn the multi-attribution knowledge, and second, to fully leverage this knowledge to serve the main task. Motivated by these findings, we propose Mixture of Asymmetric Experts (MoAE), an effective MAL approach incorporating multi-attribution knowledge learning and main task-centric knowledge utilization. Experiments on MAC show that MoAE substantially surpasses the existing state-of-the-art MAL method. We believe that our benchmark and insights will foster future research in the MAL field. Our MAC benchmark and the PyMAL algorithm library are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Shattered Compositionality: Counterintuitive Learning Dynamics of Transformers for Arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22510)**  `arXiv:2601.22510`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyu Zhao, Darsh Sharma, Rheeya Uppaal, Yiqiao Zhong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often achieve strong benchmark accuracy yet remain brittle under small distribution shifts. While recent mechanistic studies reveal the discrepancy between LLMs and humans in skill compositions, the learning dynamics of skill acquisition and the role of data distributions remain elusive. In this study, we train transformers on synthetic arithmetic tasks with black-box model-agnostic metrics for analyzing non-human skill compositions. We discover that transformers often acquire skills for arithmetic in reverse order or in parallel instead of human-like sequential rules--a phenomenon we refer to as shattered compositionality. To explain these behaviors, we provide evidence that correlational matching to the training data, rather than causal or procedural composition, shapes learning dynamics. As a consequence, this non-human acquisition creates competition between partially learned skills, producing characteristic mixing errors and weaker robustness under controlled distribution shifts. We further show that the same qualitative behavior persists in modern LLMs and is not mitigated by pure model scaling or scratchpad supervision. Our results highlight a mismatch between training-time skill acquisition and the human-like hierarchical compositions, with implications for reasoning reliability and out-of-distribution robustness.
  </details>

- **[Time Flies When You Parallelise: Pareto-efficient Time Series Foundation Models with Linear Recurrences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16934)**  `arXiv:2611.16934`  `cs.LG`  
  _Torben Berndt, Lingyi Yang, Federico Nardi, Yi Heng Lim, AJ Shears, Benjamin Walker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models have demonstrated strong zero-shot forecasting performance, but computational efficiency remains a central architectural challenge. Temporal self-attention incurs quadratic cost in the number of temporal tokens, while nonlinear recurrent state transitions restrict parallel computation along the temporal dimension. We introduce ci-proto, a family of time series foundation models based on Mamba-3, whose linear state recurrences support chunk-wise parallel-in-time computation during training, and inference costs that scale linearly with sequence length. The architecture alternates recurrent temporal processing with cross-variate attention, supporting multivariate probabilistic forecasting with past-only and future-known covariates. Across fev-bench's 100 forecasting tasks, ci-proto XL sets a new state of the art among time series foundation models. The five ci-proto variants also make up the entire Pareto frontier for mean scaled quantile loss and mean absolute scaled error against both parameter count and median runtime.
  </details>

- **[Calibration and Conformal Prediction for Medical Question Answering in Arabic and English: Evidence from Two Non-Parallel Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16821)**  `arXiv:2611.16821`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mariya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly tested on medical exam questions, but accuracy alone does not show when an answer can be trusted. I study the calibration and uncertainty of two open-weight models, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Jais-adapted-7B-chat (both run in 4-bit precision), on 2,816 English multiple-choice questions from MedMCQA and 4,927 Arabic multiple-choice questions from MedAraBench. Accuracy was 0.561 (English) and 0.430 (Arabic) for Qwen, and 0.369 and 0.308 for Jais. Raw option probabilities were overconfident in all four settings (expected calibration error 0.242 to 0.486), and a single temperature parameter removed 82% to 93% of that error. Split conformal prediction reached its nominal coverage in all 24 settings I tested (within 0.006), but the sets were large when accuracy was low: at 90% coverage, temperature-scaled sets contained on average 63% (Qwen, English), 78% (Qwen, Arabic), 86% (Jais, English) and 89% (Jais, Arabic) of the options. The margin between the top two option probabilities separated correct from incorrect answers best for Qwen on English (AUROC 0.734) and worst for Jais on Arabic (0.579). Answering only the 20% most confident questions lowered Qwen's English error from 0.439 to 0.098. Jais showed a strong prior for option C (49% of English and 60% of Arabic predictions). Scoring Latin letters only cut the Arabic share to 29% but left accuracy, raw ECE and AUROC nearly unchanged. Because the two benchmarks differ in subject, level and number of options, I do not attribute differences between languages to language itself. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Manifold Attracting Flow Matching: Physics-Constrained Sampling via Continuous Geometric Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16677)**  `arXiv:2611.16677`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guanyu Chen, Jinchen Wang, Shengze Xu, Xingyu Ren, Tieyong Zeng, Antonios Armaou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating scientific data under previously unseen equality constraints requires satisfying the prescribed constraints without destroying the physical structure learned from data. Existing inference-time approaches often enforce physical constraints by repeatedly correcting predicted endpoints, but such discrete corrections can distort learned generative structure and introduce unstable feedback. In this work, we propose Manifold Attracting Flow Matching (MAFM), which continuously evolves a persistent anchor through an ODE whose dynamics drive it analytically toward the constraint manifold. Specifically, its geometric correction preserves model-guided tangent motion while prescribing the normal motion to contract constraint violations toward the manifold. This construction separates model tracking from constraint attraction and provides theoretical guarantees for residual contraction and local feedback stabilization. Experiments across geometric and partial differential equation benchmarks demonstrate improved generation quality over variant constraint-guided baselines while maintaining low constraint residuals. MAFM provides a flexible framework for coordinating learned physical structure and explicit constraints through controllable sampling dynamics, without retraining the underlying generative model.
  </details>

- **[Learning Temporal Dynamics with Derivative Representations for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16514)**  `arXiv:2611.16514`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shujie Duan, Jia Hong Puah, Haocheng Wu, Yifei Mao, Hongting Zhou, Chenxuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting aims to predict the future evolution of complex systems by learning their underlying dynamics from past observations. Despite recent advances in deep learning, Transformers in particular still struggle to capture the temporal evolution of system dynamics. Existing approaches have been largely driven by architectural innovation, with comparatively little attention paid to training strategies that explicitly exploit the temporal structure governing system dynamics. In this work, we introduce DeltaTSF, a dynamics-aware forecasting framework based on discrete derivative representations of time series. Specifically, DeltaTSF jointly learns the series and their discrete temporal derivatives across both input and forecast horizons within a unified, model-agnostic framework. By coupling these representations over time, the model is encouraged to maintain temporal consistency, a property we show to induce a form of discrete Taylor consistency during training. This formulation enables the model to better reflect the underlying system dynamics, resulting in more stable and physically coherent forecasts. Notably, DeltaTSF does not require explicit derivative computation and introduces no additional computational overhead at inference time. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and backbone architectures demonstrate that DeltaTSF consistently improves forecasting performance and yields more dynamically coherent predictions than strong SOTA methods. These results highlight the effectiveness of enforcing dynamic coherence through derivative representations as a model-agnostic paradigm that operates alongside and complements architectural design. Source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[StateAlign: Internalizing State-Aware Decision-Making for Reliable Multi-Turn Tool Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16304)**  `arXiv:2611.16304`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhexi Lu, Subhajit Chaudhury, Tejaswini Pedapati, Keerthiram Murugesan, Lei Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has improved the tool-use capabilities of large language models. However, better task performance can coincide with increased tool hallucination. Reliable tool use requires decisions to follow the current task state: available information, tool capabilities, and task progress. Our analysis shows that agents sometimes execute when they should ask for information, retrieve it, or decline an infeasible request, even when reasoning identifies missing information. We introduce StateAlign, combining task-state construction with state-contrastive reinforcement learning. The data cover five task conditions and pair states with different action requirements. Outcome rewards guide task completion; token-level contrasts compare the same generated reasoning and actions under paired states to train outputs that fit the current state. Experiments across five base models from three families show improvements on BFCL v4 multi-turn, ToolSandbox, and When2Call. Ablations show that state construction improves performance and reliability under different reward designs. The complete method further reduces tool hallucination across three Qwen3 sizes. On Qwen3-8B, StateAlign improves BFCL v4 multi-turn accuracy from 45.5% to 55.2% and reduces no-tool hallucination on SimpleToolHalluBench from 62.2% under outcome-only training to 56.1%. Behavioral analyses show more consistent decisions, more complete multi-call execution, and stronger recovery after tool errors.
  </details>

- **[Flow-CAD: Flow Matching for Contextual Anomaly Detection and Explanation in Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16184)**  `arXiv:2611.16184`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhong Li, Qi Huang, Yuhang Wang, Yang Cao, Thomas Bäck, Niki van Stein, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contextual anomaly detection in tabular data aims to identify objects whose behavior is unexpected under their contextual conditions. In data-engineering pipelines, this task screens table rows whose measurements violate context-dependent integrity relationships. Most existing approaches rely on either shallow conditional models with limited distributional expressiveness or localized estimators that require per-context modeling, making it difficult to capture complex conditional relationships and scale to high-dimensional settings. We propose Flow-CAD, a new framework for contextual anomaly detection based on Flow Matching. Flow-CAD learns a single global and amortized conditional generative model that flexibly represents behavioral distributions conditioned on context, without assuming simple parametric forms or retraining local models. To enable robust and interpretable anomaly scoring, we introduce a scale-normalized and truncated residual energy derived from the learned conditional flow, which stabilizes anomaly scores, mitigates domination by extreme deviations, and supports feature-wise attribution. We further provide theoretical insights showing that the proposed residual-based anomaly score is stable under velocity estimation error, and that its Monte Carlo estimator satisfies concentration bounds. Experiments on 20 benchmark datasets with 13 competitors demonstrate that Flow-CAD has the best average rank for PR AUC, ROC AUC, and P@n, highlighting its effectiveness and practicality for contextual anomaly detection in tabular data. We provide the source code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Vocabulary-Free Next-Event Prediction on Temporal Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16063)**  `arXiv:2611.16063`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhishang Luo, Venu Madhav Tammali, Amila Weerasinghe, Osso Vahabzadeh, Olcay Boz, Xue Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive transformer training through next-token prediction has driven remarkable advances in language modeling and has become increasingly effective for time-series modeling. Yet its success in temporal graph learning remains limited, and most temporal link-prediction methods are discriminative rather than autoregressive. While temporal graphs can have a natural serialization through chronological ordering edges, they lack an obvious graph-independent tokenization: existing node tokenization often uses a vocabulary that grows with the number of nodes, limiting scalability and cross-graph training. We propose GTGT, Generative Temporal Graph Transformer, a simple yet powerful vocabulary-free framework for self-supervised next-event prediction on temporal graphs. It predicts events for observed destinations, behavioral descriptors for novel destinations, and time gaps before new event. These targets have shared meanings across graphs, allowing one transformer to train across disjoint node sets without node-specific learned parameters. Local node identifiers let attention capture graph structure: Theoretically, we can show that our transformer-based model has the capacity for time-respecting message passing mechanisms. On Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB), our GTGT not only achieves comparable or better link-prediction performance than established baselines, but also has much lower training cost. These results open the door to temporal graph pretraining for forecasting and other downstream tasks, providing a concrete step toward temporal graph foundation models.
  </details>

- **[WELP : Weighted Ensemble in the LooP for committor learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15963)**  `arXiv:2611.15963`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jacky Chen, Rishal Aggarwal, David Ryan Koes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding how complex molecular systems transition between metastable states is a central challenge in computational chemistry and biophysics. A key tool for characterizing these transitions is the committor function: the probability that a trajectory initiated from a given configuration reaches one state before another. Because the committor encodes both the progress and the mechanism of a transition, it is an ideal collective variable (CV). Learning it, however, requires sampling the rare transitions it describes. We address this circular dependency with a ``WE-in-the-loop'' framework that couples committor learning with weighted ensemble (WE), an enhanced sampling method that uses a CV to drive transition dynamics. Our framework alternates between the two: WE simulation generates transition data for training a neural network committor with a self-consistent loss, and the learned committor then serves as an improved CV for subsequent WE simulation. Across a two-channel 2D system, alanine dipeptide, and chignolin, our framework consistently outperforms greedy baselines, highlighting the importance of principled exploration. We also curate a public benchmark of empirically determined committor values for alanine dipeptide. Together, the framework and benchmark provide a new method for studying rare transitions in high-dimensional molecular systems and a standard for evaluating committor learning methods.
  </details>

- **[SpaceMCP: Vision Language Model Spatial Reasoning via Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15680)**  `arXiv:2611.15680`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ameen Izhac, Nobuko Yoshida, Ronald Clark_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. Recent progress in feed-forward 3D reconstruction has enabled precise, real-time geometric understanding from visual inputs. In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. Paired with the VLM's strong reasoning capabilities, the VLM is able to combine this primitive information to solve more complex spatial tasks. Without any training, SpaceMCP achieves state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench and VSTI-Bench, outperforming recent spatially post-trained VLMs and improving its base VLM by 15-22 points per benchmark. We argue that, given the contrast between the relatively slow progress in native VLM spatial reasoning and the rapid advances in feed-forward 3D computer vision, this paradigm offers a promising path forward for enabling robust spatial reasoning.
  </details>

- **[GLCR: Global-Local Collaborative Routing for Multi-Task Electromagnetic Signal Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15609)**  `arXiv:2611.15609`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fang Lu, Yao Lu, Zhuangzhi Chen, Sicheng Zhang, Shanqing Yu, Yun Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep neural networks for electromagnetic signal processing are typically developed as single-task experts with fixed inputs and objectives, limiting cross-task transferability. Electromagnetic foundation models offer broader task coverage but incur substantial pretraining and inference costs. To balance accuracy and efficiency, we adopt model routing to dynamically dispatch unknown signals to matched experts within a heterogeneous pool. We construct HeteroSignal-7, a multi-task routing benchmark with 140,000 samples from seven datasets covering six task types, paired with seven classification and waveform-recovery experts and a unified evaluation protocol. We then propose GLCR, a two-stage routing method combining global dispatch with local discrimination of similar tasks. First, a lightweight one-dimensional convolutional neural network (CNN) with an additive-margin head predicts one of seven routing classes. Second, a gradient-boosted-tree specialist uses four physical statistics to re-evaluate samples whose top two candidates match a confusion pair automatically identified from validation errors. The pipeline is trained across the full signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) range and requires neither SNR labels nor noise estimation at inference time. GLCR outperforms all 19 baselines, achieving 96.16% routing accuracy and a 76.30% system score, exceeding the respective second-best baselines by 1.16% and 1.14%. It achieves the lowest routing latency among the compared methods at 1.43 ms per sample and reduces expected overall system FLOPs by 5.9 times compared with executing all experts. This work provides a public evaluation platform and an efficient, reproducible baseline for heterogeneous expert routing in electromagnetic signal processing.
  </details>

- **[Observations Drift, Structures Remain: Structural Pretraining with Time Alignment for Electromagnetic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15566)**  `arXiv:2611.15566`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wenjin Gui, Junyu Shen, Haibo Xu, Yuchuang Sun, Luqing Luo, Wupeng Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electromagnetic signal modeling is increasingly moving toward self-supervised pretraining on heterogeneous I/Q observations. However, an observed waveform is jointly shaped by signal generation, propagation environments, and acquisition processes, which mix reusable structural regularities with observation-specific variations. Existing methods usually pursue unified pretraining with reconstruction objectives on mixed observations. This heterogeneity creates two key mismatches for reconstruction pretraining: (1) appearance mismatch, induced by propagation-dependent waveform variations; and (2) temporal mismatch, induced by acquisition-dependent sampling coordinates. These mismatches make observation-specific appearances and sampling coordinates easier to reconstruct than stable signal structures, inducing observational shortcuts. We identify this failure mode as observation-biased pretraining. To address this, we propose Structural Pretraining with Time Alignment for heterogeneous EM signals. It mitigates temporal mismatch with RoPE-fs, which maps token positions to sampling-rate-normalized physical-time positions, and reduces reliance on appearance shortcuts by replacing waveform reconstruction with masked prediction of discrete structural units. These units are organized into atomic structural units and emergent structural units that capture variable-length compositional patterns beyond fixed patch boundaries. Across diverse EM benchmarks, our method improves both full-supervision and few-shot adaptation over existing EM pretraining baselines, showing that the learned representations better preserve structural consistency when observations drift in waveform appearance and temporal scale.
  </details>

- **[What Conformal Selection Can Certify in CRISPR Off-Target Design:Benchmark Leakage, Free Baselines, and a Finite-Sample Cohort Requirement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15555)**  `arXiv:2611.15555`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Malekpouri, Somayeh Lotfi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal selection promises distribution-free, finite-sample control of the error rate of a decision, and has been proposed as a safety layer for machine-learning CRISPR guide design. We ask whether such a certificate is attainable from the validated off-target data that exist. The constraint is arithmetic: a conformal p-value cannot fall below one divided by the calibration cohort size plus one, so a Benjamini-Hochberg selection certifies a single candidate only when that cohort is at least the candidate pool size divided by the tolerated error rate. Public cohorts supply ten validated guides, whereas a pool of two thousand at a ten percent error rate needs twenty thousand; the accepted set is consequently empty in over ninety-six percent of splits. Two benchmarking findings explain why this went unnoticed. The standard CIRCLE-seq file lists ten guides under eighty on-target strings, so grouping cross-validation on the sequence column trains and tests on one guide; corrected, pair-level discrimination and per-guide fidelity collapse. With that leakage removed, a training-free position-weight score and a bare count of near-match genomic sites are not beaten by a trained oracle. We give the corrected protocol, the baselines a new model must beat, and the cohort size a certified pipeline needs.
  </details>

- **[The Illusion of Diversity: Aligning LLM Exploration via Effective Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15493)**  `arXiv:2611.15493`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoliang Fu, Jiaye Lin, Yangyi Fang, Cong Qin, Binbin Zheng, Chaowen Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has shifted the alignment paradigm of large language models from subjective preference to objective correctness, yet a fundamental disconnect persists between exploration diversity and reasoning validity. Existing strategies predominantly rely on "blind" entropy metrics, e.g., token-level or global sequence-level entropy, which structurally fail to distinguish between effective reasoning and potential hallucinations. This misalignment creates an illusion of diversity, where high entropy tends to signal chaotic degeneration rather than valuable exploration. To bridge this gap, we introduce Effective Entropy, a validity-aware entropy concept that quantifies diversity exclusively within the valid subspace and systematically filters out the noise of incorrect trajectories. Built on this concept, we instantiate VEPO, a simple Validity-aware Entropy Policy Optimization method that directly incorporates effective entropy into RLVR training. VEPO induces an adaptive attraction dynamic that drives up the probability of under-explored valid trajectories, a mechanism that matches the practical regime of large-scale autoregressive models where sequence probabilities remain extremely small. Experiments on six mathematical reasoning benchmarks with 1.5B and 7B DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models show consistent aggregate accuracy gains over GRPO and competitive repeated-sampling success. A direct evaluation with matched correct counts finds more distinct strategies on commonly solved MATH500 problems, supported by paired statistical tests and three-seed training comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Overcoming the Resolution Limit: Significance-Aware Regularization for Intersectional Fairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15427)**  `arXiv:2611.15427`  `cs.LG`  
  _Antonio Ferrara, André Panisson, Francesco Cozzi, Alan Perotti, Francesco Bonchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intersectional fairness requires evaluating models across fine-grained subgroups. However, as group definitions become more granular, the sample size per subgroup shrinks, causing fairness estimates to become increasingly unstable. Existing in-processing methods are certainty-blind: they penalize observed disparities without distinguishing statistically certified unfairness from sampling noise. This leads to fairness overfitting, where models sacrifice utility to "fix" spurious disparities in sparse regimes. To address this, we introduce the significant-excess metric family, a continuous, uncertainty-aware fairness score that measures only the portion of a subgroup's disparity exceeding what sampling variation can explain at a specific confidence level. Building on this metric, we propose SAFER (Significance-Aware Fairness Regularizer), a differentiable in-processing method that gates fairness penalties using a soft-count likelihood-ratio evidence statistic. SAFER satisfies a provable safety property: when every subgroup disparity lies well below the gate threshold, the unconstrained optimum is nearly stationary for SAFER. At that optimum, the back-propagated contribution of its significance-gated component is exponentially small, and the additional fairness contribution comes from a small ungated term with a fixed weight. Mechanism ablations across three benchmark datasets and purpose-built stress variants show that removing any component of SAFER (threshold smoothing, the significance gate, or a dense-tail term) produces the failure the theory predicts on at least one stress variant. In sparse intersectional settings, SAFER achieves a better fairness-utility Pareto frontier than the baseline methods, and it preserves utility when certified unfairness is absent.
  </details>

- **[MORA: Correcting Time Series Forecasts with Coarse Future Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15413)**  `arXiv:2611.15413`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hu Li, Kenan Li, Haonan Xu, longlong, Shuqin Gao, Yucheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent architectural advances have improved time-series forecasting. However, our analysis of forecasters with diverse architectures reveals a common pattern: a substantial share of residual energy is concentrated in a small number of low-frequency components. Simple linear probes further show that the forecasters' historical inputs still contain predictive information about errors in the forecast mean. Building on these findings, we propose Model-Output Residual Adaptation (MORA), a model-agnostic residual adapter that refines the outputs of frozen forecasters without requiring additional inputs. MORA estimates segment-wise means of the target sequence through structural extrapolation and learned estimation, rather than directly predicting backbone-specific residuals. It then converts discrepancies between these estimates and the corresponding statistics of the backbone forecast into stagewise corrections, scaling each update according to estimation reliability. Extensive experiments on twelve benchmarks with six forecasting backbones show average MSE reductions of 7.2% for long-term forecasting and 28.1% for short-term forecasting, with lower MSE in 96.2% of the 288 evaluated cases. The benefits also extend to three time-series foundation models evaluated at different context lengths. Together, these results demonstrate that predictable coarse-scale residuals can be effectively exploited to improve existing forecasts. Code repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MechBench: A Domain-Specific LLM Benchmark for Parallel Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15342)**  `arXiv:2611.15342`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhihao Xia, Tingting Wang, Hui Li, Yongheng Xing, Chunxu Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel mechanisms pose distinctive challenges to large language models (LLMs), as valid reasoning depends jointly on mechanism topology, loop closure, geometry, joint types, and modeling assumptions. Yet existing LLM benchmarks provide little systematic coverage of such domain-specific knowledge, constraint-sensitive analysis, and constructive design. To fill this gap, we introduce MECHBENCH, a 2,541-question benchmark for evaluating LLMs in parallel mechanisms across professional knowledge, mechanism analysis, and design. MECHBENCH contains standard and challenge tiers spanning six capability categories and nine executable analysis and design tasks. It is constructed through MECHFORGE, a domain-grounded framework that combines expert-organized knowledge, LLM-assisted question generation, programmatic instance generation, two-stage expert review, and task-aware scoring with exact matching, rubric-based evaluation, and program verification. We further develop MECHGPT as a diagnostic domain-adapted model. Experiments show that strong knowledge performance does not directly translate into executable mechanism reasoning: leading closed-service models achieve over 95 on the standard tier but only 47-60 on the challenge tier, while domain adaptation substantially improves MechGPT over its Qwen3-8B baselines but leaves a large gap in constrained analysis and design. These results establish MECHBENCH as a systematic testbed for measuring domain-specific LLM capabilities in mechanism science.
  </details>

- **[TabOmics: A Small, Open Tabular Foundation Model for Wide Omics Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15329)**  `arXiv:2611.15329`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea López-García, Luis Bote-Curiel, Francisco Javier Gimeno-Blanes, José Luis Rojo-Álvarez, Sergio Muñoz-Romero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omics studies often produce tables with thousands to tens of thousands of molecular features and few samples. Tabular foundation models use diverse strategies to predict labels for such tables, with some relying on in-context learning, while approaches to wide tables may involve subsampling, grouping, or compressing features. We present TabOmics, a small, open family of tabular foundation models with 7.3M parameters, pretrained exclusively on synthetic tables. TabOmics processes all features retained after preprocessing jointly and derives predictions, attention-derived feature-importance scores, and test sample embeddings from the same forward pass. On 46 classification tasks from three benchmarks of high-dimensional, low-sample-size tables, TabOmics v2.5 with eight ensemble members achieves the highest mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) among the evaluated models (0.943), outperforming TabPFN-3.5 with eight members on 32 tasks despite having about 30 times fewer parameters. With a single member, TabOmics v2.5 reaches a mean AUROC of 0.941, versus 0.942 for TabPFN-3.5 with eight members, with no significant paired difference under the reported task-level analysis. On synthetic tables, its feature-importance scores achieve feature recovery similar to that of TabPFN-Wide, and its predictions remain above chance at 30,000 features. On breast cancer tasks, the top ranked genes include PAM50 genes, which define the subtype labels, indicating concordance with the label definition rather than independent biological validation. The code, model weights, training recipes, and experimental artifacts are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[MOON-RL: Multi-Objective Optimization via Objective Space Navigation with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15227)**  `arXiv:2611.15227`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrii Artomov, Soheyl Massoudi, Jürg Schiffmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many engineering design problems involve multiple conflicting objectives and strict feasibility constraints. They are typically solved with population-based optimizers that require many evaluations and offer limited online control over where the search is focused. We present MOON-RL, a reinforcement-learning-based optimization framework that formulates constrained multi-objective search as preference-directed navigation in the objective space. In MOON-RL, a lightweight PPO policy receives objective-space and constraint-related observations and acts as an instance-specific adaptive search process. It learns how to update the design so that the current solution advances along a user-specified trade-off direction, while a simple meta-algorithm periodically resets the policy, resamples starting designs from a continuously updated archive of non-dominated solutions, and adjusts step-size and penalty schedules. We evaluate MOON-RL on real-world actuator-design problems and additional convergence-hard constrained benchmarks, comparing against established evolutionary algorithms under matched evaluation budgets. In focused search scenarios, MOON-RL achieves competitive performance in the region of interest, and ablation studies show that both the RL-driven adaptive sampling process and the meta-algorithm contribute to this behaviour, suggesting that repurposing RL training as objective-space navigation is a viable building block for interactive, preference-guided design tools. Additional experiments show that the framework also accommodates adaptive non-RL samplers, while PPO remains a strong choice for fixed-target, preference-directed objective-space navigation.
  </details>

- **[When Does Causality Help in Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15073)**  `arXiv:2611.15073`  `cs.LG`  
  _Long Zhang, Wei Huang, Aishui Rao, Jian Hu, Binxiang Wu, Jie Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causality-aware scoring is common in multivariate time-series anomaly detection, yet the literature rarely states when it helps. Holding one causality-guided autoencoder (DeCauAE) fixed, we vary graph quality, anomaly type and protocol against ground truth. Graph quality fixes the payoff: on a twenty-variable DAG the gain over a graph-free autoencoder decays as the graph is corrupted. Anomaly type fixes the target: the structural factor lifts window F1 from 0.800 to 0.976 where anomalies break conditional structure, and its advantage vanishes below a break severity s 0.3. Protocol fixes the ranking: point-adjusted F1 lets a random score match the full model, while range-aware VUS separates the autoencoder family from the classical baselines. On real benchmarks the causal module pays only where density makes a system eligible and cross-subsample reproducibility selects entities inside it; enabling it everywhere costs 0.012 VUS-ROC. The structural factor also transfers to classical detectors .
  </details>

- **[PhysXAI: A Theoretical Framework for Diagnosing Physical Hallucinations in Weather Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15053)**  `arXiv:2611.15053`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anjan Mahapatra, Nikhil Raj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern machine learning models for global weather forecasting achieve remarkable predictive accuracy, yet they frequently produce states that violate fundamental physical laws, including conservation of mass, hydrostatic balance, or spectral energy scaling. We term these outputs physical hallucinations and propose PhysXAI, a rigorous diagnostic framework that detects, localises, and attributes such inconsistencies. Unlike existing post-hoc metric suites, PhysXAI is a learnable system that explicitly decouples forecast error from physical plausibility. We formalise the problem through a novel joint condition on numerical and physical residuals, and introduce a multi-branch encoder that extracts representations from conservation, dynamical, spectral, cross-variable, and spatiotemporal residuals. A key innovation is a physics-consistency contrastive learning objective, which we theoretically show the latent representation to be approximately isometric to the physical residual space, ensuring that diagnostic decisions remain grounded in physically meaningful deviations. We further derive an adaptive multi-objective loss with gradient-based weighting to balance competing diagnostic objectives. The framework produces spatially resolved hallucination maps, violation-type classifications, and calibrated Bayesian uncertainty, enabling both detection and interpretable attribution of physical inconsistencies. We conduct a comprehensive comparative evaluation of PhysXAI across ERA5, WeatherBench 2, and MERRA-2, using forecasts from GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, and NeuralGCM, with Python-written code and a fully reproducible experimental protocol. Our theoretical analysis and empirical evaluation establish PhysXAI as a principled framework for diagnosing physical hallucinations and advancing trustworthy AI for weather forecasting.
  </details>

- **[AI4Plasma I: Data-Efficient Surrogate Modeling of Capacitively Coupled Plasma Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15002)**  `arXiv:2611.15002`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenyu Zou, Junjue Liao, Hao Yang, Luowei Yin, Chenyu Wang, Yufeng Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference capacitively coupled plasma (CCP) simulations provide spatially resolved plasma fields but are costly to generate across operating conditions. We compare the data efficiency of five surrogate architectures--Coordinate MLP, PCA-Net, U-Net, Fourier neural operator (FNO), and Transolver--for predicting RF-cycle-averaged electron density, ion density, electric potential, and electron temperature in 1D planar and 2D axisymmetric GEC CCP benchmarks. We assess the number of reference simulations needed to achieve 1% and 2% mean relative L2 error for parameter-interior and parameter-boundary test cases, and examine architecture-specific mechanisms. FNO achieves the lowest or joint-lowest reference-case requirement in seven of eight comparisons. In 1D, FNO reaches 1% interior error with 50 training cases, compared with 100 for Transolver and 200 for U-Net. In 2D, FNO and U-Net each reach 1% interior error with 100 cases; FNO and Transolver each reach 2% parameter-boundary error with 200 cases, compared with 400 for U-Net. At the stricter 1% 2D parameter-boundary target, Transolver requires 400 cases versus 800 for FNO. Additional analyses investigate FNO spectral modes and padding, U-Net skip connections, Transolver adaptive slicing, and PCA-Net projection versus prediction error. The results support FNO as a data-efficient baseline for the tested CCP settings, while showing that the preferred architecture depends on the accuracy target and operating-parameter region.
  </details>

- **[Generalize Then Personalize: Wearable-Based Heart Rate Forecasting Under Scarce Ruck-March Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14849)**  `arXiv:2611.14849`  `cs.LG`  
  _Muntaqim Ahmed Raju, Ruizhe Ma, John W. Ramsay, David J. Cornell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heart rate (HR) forecasting may provide advance information about near-future cardiovascular response, potentially supporting physiological monitoring and workload-management decisions during ruck marching. However, robust model development is constrained by the difficulty of collecting large-scale ruck-march datasets, creating a scarce-data problem in which models must generalize across participants while adapting to individuals from limited personal data. To address this problem, we benchmark population-only training (POT), personalized-from-scratch training (PFST), and population-pretrained fine-tuning (PPFT) for HR forecasting during loaded ruck marching using data from eight active-duty U.S. Army medics across thirteen sessions. LSTM, Attention-LSTM, and CNN-LSTM backbones were evaluated against a persistence baseline at 30 s and 60 s horizons. Across all neural architectures and horizons, PPFT yielded lower mean MAE, RMSE, and endpoint MAE than both POT and PFST. Participant-level paired analysis after averaging across three training seeds showed that, relative to PFST, PPFT reduced trajectory MAE by 2.22-4.30 bpm and endpoint MAE by 1.24-2.88 bpm. All six trajectory-MAE comparisons remained significant after Holm adjustment ($p_{adj}$ = 0.0469), while four of six endpoint-MAE comparisons remained significant after adjustment. At 30 s, persistence achieved the lowest MAE (3.555 $\pm$ 1.274 bpm), while CNN-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest RMSE (4.669 $\pm$ 1.916 bpm); LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest endpoint MAE (5.093 $\pm$ 2.365 bpm). At 60 s, Attention-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest MAE (5.573 $\pm$ 1.939 bpm) and RMSE (7.134 $\pm$ 2.260 bpm), while CNN-LSTM PPFT achieved the lowest endpoint MAE (7.553 $\pm$ 2.357 bpm). These results suggest that population pretraining followed by participant-specific fine-tuning may provide a practical strategy for scarce-data wearable HR forecasting.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Row-Level Prediction: Evaluating and Learning Composition for Reusable Table Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14697)**  `arXiv:2611.14697`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Younes, Saeed Ghoorchian, Maximilian Schambach, Georgia Chalvatzaki, Johannes Höhne_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tables are compositional, with content spread across a schema, cell values, rows, and columns. Large-scale operations such as retrieval, overlap estimation, deduplication, and data curation need these local signals composed into one reusable embedding vector per table. Table representation methods, however, are mostly evaluated through row-level prediction or supervised downstream tasks, which do not measure table-level embedding quality directly. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates table-level embeddings through four desiderata: consistency under partial views, discriminability across label granularities, robustness to benign perturbations, and efficiency. Across twelve methods on synthetic and real corpora, lightweight schema- and text-based fingerprints are among the strongest. The sharpest contrast comes from two tabular foundation models, TabPFN and ConTextTab: although both are strong row-level predictors, their states, composed by mean, max, or three further parameter-free pooling rules, trail the strongest fingerprints. We therefore ask whether the gap lies in the encoder or in the compositional rule. Keeping ConTextTab frozen, we learn only the compositional rule over its cell states, using support-conditioned distribution matching (SCDM), a self-supervised loss we propose that matches the distribution of embedding similarities to the distribution of partition overlaps within and across tables. Across all fifteen head configurations, the learned rule raises the Spearman correlation between embedding similarity and partial-view overlap from 0.04 to 0.43-0.50 on synthetic data and improves triplet ranking, clustering, and retrieval, with smaller but systematic gains on real data. A downstream parent-table retrieval task confirms these findings. How local states are composed, and with what objective, substantially affects the quality of table-level embeddings.
  </details>

- **[Continual-ARC: Measuring Skill Acquisition over a Working Lifetime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14505)**  `arXiv:2611.14505`  `cs.LG`  
  _Daniel Lenton, Yusha Arif, Haris Mahmood_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses have emerged as a promising mechanism for continual learning, alongside test-time parametric learning. However, the current benchmarks have several limitations: they generally focus on a single applied domain, serve each instance only once, and assume a single family of learner, so memory-based and weight-based systems are rarely compared on the same scale. We propose Continual-ARC, a lifelong stream built from the three ARC-AGI editions, whose tasks need no domain knowledge. The same tasks return after dormant gaps with fresh instances, while new tasks keep arriving. On each instance, the learner may pay for demonstrations, submit answers, or act inside the harness, with retries permitted on incorrect submissions, which are paid for. Each instance is scored based on the share of this feedback budget consumed. Since a learner may keep anything between instances, memory-based and weight-based systems are scored on the same scale. After the stream, the learner is frozen and tested on fresh instances of the tasks it met, which measures memorization, and on unseen tasks, which measures generalization. We run an agent harness with frozen weights, with and without persistence, on Qwen3.5-9B and GLM-5.3 Flash, comparing them with two prompting floors, a program-induction method and Qwen3.5-9B's weights trained to imitate GLM-5.3 Flash. Persistence in the harness generally does not improve memorization unless provided with explicit expert guidance. Most harness-based methods still learn less than a floor that simply caches each task's demonstrations, across all models we tested. No harness-based method gains on unseen tasks, whereas weight-based learning shows substantial gains. This opens the door to future weight-based continual learning that could teach models to drive harnesses in new domains.
  </details>
