- **[Correct First, Fast Second: What a Local 7B Code Model Can and Cannot Do for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12079)**  `arXiv:2611.12079`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jui-Chien Tsou, Po-Yi Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how much the interaction strategy around a fixed, locally hosted 7B-parameter code model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) affects its ability to replace PyTorch operators with custom CUDA kernels. On 30 KernelBench tasks (levels 1-3, first 10 each, evaluated on a V100), we compare four regimes of increasing structure: zero-shot prompting, profiling-guided prompting, iterative refinement on execution feedback, and a multi-agent loop (analysis, retrieval, generation, evaluation, feedback triage) in which the same model fills four roles around a non-LLM evaluator. Three findings stand out. (1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[CHARM: Certified Homology-Aware Reverse-scale Mapping via Diffusion-Guided Quotient Towers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10375)**  `arXiv:2611.10375`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional dimensionality-reduction methods optimize geometric fidelity but do not return auditable homology certificates or identify the input observations associated with nontrivial classes. Direct high-dimensional persistent-homology computation is costly, and its representatives are rarely linked to a shared planar view. We introduce Certified Homology-Aware Reverse-scale Mapping (CHARM), which augments two-dimensional coordinates with algebraically certified, sample-traceable structural records. Diffusion-guided cover proposals and homotopy-safe contractions compress the input neighborhood flag complex. A Cover Quotient Tower supports cross-scale representative tracking and exact $H_k$ certification when algebraic tests complete. Reverse-scale layout connects quotient-chain preimages to the same coordinates used for geometric exploration. Contraction preserves the input neighborhood flag-complex homotopy type; the quotient tower inherits a multiplicative 3-interleaving with coarse-hop Rips, and conditional distortion bounds connect it to original Euclidean Rips. In a fixed-seed benchmark of 22 datasets (41,637 samples), CHARM achieves the highest share of first- or second-place scores across 15 metrics. On nonsynthetic data, it ranks first or second on every metric and first on all four $H_0/H_1$ persistence-diagram metrics. CHARM couples strong nonsynthetic geometric performance with an auditable structural layer.
  </details>

- **[Sheaf-MPNN: A Library for Sheaf Neural Networks as Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04670)**  `arXiv:2611.04670`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alessio Borgi, Mario Severino, Luke Braithwaite, Steve Azzolin, Rémi Bourgerie, Olga Zaghen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) implicitly assume that neighboring nodes share the same feature space, which contributes to oversmoothing and poor performance on heterophilic graphs. Sheaf Neural Networks (SNNs) remove this assumption by giving each node and edge its own vector space and learning restriction maps that transform features as they move between neighbors. These maps define a sheaf Laplacian that drives diffusion over the graph, but existing implementations often assemble it explicitly as a global sparse block matrix, which adds memory and computational overhead and can hinder scalability. In this paper, we introduce Sheaf-MPNN, a PyTorch Geometric library that reformulates sheaf diffusion as local incidence-wise transport and aggregation, eliminating the need to construct the global Laplacian. Sheaf-MPNN implements fifteen families of SNNs under a unified message-passing framework, each supporting both homogeneous and heterogeneous graphs, and directed and undirected settings, with diagonal, general, orthogonal, and low-rank restriction maps and attention-based predictors. We further provide a unified benchmarking suite covering node- and graph-level tasks, heterogeneous graph learning, and computational efficiency, enabling systematic and reproducible comparisons across architectures. We establish theoretical equivalence between these message-passing formulations and their original counterparts and identify the architectural mechanisms needed to preserve their propagation dynamics. This unified perspective provides a principled foundation for implementing, comparing, and extending sheaf-based architectures within standard graph learning frameworks. Source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Noise-shaped Sampling and Annealing for Scalable On-the-edge Training of Binary and Ternary AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22621)**  `arXiv:2610.22621`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zihao Chen, Zhili Xiao, Rui-Jie Zhu, Yipeng Wang, Jason Eshraghian, Shantanu Chakrabartty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The training loss of binary and ternary neural networks, expanded as a multilinear polynomial over the discrete weights, is structurally equivalent to the energy of a hierarchical high-order Ising model. We exploit this equivalence to apply Ising solvers to discrete-weight network training, while accounting for a fundamental difference in objectives: an Ising solver seeks the ground state of a fixed energy function, whereas training seeks solutions that generalize to unseen data. Our key contribution is $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler, an optimizer that reconciles these objectives by embedding a stochastic sampling-and-annealing step as a noisy quantizer inside a first-order $\Sigma\Delta$ noise-shaping loop. The loop pushes noise from weight quantization, high-order frustration, and mini-batch sampling out of the low-frequency adaptation band, so that discrete weight updates follow the smooth descent trajectories embedded in the rugged loss landscape. We validate the framework on a planted quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) benchmark with a known minimizer; on CIFAR-10, where a $13$M-parameter ternary model comes within $1\%$ of full-precision accuracy; on ImageNet-1k, where a ternary ResNet-18 comes within $0.4\%$ Top-5 accuracy of a ternary baseline; on class-incremental continual learning, where prior-task accuracy is preserved; and on MatMul-free language models up to $2.7$B parameters, where it outperforms a straight-through-estimator baseline under matched continued pre-training. Compared with standard quantization-aware training, $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler reduces the training memory footprint by over $50\%$, delivers an order-of-magnitude speed-up, and reduces weight-update memory traffic by four orders of magnitude, making it attractive for on-device training and fine-tuning of large binary and ternary models.
  </details>

- **[Pro-t-VAE: Closed-Form Student's t Mixtures for Robust Prototype Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14894)**  `arXiv:2610.14894`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhikang Xu, Jiarui Xing, Jian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a robust deep generative framework for unsupervised image prototype discovery, built on a heavy-tailed Student's t mixture prior, that bridges statistical robustness and mathematical tractability. Existing generative approaches typically rely on noise-prone Gaussian priors or computationally intensive diffusion models; neither simultaneously provides explicit latent structure nor exact analytic optimization. While diffusion models can yield strong priors, iterative sampling and intractable likelihoods limit scalability and interpretability for prototype discovery. We address these issues by constructing a Student's t latent space via a Bayesian scale-mixture formulation. This yields a fully closed-form augmented ELBO for unsupervised Student's t mixture variational autoencoders, which, to our knowledge, is the first such objective applied to robust image prototype discovery, avoiding biased variational approximations and high computational costs in prior heavy-tailed or diffusion-based frameworks. Treating precision as a latent variable automatically modulates each data point's influence, downweighting outliers to learn a data-dependent measure of trust. The result is improved robustness, accuracy, and efficiency, with well-separated latent representations and more stable manifold learning than state-of-the-art methods. We validate our model on standard vision benchmarks and challenging neuroimaging tasks. In brain MRI-driven neurodegenerative disease analysis, our model recovers clean, anatomically coherent prototypes where clinical noise often collapses rare subtypes. These results highlight the ability of our framework to preserve anatomy-associated patterns for precise discovery in clinical settings. Our code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PatchKV: Weight-Space Compensation of KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39329)**  `arXiv:2609.39329`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chanryeol Lee, Chanhyuk Lee, Yeonwoo Choi, Donggyun Kim, Seunghoon Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context inference with Large Language Models (LLMs) is bottlenecked by the linearly growing memory of the key-value (KV) cache. Existing compression methods reduce the cache through token eviction or approximation, but degrade sharply at aggressive compression budgets. We propose PatchKV, a training-free framework that compensates KV cache compression methods by carrying part of the context in the model's weights. PatchKV pairs an off-the-shelf compressed KV cache with a context-specific weight patch, which is computed once at context-loading time and served for downstream queries for the context. The weight patch is derived in closed form via ridge regression, by aligning the block-wise activations of context-derived reference query tokens under the full cache and the compressed cache. Once merged into the model, the patch leaves the forward graph and per-query inference cost unchanged in the single-context, multi-query setting. Across long-context QA (SCBench with up to 170K tokens, SQuAD, NIAH) and math (GSM8K) benchmarks on three model architectures, PatchKV consistently improves cache compression methods, suggesting an alternative direction to compensate them at aggressive budgets. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Free Knob: A Sensitivity Audit for Calibration in Threshold-based Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33457)**  `arXiv:2609.33457`  `cs.LG`  
  _Md Tanveer Hossain Munim, Bijoy Ahmed Saiem, Al-Amin Sany, Tanzima Hashem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many benchmarks for rare events score a prediction in three steps: pool the prediction and the target over spatial blocks, threshold each pooled field, and score the resulting contingency table. We show that at a fixed rare threshold the max-pooled Critical Success Index (CSI) cannot separate two different things: how well a system places events, and how well its amplitudes are calibrated. Sharp observations push many blocks above the threshold, while the attenuated predictions of squared-error regression leave those same blocks below it. A monotone calibration map is fitted on held-out data and applied separately to each system. The map needs no retraining and cannot change the order of any two pixels, so whatever part of a difference it reproduces cannot be evidence of better spatial ranking. We report how much of a reported difference the map removes. On SEVIR, two released checkpoints of one architecture differ by -29.5% in extreme-threshold CSI before the control and by +5.3% after it. Across 450 pairwise contrasts among 6 systems, how far apart two systems sit in pooled frequency bias predicts how far their CSI difference moves under the control (r = 0.896 when the map is fitted after pooling), and 64 contrasts reverse sign. At CasCast's published extreme-event operating point, calibration alone removes 88%-106% of the cascade's gain over its backbone across three controls. Calibration also reveals advantages hidden by a better-calibrated baseline. On geostationary infrared imagery, a larger share of the score can be explained by calibration as events get rarer. The problem therefore needs two things at the same time: a threshold that is fixed in advance, and a model whose output is poorly calibrated at that threshold. We recommend reporting pooled frequency bias and a symmetric held-out FreeKnob Audit alongside rare-event pool-and-threshold scores.
  </details>

- **[KG-Guard: Graph-Based Hallucination Detection for Knowledge Base Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00328)**  `arXiv:2606.00328`  `cs.LG`  
  _Albert Sawczyn, Piotr Bielak, Tomasz Kajdanowicz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for knowledge base question answering (KBQA), also referred to as knowledge graph question answering (KGQA), where answering requires selecting entities from a question-specific knowledge graph subgraph. Yet LLMs are known to hallucinate across tasks, and KBQA is no exception: even when we provide a graph as the knowledge source, the model may rely on parametric knowledge instead of graph evidence or perform invalid reasoning over the given relations. Such hallucinated answer nodes can limit the practical deployment of KBQA systems, especially in high-stakes domains such as healthcare. We formulate hallucination detection in KBQA as an answer-node classification problem and propose a lightweight graph-based framework that treats the answering LLM as a black box. KG-Guard represents each KBQA instance as an augmented graph. It initializes node features with semantic representations of KG entities, marks topic entities and LLM-proposed answer nodes with learned vectors, and connects a virtual question node to the topic entities. A graph encoder then produces verification-oriented node representations, and a small MLP classifies each proposed answer node using its graph representation together with the question embedding. Experiments on WebQSP, ComplexWebQuestions, and PUGG show that our detector achieves the best average rank across all three benchmarks (F1 82.0, 87.4, and 84.3), while using ${\sim}$305$\times$ fewer parameters than LLM-based baselines. It also retains the best average rank when jointly evaluated across an extended set of pipelines and settings. Beyond detection, the node-level feedback is actionable: when flagged answers are fed back to the KBQA system for iterative refinement, downstream KBQA F1 improves by 13.0-14.5 points and Exact Match by 16.9-17.6 points.
  </details>

- **[Fairness-Aware Federated Learning with Trajectory Shapley Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30336)**  `arXiv:2605.30336`  `cs.LG`  
  _Daniel Kuznetsov, Ziqi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning is an emerging distributed paradigm that addresses the challenges posed by heterogeneous, privacy-sensitive data. It enables multiple clients to train a model collaboratively by aggregating their local updates at a server. However, conventional aggregation schemes typically use fixed weights that fail to reflect unequal and time-varying client contributions, leading to biased and unstable learning. To improve fairness and stability, we propose the Trajectory Shapley Value (TSV), a contribution metric that evaluates how each client influences the optimization trajectory of the global model using a validation-based, temporally consistent utility. Building on TSV, we design FedTSV, an adaptive aggregation method that converts per-round evaluations into dynamic client weights, allowing the server to respond to heterogeneous and adversarial participation in real time. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that FedTSV accelerates convergence, improves robustness, and yields more equitable contribution assessments, thereby providing a principled foundation for fairness-aware federated optimization. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where LLM Annotators Fail: Label-Free Learning on Graphs with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27913)**  `arXiv:2605.27913`  `cs.LG`  
  _Safal Thapaliya, Jiatan Huang, Chuxu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Node classification on graphs often requires labeled nodes, yet obtaining labels at graph scale is expensive. When node attributes contain semantic content, such as paper abstracts, web pages, or product descriptions, large language models (LLMs) can provide low-cost supervision by annotating a small subset of nodes. However, these LLM-generated labels are noisy, and existing label-free graph learning methods usually treat this noise as either global or class-conditional. We find that LLM annotation errors are not only class-dependent but also region-dependent: within the same class, reliability can vary sharply across feature-space clusters. In light of this, we propose Cluster-Aware Noise Estimation (CANE), a label-free learning framework that estimates cluster-conditional LLM reliability without ground truth labels, and uses this estimate to decide which pseudo-labels to trust, and which labels to correct. Across various graph benchmarks and GNN backbones, CANE improves over the strongest label-free baselines, with the largest gains on datasets exhibiting stronger cluster-conditional noise. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[How Hard Is It to Rig a Benchmark? A Social Choice Analysis of Leaderboard Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23628)**  `arXiv:2605.23628`  `cs.LG`  
  _Polina Gordienko, Georg Schollmeyer, Frauke Kreuter, Christoph Jansen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task benchmarks have become a central pillar of machine learning research, yet their growing influence has incentivised benchmark gaming -- strategic actions taken to improve the leaderboard rank of a specific model. Treating datasets as voters and models as candidates, we consider benchmark-specific training -- the inclusion of benchmark data in training -- as a form of election manipulation. For any ordinal benchmark, the problem of choosing datasets to train on so that a target model becomes top-ranked corresponds to shift bribery, a class of manipulation problems from computational social choice. Leveraging this identification, we show that the benchmark-specific training problem is NP-hard under Borda count and mean win rate. Complementing this worst-case perspective, we introduce the instance-level robustness, the minimum number of datasets a model developer must include in training to top a given leaderboard, and derive expressions for it under arithmetic mean, median, mean win rate and pairwise majority. We evaluate these expressions on MMLU under HELM and on BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) under the Open LLM Leaderboard. Across both suites, mean win rate is hardest to manipulate: this gap is clear on BBH (24 tasks, 4507 models), where its median robustness is 22 tasks (92%), compared with 13 (54%) under arithmetic mean and 12 (50%) under median and pairwise majority.
  </details>

- **[Retain-Neutral Surrogates for Min-Max Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05871)**  `arXiv:2605.05871`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junhao Cai, Dohun Kim, Dowon Kim, Sung Il Choi, Chengjun Jin, Juhyun Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to make a model forget designated training data while retaining performance on the rest. In min-max unlearning, an inner adversary perturbs the model to maximize the forget loss, and the model is updated to minimize the retain loss at the resulting surrogate point. When the forget and retain gradients are positively coupled, the standard perturbation also moves toward higher retain loss, and the retain step begins by recovering that loss. We propose Retain-Orthogonal Surrogate Unlearning (ROSU), which redefines the adversary to admit only perturbations orthogonal to the retain gradient. The one that raises the forget loss fastest has a closed form, and ROSU uses it to set the surrogate point and amplifies it into the forgetting step. Under positive coupling the ROSU surrogate avoids the retain cost that coupling induces in the standard surrogate, and the difference shrinks as the gradients approach orthogonality. ROSU has the smallest accuracy gap to retraining in every vision setting (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet) and is competitive with the baselines on language benchmarks (TOFU, WMDP). The redefinition helps most under high positive coupling. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Prediction to Practice: A Task-Aware Evaluation Framework for Blood Glucose Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00645)**  `arXiv:2605.00645`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alireza Namazi, Heman Shakeri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical time-series forecasting is increasingly studied for decision support, yet standard aggregate metrics can obscure whether a model is actually useful for the task it is meant to serve. In safety-critical settings, low average error can coexist with dangerous failures in exactly the high-risk regimes that matter most. We present a task-aware evaluation framework for blood glucose forecasting built around two downstream uses: hypoglycemia early warning and insulin dosing decision support. For early warning, we evaluate on real data from three clinical cohorts using event-level recall and false alarms per patient-day, metrics that reflect operational alarm burden rather than aggregate accuracy. We show that models appearing acceptable overall, with recall above 0.9 on the full test set, can fail badly in the post-bolus slice, where insulin-on-board is elevated and missed warnings carry the greatest clinical consequences. Standard forecasting evaluation, however, does not test whether a model can reason about the effects of actions, a requirement for supporting insulin dosing decisions. We therefore add a second, interventional arm using the FDA-accepted UVA/Padova simulator, where we evaluate whether forecasters can predict glucose responses to altered insulin plans in paired factual/counterfactual scenarios. We show that models that look strong on real-data forecasting often fail to predict the direction, magnitude, or ranking of intervention effects, and choose poor insulin doses when evaluated under a clinically motivated cost. Taken together, the two arms reveal a consistent gap between forecasting accuracy and task-relevant usefulness. We release the benchmark, the standardized preprocessing pipeline for public cohorts, and the simulator-based interventional dataset as a reproducible toolkit.
  </details>

- **[Reward-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05066)**  `arXiv:2603.05066`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michal Nauman, Marek Cygan, Pieter Abbeel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sample-efficient reinforcement learning typically uses a fixed reward function both to guide exploration and to supervise learning. However, the same experience can provide supervision under multiple related reward specifications, even when the objective of interest remains fixed. We introduce Reward-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning (RCRL), an off-policy approach that collects experience under a nominal objective while training a reward-conditioned agent using counterfactual rewards recomputed from replay. RCRL constructs alternative reward specifications by perturbing the nominal reward, providing diverse supervision without additional environment interaction. Across single-task, multi-task, and vision-based control benchmarks, RCRL improves nominal task performance and sample efficiency when integrated with strong RL backbones. As a secondary capability, the learned policies support zero-shot behavioral adjustment toward related objectives without further training. These results demonstrate that diverse reward supervision can improve learning of a predefined objective while also enabling policy steerability.
  </details>

- **[TENG-BC: Time-Evolving Natural Gradient for High-Accuracy Neural PDE Solvers with General Boundary Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00397)**  `arXiv:2603.00397`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongjie Jiang, Di Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately solving time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) with neural networks remains challenging due to long-time error accumulation and the difficulty of enforcing general boundary conditions. We introduce TENG-BC, a high-precision neural PDE solver based on the Time-Evolving Natural Gradient, designed to perform under generic boundary constraints. At each time step, TENG-BC performs a boundary-aware optimization that jointly enforces interior dynamics and boundary conditions, accommodating Dirichlet, Neumann, Robin, and mixed types within a unified framework. This formulation admits a natural-gradient interpretation, enabling stable time evolution without delicate penalty tuning. Across benchmarks over diffusion, transport, and nonlinear PDEs with various boundary conditions, TENG-BC achieves solver-level accuracy under comparable sampling budgets, outperforming conventional solvers and physics-informed neural network (PINN) baselines.
  </details>

- **[Neural Optimal Transport in Hilbert Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14086)**  `arXiv:2602.14086`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jae-Hwan Choi, Jiwoo Yoon, Dohyun Kwon, Jaewoong Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Functional data such as time series, trajectories, and fields are increasingly central in machine learning and are naturally modeled in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. We study Semi-dual Neural Optimal Transport (SNOT) for learning transport maps between probability measures on separable Hilbert spaces. A critical challenge in this setting is the spurious solution problem: a neural map can globally optimize the SNOT objective while failing to recover an optimal transport map. We show that this failure arises from non-regular source measures, which make the inner minimization in the semi-dual objective non-identifiable. We prove that regular source measures, defined via Gaussian null sets, restore inner-minimizer uniqueness and recover the Monge map. For singular sources, we introduce Gaussian smoothing and establish a necessary and sufficient kernel condition for smoothing to restore regularity. We further prove plan-level consistency: as smoothing vanishes, every Wasserstein accumulation point of the induced optimal plans solves the original Kantorovich problem. Experiments on synthetic singular transports and real-world unpaired time-series imputation benchmarks validate the theory, achieving the best performance on several benchmarks and competitive performance on the remaining ones.
  </details>

- **[Ultra Fast PDE Solving via Physics Guided Few-step Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03627)**  `arXiv:2602.03627`  `cs.LG`  
  _Cindy Xiangrui Kong, Yueqi Wang, Haoyang Zheng, Weijian Luo, Guang Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based models have demonstrated impressive accuracy and generalization in solving partial differential equations (PDEs). However, their practical utility still suffers from high sampling costs and insufficient physical consistency, mainly due to many-step iterative sampling and insufficient physics guidance. To address these issues, we propose \emph{Phys-Instruct}, a physics-guided distillation framework that compresses a pre-trained diffusion PDE solver into a few-step generator while explicitly enforcing PDE consistency through distillation guidance. We reformulate the training objective as a constrained problem with PDE residual, and derive a score-function identity that leads to a practical physics-guided generator update. Across five PDE benchmarks, Phys-Instruct achieves orders-of-magnitude faster inference while reducing the PDE Error by factors of $2.4$ to $15.5$, compared with the corresponding 200-step EDM teachers. Moreover, the resulting unconditional student model functions as a compact prior, enabling efficient and physically consistent inference for various downstream conditional tasks. Our results indicate that Phys-Instruct is an effective and efficient framework for ultra-fast PDE solving powered by generative models.
  </details>

- **[Next-Latent Prediction Transformers Learn Compact World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.05963)**  `arXiv:2511.05963`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jayden Teoh, Manan Tomar, Kwangjun Ahn, Edward S. Hu, Tim Pearce, Pratyusha Sharma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers replace recurrence with a memory that grows with sequence length and self-attention that enables ad-hoc lookups over past tokens. Consequently, they lack an inherent incentive to compress history into compact latent states with consistent transition rules. This often leads to learning solutions that generalize poorly. We introduce Next-Latent Prediction (NextLat), which extends standard next-token training with self-supervised predictions in the latent space. Specifically, NextLat trains a transformer to learn latent representations that are predictive of its next latent state given the next token. Theoretically, we show that these latents converge towards belief states, compressed information about the history necessary to predict the future. This simple auxiliary objective injects a recurrent inductive bias into transformers while leaving their architecture, parallel training efficiency, and inference unchanged. NextLat effectively encourages transformers to form compact internal world models with coherent belief states and transition dynamics -- crucial properties not guaranteed by standard next-token prediction alone. Empirically, across benchmarks in world modeling, reasoning, planning, and language modeling, NextLat demonstrates significant gains over standard next-token prediction and other baselines in downstream accuracy, representation compression, and lookahead planning. Furthermore, NextLat enables variable-length self-speculative decoding, accelerating inference by up to 3.3x in language modeling. NextLat offers a simple yet effective paradigm for learning compact, predictive representations in transformers that generalize better. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CS-54k: A Paper-Grounded Corpus and Benchmark for AI Research QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20279)**  `arXiv:2510.20279`  `cs.LG`  
  _Penghao Wang, Yuhao Zhou, Mengxuan Wu, Ziheng Qin, Bangyuan Zhu, Shengbin Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to support computer science research. To contribute reliably, these systems must be able to work with information distributed across research papers and across different aspects of the research process. However, large-scale resources that systematically organize such information into structured supervision for both model evaluation and post-training remain limited. To address this gap, we propose a systematic paper-to-QA construction pipeline that organizes information from AI research papers into structured QA pairs supported by source evidence. Using this pipeline, we construct CS-54k, a 54k-pair corpus derived from 14,474 openly licensed AI research papers. The corpus covers eight recurring paper components, including research domain, prior work, existing challenges, motivation, findings and assumptions, methods, experimental settings, and experimental results. We further derive two complementary subsets: CS-4k, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on paper-derived research QA, and CS-50k, a companion corpus for post-training. Extensive evaluations on CS-4k reveal substantial performance differences across existing LLMs and paper components, with experimental settings and results remaining particularly challenging. Moreover, post-training on CS-50k substantially improves Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on CS-4k, demonstrating the practical utility of structured, paper-derived supervision. Together, CS-4k and CS-50k provide a unified and scalable resource for benchmarking and improving LLMs on research paper question answering.
  </details>

- **[HeadIO: Body-Frame Heading Decomposition for Neural Inertial Odometry on Head-Mounted Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14895)**  `arXiv:2611.14895`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Taeyoung Yeon, Yiquan Li, Chenfeng Gao, Vasco Xu, Karan Ahuja_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inertial odometry (IO) using only Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) enables camera-free head tracking in head-mounted devices such as VR headsets, AR glasses, and earbuds. Prior IO methods operate in a gravity-aligned world frame, which is not yaw-equivariant. Yaw augmentation improves robustness but does not guarantee equivariance; learned equivariant layers achieve it, but EqNIO's full pipeline costs nearly 3$\times$ the FLOPs of ours. While raw body-frame IMU inputs are inherently yaw-invariant, head tilt redistributes both gravity and motion signals across device axes, making the representation tilt-variant. We propose HeadIO, which combines a body-frame heading decomposition with a bidirectional Mamba backbone for head-mounted inertial odometry. Our decomposition achieves tilt invariance by separating gravity-aligned and horizontal components using only the tilt component of device orientation. We evaluate on four head-mounted datasets and find that HeadIO improves RTE-5s by 8.1% (0.260 m $\to$ 0.239 m) over world-frame methods with learned equivariant layers on Nymeria, the largest benchmark dataset, at nearly 3$\times$ lower FLOPs and 8$\times$ fewer parameters. HeadIO achieves state-of-the-art performance on our primary metric, RTE-5s, on all Aria-glasses datasets, which natively provide body-frame semantics for our decomposition method. We further evaluate the IMU-only deployment scenario on Nymeria, where HeadIO remains state-of-the-art. We show that our method relies mainly on tilt accuracy, which can be obtained from the gravity signal; for yaw, a single-coefficient gyroscope correction removes 11.6% of the yaw error and improves RTE-5s by 2.5%, and sparse external yaw corrections (for example, one every 30 s) can improve RTE-5s by 12.0%. Our decomposition is lightweight, composes with any backbone, and is suitable for real-time deployment on head-mounted devices.
  </details>

- **[HAVANA: A Hierarchical Video-Action Model for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24122)**  `arXiv:2610.24122`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Dongzhihan Wang, Yi Du, Chen Wang, Xiuxian Li, Liang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) requires long-horizon instruction-consistent planning and short-horizon path planning grounded in the currently observed geometry. Anticipating how future observations evolve along the instructed route provides predictive guidance beyond the current field of view, motivating video-action models (VAMs) that learn representations of future visual states. However, many existing VAMs couple future visual prediction with action generation during inference, despite their distinct temporal requirements: instruction-conditioned route guidance can be reused across multiple control steps, whereas local trajectories must continuously adapt to fresh observations. To bridge this gap, we introduce HAVANA, a dual-system VAM that decouples predictive reasoning from reactive local planning. A slow system produces a compact language-conditioned latent plan from observation history, while a fast system combines the latest plan with the current observation to generate local trajectories. Their asynchronous execution enables rapid adaptation without repeated future prediction, and no future video is generated during inference. Experiments on OmniNavBench demonstrate state-of-the-art navigation performance over prior baselines, while zero-shot real-world deployments further show robust generalization to unseen environments.
  </details>

- **[ActionUNet: Improving Robustness of VLA Models with Efficient Multi-scale Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34982)**  `arXiv:2609.34982`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Di Zhu, Ziheng Yan, Fang Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown great promise for robotic manipulation by mapping multi-modal semantics to physical actions. However, this mapping inherently struggles to align these coarse-grained semantics with fine-grained temporal execution. It leaves VLA models with limited generalization and insufficient robustness in cluttered environments. To overcome this issue, we propose ActionUNet, an efficient multi-scale fine-tuning framework that enhances pre-trained VLA models with minimal computational cost. ActionUNet first constructs a lightweight temporal U-Net within the temporal-aligned action feature space to fuse hierarchical structural priors, effectively bridging the scale gap between semantics and temporal executions. Recognizing that multi-scale modeling can disrupt microscopic temporal continuity and cause mechanical oscillations, ActionUNet then employs a conditional SIREN as a continuous action decoder. Equipped with explicit second-order smoothness constraints, this decoder guarantees temporal continuity and reduces high-frequency motion jitter. By smoothing temporal discontinuities from multi-scale fusion, this continuous formulation reduces mechanical execution failures while preserving the base VLA model's generalization and manipulation robustness. Extensive experiments on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO-Plus benchmarks, together with real-world hard evaluations, demonstrate that ActionUNet significantly improves {\pi}0.5 success rates by absolute 9.8%, 6.1%, and 11.4%, respectively, while also generalizing to the regression-based OpenVLA-OFT backbone, highlighting its effectiveness and efficiency as a fine-tuning strategy. Code and implementation details are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Unbundling Multimodal Vision Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17334)**  `arXiv:2611.17334`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ellis Brown, Chris Hoang, Shengyi Qian, Shang-Wen Li, Mike Lewis, Marjan Ghazvininejad, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How multimodal language models should encode visual input remains up for debate. Most models use a pretrained vision encoder, while some recent recipes train the encoder from scratch with the language model or remove it entirely. Vision encoders bundle together three design choices: a visual prior from upstream pretraining, dedicated vision capacity, and pre-fusion placement of that capacity (i.e., before the multimodal backbone). Because published models also differ in data, architecture, and compute, comparing them cannot isolate the effect of each choice. We disentangle these choices via a controlled framework where we pretrain all models from scratch on common data and compare them on visual question answering and language benchmarks at matched lifetime compute. We find that the visual prior is not necessary; an encoder trained jointly from scratch is on par with a pretrained one, without a separate training stage. What matters instead is dedicated vision capacity: an encoder-free model trails on visual understanding even when its backbone is enlarged by the encoder's parameter count. Yet that capacity need not precede multimodal fusion, as vision-only modules added in-fusion to early backbone layers perform comparably to a pre-fusion encoder and can lower time to first token. These results also hold across backbone sizes, when encoder pretraining is treated as free, and when starting from a pretrained language model. Overall, our findings favor dedicated vision capacity trained jointly with the backbone and placed early within it.
  </details>

- **[FLEX-LBP: Learning Local Binary Patterns with Exact Binary Inference for Texture Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17302)**  `arXiv:2611.17302`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohamed Alimoussa, Amal El Kaid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local Binary Patterns (LBP) remain among the most widely used texture descriptors, from industrial inspection to medical imaging. Yet their radius, sampling angles, color transform and thresholds are fixed by hand for each dataset. We present FLEX-LBP, which learns these parameters by backpropagation while every forward pass computes exact binary codes and hard histogram counts. A straight-through estimator acts only in the backward pass, so the deployed descriptor is an interpretable hard-binary LBP with 90 learnable parameters and unchanged inference cost. In a controlled ablation on six color texture benchmarks, the component worth learning is dataset-dependent. One configuration, applied unchanged everywhere, learns the geometry and thresholds with the color transform frozen. It improves the nearest-neighbor accuracy of the identical fixed descriptor by 3.55 points on average over five seeds, significantly on five of six benchmarks and never significantly worse. With a linear discriminant readout, it gains 0.9 points; the fully learned descriptor gains 2.7 and has the highest mean. FLEX-LBP is more accurate than five classical LBP-family descriptors, PCANet and a neural LBP on five of six benchmarks. Its mean nearest-neighbor accuracy exceeds that of frozen ImageNet ResNet-50 features (23.5 million parameters). On the Kather colorectal cancer histology benchmark, the fully learned descriptor reaches 93.8%, significantly above the best classical descriptor (Completed LBP, 92.8%). The learned radii adapt in directions that recur across seeds. Noise drowns single-pixel comparisons and illuminant change flips inter-channel ones; median sampling or gray-world normalization restores 96-97%. The code is available as a drop-in PyTorch layer atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Democratising Low-Data Medical Image Classification: CPU-Trainable ZACH-ViT and Its Geometric Variants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16608)**  `arXiv:2611.16608`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Athanasios Angelakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical image classification in resource-constrained settings must contend with limited annotations and computational capacity. We evaluate four ZACH-ViT variants with linear, Euclidean prototype, Poincare and spherical heads alongside two compact multiple-instance-learning baselines. All models are trained from scratch with fewer than 260,000 parameters across seven MedMNIST+ datasets and three external benchmarks (PCam, ISIC2017 and Shenzhen tuberculosis radiographs). The low-data protocol uses 50 training images per class and five seeds, with validation-selected checkpoints at 23 and 47 epochs; a full-data reference uses one seed. Compact-TransMIL achieves the highest ten-dataset mean test Macro-F1 at 23 epochs (53.34%) and with full data (73.93%). At 47 epochs, ZACH-Linear narrowly leads (57.76% versus 57.54% for Compact-TransMIL). Extending training improves Macro-F1 in 239 of 300 paired comparisons. On ISIC2017, hZACH-Spherical has the highest 47-epoch low-data Macro-F1 (44.09% with SD 4.09%), whereas ZACH-EuclideanProto leads with full data (51.27%). We additionally conduct 40 CPU-only training runs on ISIC2017 and Shenzhen using four CPU threads; model-only single-image inference requires approximately 4-9 ms, while image decoding and preprocessing substantially increase end-to-end latency. The results demonstrate the feasibility of CPU-only training on the tested platform and the task- and regime-dependence of compact medical imaging under strict resource constraints.
  </details>

- **[Verifier Choice is a Benchmark Design Variable: Auditing Structural Counting Evaluation in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15433)**  `arXiv:2611.15433`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shurun Li, Michael Hua Wang, Haibo Zeng, Long Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic evaluation is now central to text-to-image benchmarking, but the verifier that converts generated images into scores is often treated as an implementation detail. We argue that this is unsafe: an automatic benchmark score is a property of the image generator, prompt distribution, verifier, verifier-use protocol, and scoring rule, not of the generator alone. We study this issue in structural counting, a controlled setting where human-visible counts can be audited. We construct a verifier-centric audit suite over seven counting objects: cell phones, chairs, and traffic lights for object counting, and clover leaflets, dresser drawer fronts, power-strip sockets, and turbine blades for substructure counting. Across 6,240 generated images from Qwen-Image-2512 and FLUX.2-dev, we compare open MLLM verifiers under count-only, reject-aware, zero-shot and few-shot protocols, plus TIFA-like target queries, a detector baseline, and frontier closed-model checks on two hard objects. On a balanced 2,280-image evaluation split, aggregate score matching can coexist with image-level disagreement, and verifier-use protocols and generator distributions change the measured reliability. Every verifier-protocol configuration also shrinks the human score difference between the two generators, by 7.5-25.0 pp, and with a third generator, Z-Image, all four tested protocols reverse the human ordering of Z-Image and FLUX.2-dev on our fixed prompt suite. GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7 checks do not remove the failure modes: GPT-5.5 still has only 59.92% clover valid-count fidelity, while Claude Opus 4.7 has lower same-slice agreement ($\kappa=0.432$). We propose reporting automatic benchmark scores with a factorized verifier reliability profile: aggregate score gap, image-level agreement, generator-comparison distortion, valid-image count fidelity, rejection behavior, output compliance, and directional bias.
  </details>

- **[Arbor: When Labels Are Not Coarsened at Random](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15311)**  `arXiv:2611.15311`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Faisal Mohammad, Mohamed Younis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Merging datasets labelled at different levels of a taxonomy leaves some images with only a coarse label, and some classes with no fine-grained label at all. Standard training scores a coarse label by the total probability of the classes under it. Coarse labels name superclasses, and the standard objective is exact only when every class within a superclass is equally likely to receive a coarse label. We call a class novel if it is never labelled finely, and known otherwise. A novel class violates the assumption, and the classifier assigns its images to a known sibling. We propose Arbor, which models how labels become coarse. In each superclass with a novel class, Arbor adds a virtual novel class, and a logistic gate divides the superclass's probability between it and the known classes. The gate is trained with the likelihood of the observed labels under this model, the coarsening likelihood. We prove that at its population optimum the gate equals the Elkan-Noto correction of positive-unlabelled learning, and show that training the correction into the classifier improves on applying it after training. Across Cars, CUB and Aircraft with 90% coarse labels, Arbor correctly assigns 76-85% of novel-class images to their superclass, more than hierarchical classifiers, hierarchical novelty detection and the Elkan-Noto correction. Its top-1 accuracy over the leaf classes on known-class images is within 1.6 points of the best baseline, although its final predictions return the superclass more often. On the more challenging iNaturalist-2019 benchmark, Arbor improves novel-class accuracy from 46% (uniform target, the strongest baseline) to 68%.
  </details>

- **[Sophon: A Procedural Diagnostic for Spatial Reasoning in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14838)**  `arXiv:2611.14838`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _J. Zach Gazak, Ryan Swindle, Justin Fletcher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-weight Vision-Language Models (VLMs) score 85-94% on spatial benchmarks (BLINK Spatial, What's Up CLEVR) yet collapse to chance on simple geometric questions when object, scene, and texture priors are stripped away. We introduce and open-source Sophon, a procedurally generated diagnostic of paired colored point-cloud stimuli across three coordinate systems (1D linear, 2D Cartesian, 2D polar) and four question types (binary, categorical, comparative, quantitative). Across four open architectures (LLaVA-NeXT, LLaVA-OneVision, Qwen2.5-VL, Idefics3), accuracy clusters at 47-52% while frontier models (Opus 4.6, Gemini 2.5 Pro, GPT-5.4) clear 77-83% and humans reach 84-94%. We use linear probes and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to trace the open-weight failures. Probes of the vision encoder (ViT) and language decoder (LLM) recover Sophon spatial information at 83-85% and 80-86% accuracy, respectively, and the language tower's reasoning circuit answers text-only controls (point clouds as sets of coordinates) at +40 pp over visual stimuli. Language tower per-layer attention attribution localizes visual integration to mid-stack layers. At readout, models elevate the correct answer-set tokens but select among them randomly. We localize the mechanism by tuning individual and grouped components across every architecture's stack, including the vision tower (ViT workspace and reader), the projector, and the language tower (LLM workspace and reader). These experiments demonstrate that this mid-stack workspace holds circuitry capable of organizing the spatial signal so that the frozen reader can decode it. LoRA fine-tuning adjusts that circuitry such that 7B-class VLMs answer Sophon spatial reasoning questions at frontier aggregate accuracy. This work suggests that these models do not have a capability gap for spatial reasoning but suffer from a routing bottleneck that targeted fine-tuning repairs.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Spatio-Temporal Priors: A Generalizable Approach for Dense Correspondence Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12421)**  `arXiv:2610.12421`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Luping Liu, Bingyi Kang, Yifan Wang, Dong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense correspondence matching has historically been bounded by simplifying spatio-temporal priors, such as smooth motion and rigid geometry. While effective for classical tasks, these assumptions break down in image editing and reference-guided generation (IEG), where transformations can preserve visual identity while breaking physical continuity. To establish identity-preserving correspondence across such transformations, we introduce FreeMatching, a generalizable framework combining generative and semantic foundation representations with heterogeneous supervision from classical datasets, tracked videos, and synthetic scenes. Teacher-guided iterative refinement further improves correspondence in IEG without dense correspondence annotations. Experimentally, a single FreeMatching model substantially improves correspondence quality on challenging IEG image pairs while retaining competitive performance on classical benchmarks. Furthermore, we demonstrate its utility as a quantitative metric for evaluating identity preservation, with scores that correlate with human judgment. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MoTE: Mixture of Task Experts for Multi-Task Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24763)**  `arXiv:2608.24763`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Muhammad Asad Ali, Umar Khan, Nadia Robertini, Didier Stricker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Procedural video-language models must solve heterogeneous tasks from the same visual evidence, including action recognition, forecasting, and procedure prediction. Dense transformer decoders share the same feed-forward networks across tasks, which can entangle task behavior and make controlled capability expansion difficult. Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoders provide conditional computation, but token-level learned routing is not naturally aligned with task-level procedural objectives. We propose MoTE (Mixture of Task Experts), a decoder architecture that converts large language model feed-forward networks into task-specific experts while keeping the multimodal backbone shared. Each example follows one sample-level task route, so active task-expert computation remains independent of the number of stored task experts. We instantiate this design as VideoLLM-MoTE and evaluate it on five COIN benchmarks using explicit task routes. The five-expert model activates ~2B LLM parameters per sample and achieves higher average top-1 accuracy than recent VideoLLM baselines. Under the same expert topology, it improves over dense all-expert activation and learned sparse-routing controls. These results show that task-structured routing provides an interpretable and compute-efficient decoder alternative for multi-task video-language learning.
  </details>

- **[Addressable Memory for Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07408)**  `arXiv:2608.07408`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Xindi Wu, Sven Elflein, James Lucas, Olga Russakovsky, Laura Leal-Taixé, Despoina Paschalidou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study visual persistence in interactive video world models. These models rely on a Key-Value (KV) cache as a growing visual memory to carry forward previously generated frames. However, we find that models can no longer reliably address stored content once rollouts extend beyond the training horizon, because temporal Rotary Positional Embedding (RoPE) offsets can exceed the training range and the model struggles to retrieve the relevant visual information through attention. Moreover, naively compressing the cache in the RoPE-rotated space degrades memory by averaging keys with incompatible positional phases. To address this, we propose WorldTrace, a training-free memory framework for long-horizon visual persistence. WorldTrace keeps compressed memory addressable by assigning each summary slot a distinct, in-distribution virtual position. Within this cache, WorldTrace uses the model's own attention to decide which observations to retain verbatim and which redundant ones to merge into compressed summaries, all within one bounded addressable memory bank. We evaluate this ability with LoopBench, a benchmark that tests whether a compressed cache can reconstruct a previously visited scene after a long detour. On LoopBench, WorldTrace improves average DINOv2 recall similarity by 32.6% over a sliding window at the same cache budget, extending visually persistent generation without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Streaming Interventions: Can Video Large Language Models Correct Mistakes as They Occur?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09547)**  `arXiv:2606.09547`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Apratim Bhattacharyya, Shweta Mahajan, Sanjay Haresh, Rajeev Yasarla, Reza Pourreza, Litian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning everyday skills, like cooking a dish, relies increasingly on instructional media such as online videos. This opens the door to the use of video (and multimodal) large language models (LLMs) as task guidance assistants. A crucial capability for the real-world success of a prospective task guidance assistant is it's ability to intervene proactively as soon as a mistake is apparent in order to guide the user. To evaluate this crucial capability, we introduce Ego-MC-Bench (Mistake Corrections), a benchmark for evaluating reactive, step-by-step task guidance in realistic cooking scenarios. Extensive experiments show that Ego-MC-Bench is highly challenging for state-of-the-art video LLMs. We argue that a key reason is the limited availability of training data for fine-tuning models on this task. Although there exists a wide range of cooking video datasets, existing datasets lack examples of mistakes along with appropriately timed interventions. To help address this data limitation, we also introduce Ego-CoMist, a counterfactual synthetic dataset created by transforming non -interactive cooking videos into supervised training examples showing proactive interventions. We show that fine-tuning on Ego-CoMist yields performance gains especially for smaller and more efficient video LLMs that are well suited for delivering assistance on edge devices.
  </details>

- **[Visual Instruction Tuning Aligns Modalities through Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03871)**  `arXiv:2606.03871`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Luis Palacios, Lorenzo Basile, Diego Doimo, Alberto Cazzaniga_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual instruction tuning effectively adapts a pre-trained Large Language Model (LLM) to process image information alongside text. Yet it remains unclear how visual features are embedded within the layer-wise hierarchy of abstractions in the LLM backbone. Across a diverse set of vision-language architectures, we show that instruction tuning primarily serves as a bridge, embedding visual features directly into the intermediate semantic layers of the LLM, bypassing the early layers devoted to unimodal processing. With probing analyses and causal interventions, we show that these intermediate layers constitute the semantic core of vision-language processing and play a critical role in performance across a broad set of multimodal benchmarks. In addition, by comparing the geometry of semantically equivalent visual and textual representations, we find that fine-tuning extends and strengthens the existing abstraction phase, aligning visual features with pre-existing textual ones. Finally, we confirm the functional role of this localized alignment by restricting fine-tuning to intermediate layers alone: this strategy preserves the performance of full fine-tuning on vision-centric benchmarks while reducing training time. Our results suggest that multimodal integration is a localized phenomenon driven by the repurposing of the internal abstraction engine of the LLM.
  </details>

- **[Contrastive Bootstrap Representation Learning for Label-Efficient Agricultural Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15610)**  `arXiv:2611.15610`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hazem Ahmed, Ali Hamdi, Ammar Mohammed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert annotation remains a major bottleneck in agricultural image classification, where collecting images is often easier than obtaining reliable labels from domain specialists. Self-supervised learning can reduce this dependence by pretraining transferable representations on unlabelled imagery. Contrastive and bootstrap methods provide two established but distinct training signals, motivating an empirical study of a formulation that applies both signals within one representation path under severe downstream label scarcity. We present Contrastive Bootstrap Representation Learning (CBRL), which jointly optimises SimCLR-style NT-Xent contrastive learning and bidirectional BYOL-style exponential-moving-average target prediction within a shared online projection space. Using a ViT-L/16 backbone and 10% of the downstream labels, we report single-seed point estimates on four primary agricultural test benchmarks plus an exploratory 33-image New Plant Diseases evaluation. CBRL records the highest macro-F1 point estimate on the four primary benchmarks. On DeepWeeds, its 79.77% test macro-F1 is 32.29 points above the random-initialisation, label-only baseline and 15.78 points above the next-highest comparison point estimate; on PlantVillage, its 98.14% macro-F1 is 10.43 and 0.43 points higher, respectively. On PlantDoc, however, all methods fail to reach practically useful performance. These results identify the evaluated joint formulation as a promising candidate for label-limited transfer, but they do not establish objective synergy, statistical superiority, or robustness across stochastic runs.
  </details>

- **[TraceKV: Policy-Preserving Low-Rank KV Cache Compression for Reasoning Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15569)**  `arXiv:2611.15569`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ke Li, Zishan Shao, Yiran Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning vision-language models (VLMs) impose substantial KV cache demands from both large multimodal contexts and growing reasoning traces. Low-rank KV compression has emerged as a promising paradigm that stores low-dimensional latents of KV states and can be combined with quantization. However, existing low-rank methods often induce growing predictive policy drift from the dense model when compressing reasoning KV states, resulting in reasoning failures. We identify two key causes of this degradation: calibration-state mismatch between static contexts and on-policy reasoning states, and unequal error sensitivity across KV states. To address these challenges, we propose TraceKV, a training-free low-rank KV compression framework that unifies on-policy calibration, sensitivity-aware SVD, and global rank allocation under a single policy-preserving principle, substantially reducing policy-KL divergence during reasoning. Experiments across multiple VLMs and visual reasoning benchmarks demonstrate superior accuracy--memory trade-offs, achieving near-lossless reasoning accuracy with 5.64$\times$ KV cache compression when combined with quantization. Optimized low-rank attention kernels further deliver up to 1.83$\times$ end-to-end decoding speedup. Code will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GTAGym: AAA Game Tasks Environment for Evaluation and Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13028)**  `arXiv:2611.13028`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiajun Chen, Shicheng Fang, Jiahao Lu, Yuxin Wang, Xipeng Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AAA games offer a rich testbed for evaluating agent perception, reasoning, and control. However, evaluating whether agents can complete tasks in AAA games is challenging and often human-annotated because task outcomes are often difficult to verify automatically, let alone applying it for Reinforcement Learning (RL). We introduce GTAGym, an environment and framework for evaluation and RL on GTA V tasks. Its evaluation benchmark, GTAVBench, derived from GTA V main-story missions, contains 103 online tasks reconstructed across nine task families, alongside 47 image-based shooting and sniping tasks using GTA V imagery. A web server provides access to the online environment and evaluation interface, supporting parallel testing through screenshots and keyboard-mouse actions with automatic engine-state outcome checks. We evaluate six frontier LLMs on GTAVBench with textual task instructions and on its GTAVBench-AGI branch, where instructions appear only in the first screenshot and a harness automatically compresses interaction history. The best configuration achieves 70.9% overall accuracy in GTAVBench-AGI, yet aerial navigation, evasion and interception remain difficult. GTAGym also provides a scalable generator that samples task parameters within user-specified ranges and option sets under fixed templates, creating batches of 3D and image-based shooting environments for RL with automatic reward and termination signals. LoRA RL of Qwen3.8-27B on generated on-foot navigation tasks improves the in-domain performance and transfers to out-of-domain task categories, and native GTA V tasks from 15% to 23%.
  </details>

- **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305)**  `arXiv:2611.12305`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zixin Su, Yangguang Lin, Zegang Peng, Yuntong Sun, Xuanyi Wang, Jiahao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. Evaluating the differences between MLLMs and human-level visual perception is challenging due to end-to-end evaluation which combines various skills, or because we are uncertain if individual items are valid. Specifically, observed errors may reflect visual perception, language priors, background knowledge, question ambiguity, and underspecified answers. Our guideline for HumanSightBench is that we construct items that are human-easy by construction and model-hard by evaluation. HumanSightBench contains 2,876 visual questions with short answers across 7 top-level perceptual categories and 21 subcategories. We only include items that pass five inclusion gates: question clarity, answer uniqueness, visual grounding, human solvability, and text-only shortcut filtering. According to the protocol, errors are defined as incorrect answers on human-validated visual tasks with the evaluated interface. The inclusion gates are intended to reduce confounds from ambiguity and external knowledge. The best pass@3 score is 65.6% across eight evaluated MLLMs. No evaluated model exceeds 27.5% on Counting. These results identify capability gaps that are hidden by overall scores and can help future training experiments and held-out evaluation.
  </details>

- **[MALNISI-Infused Graph Construction and Weighted Contrastive Loss based GAT for Unsupervised Representation Learning in HSI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17199)**  `arXiv:2611.17199`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Soumi Sarkar, Subhadip Boral, Sarbani Palit, Ashish Ghosh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperspectral images (HSI) possess discriminating spectral characteristics with respect to land utilization due to high-dimensional information. Recently, unsupervised representation learning using graph neural networks has unveiled these utilization-specific complex spectral relations. This article has three main contributions. First, it proposes an index, the Mean Absolute Local Neighborhood Inverse Spectral Index (MALNISI), which measures the similarity between two pixels based on their neighborhood information. The index is used to construct a weighted adjacency matrix, giving the HSI a graph structure. Second, it proposes an upgraded Graph Attention Network that learns compact latent representations of hyperspectral data from the constructed graph. In this network, an enhanced multi-head attention mechanism incorporates the MALNISI weights as an auxiliary feature to emphasize learned attention between neighboring pixels. To alleviate over-smoothing and preserve fine-grained spectral details, the architecture integrates residual connections, skip projections, and feature normalization, which together enhance representation capability, stabilize learning, and accelerate convergence. Third, it proposes a weighted contrastive loss function that incorporates neighborhood-aware MALNISI between connected pixels, encouraging strongly connected pixels in the high-dimensional space to remain close in the latent space, and weakly connected pixels to stay apart. The resulting discriminative low-dimensional embeddings are then clustered using spectral clustering. Experimental analysis on six benchmark HSI datasets demonstrates the efficiency and necessity of the three aforementioned contributions, and the comparative study highlights superior performance over fourteen state-of-the-art approaches.
  </details>

- **[AURA 2.0: From User-Driven Responses to Model-Initiated Conversations in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17048)**  `arXiv:2611.17048`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuhan Li, Huankang Guan, Xintong Guo, Yang Bo, Yuyang He, Dunyuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most AI assistants respond only when prompted, remaining silent even when events in the video stream offer meaningful opportunities for conversation. To move beyond purely user-driven interaction, we present AURA 2.0, a streaming video large language model that introduces three core upgrades to AURA. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) enables the model to invoke external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporate returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities. We open-source the model weights and inference code.
  </details>

- **[ElasticDino: Dense Semantic Features Using Elastic Deformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16984)**  `arXiv:2611.16984`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ulysse Mizrahi, Sean Zaretzky, Dov Danon, Amit H. Bermano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision transformers (ViTs) serve as the backbone for countless vision tasks, including dense prediction tasks, such as segmentation and depth estimation, even though the quadratic complexity of self-attention severely limits feature resolution and induces spatial artifacts. In this work, we introduce ElasticDino, a lightweight adapter which produces high-resolution feature maps, adhering to the fine details of the input image. As a key insight, we observe the consistency of artifacts across channels, and simultaneously resolve them to match the input image structure. Using a slim architecture based on spatial deformations, ElasticDino produces pixel-aligned, high-resolution feature maps efficiently. Leveraging deformations allows strictly preserving sharp semantics, and thereby avoiding the smoothness biases inherent to alternative approaches. Due to the absence of ground truth, we employ pixel-level semantic segmentation and depth estimation with linear heads to predict a set of downstream dense tasks, guiding the deformation process without leaking task-specific semantics into the model. We demonstrate superior accuracy over current methods on a range of popular dense task benchmarks, with better computational performance.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Video LLMs' Understanding of Corner Cases in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16767)**  `arXiv:2611.16767`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriel Manalu, Minh Son Hoang, Hyeontaek Hwang, Jaesung Lee, Hyojin Choi, Tran Tuan Phong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language explanations promised by modern Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) offer autonomous driving systems a path toward detailed scene description and interpretable decision making on the road. While recent benchmarks have begun assessing these models on driving scenes, they mainly target routine traffic conditions and general comprehension tasks based on existing driving datasets and therefore fail to evaluate model performance on the rarest and most safety-critical driving events, also known as "corner cases". To provide a comprehensive evaluation of Video LLMs on precisely these scenarios, we introduce DashBench, a benchmark testing 25 Video LLMs on 1,709 carefully curated driving videos spanning not only collision-related corner cases but also rare non-collision ones. Through extensive prompts and open-ended question answering, we expose a critical capability gap: all evaluated models perform substantially below the threshold required for safety-critical driving applications, frequently misjudging or missing dangerous events on the road ahead and hallucinating non-existent agents or actions in their explanations. We believe DashBench, combining an extensive dataset with a rigorous evaluation toolkit and a detailed diagnosis of current model limitations, drives progress toward safer, more explainable autonomous driving systems. The code and data will be shared at:this http URL
  </details>

- **[ORCA:Orthogonal Residual Consensus Alignment for Multi-View Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16749)**  `arXiv:2611.16749`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaojian Ding, Xian Li, Xiaoying Zhu, Lin Zhao, Kaixiang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep multi-view clustering often suffers from redundant information accumulation during fusion and indiscriminate alignment across representation levels. We propose Orthogonal Residual Consensus Alignment (ORCA) to address these challenges. Its semantic incremental recurrent fusion module extracts cross-view orthogonal residuals as non-redundant increments and adaptively weights recurrent states through divergence-guided gating. Its consensus-calibrated hierarchical contrastive alignment module uses the global consensus to weight contrastive alignment of view-level, pairwise, and recurrent representations. Together, these modules balance view-specific structure with cross-view consistency. Experiments on five benchmarks against fifteen competing methods demonstrate ORCA's effectiveness, including a 5.25 percent accuracy improvement over the strongest baseline on 100leaves. Ablation studies validate both modules. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhysLoop: Harnessing LLMs' Physics Awareness for Self-Improving Video Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16748)**  `arXiv:2611.16748`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Chen, Xiaojie Li, Hao Chen, Juncheng Ma, Kewei Zhang, Zhongjing Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models produce visually realistic videos but still struggle with physical dynamics and causal consistency. Learning from generated videos requires fine-grained physical supervision, yet reliable quality judgments, correction pairs, and controlled variations are difficult to obtain. Large language models (LLMs) possess physical knowledge, but struggle to reliably guide video generation models in producing their training videos. We present PhysLoop, a unified harness designed to support physically grounded self-improvement in video generation models. The harness guides and constrains LLMs throughout video generation, selection, and correction, distilling their physical knowledge into physically consistent videos. The framework supports controllable training-data construction and inference-time video repair. By identifying and correcting failures, PhysLoop marks a key milestone toward recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video generation models with limited human intervention. We release 100K videos spanning diverse, realistic indoor and outdoor scenes, along with generation tools and two evaluation datasets: PhysLoop-Gen and PhysLoop-Repair. We evaluate controllable generation and localized repair on these datasets, respectively, and assess physical behavior on the external Physics-IQ Verified benchmark.
  </details>

- **[From Foundation VLM to Versatile Driving VLM: A Triplet Incremental Training Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16727)**  `arXiv:2611.16727`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shengtao Wu, Zichao Dong, Weixuan Qiao, Xiaolu Liu, Xin Zhan, Junbo Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting general Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to Autonomous Driving (AD) is thought to require heavy 3D spatial priors or intrusive architectural overhauls to compensate for a lack of spatial grounding. In this work, we present a counterintuitive pilot study: off-the-shelf general VLMs already exhibit remarkable adaptability across diverse driving tasks, while precise metric depth remains the primary missing link, a bottleneck readily addressable via onboard sensors and depth foundation models. To this end, we introduce the Triplet Incremental Training Framework, a non-intrusive paradigm that seamlessly instills 3D geometric awareness into a general VLM backbone while fully preserving its open-world capabilities. Driven by automatically constructed (image, depth, QA pair) triplets, our framework operates via a unified tri-stream training pipeline: an unfrozen primary VLM stream to adapt to driving tasks, an auxiliary depth stream that guides the visual backbone to implicitly imagine 3D scene geometry via explicit geometric constraints, and a frozen twin VLM stream employing logit-level KL distillation to prevent semantic drift. Crucially, the auxiliary depth branch is discarded during deployment, ensuring the enhanced geometric awareness comes with zero inference overhead. We evaluate our framework across four major benchmarks targeting four diverse capabilities spanning 3D spatial perception, driving-oriented language reasoning, end-to-end planning, and general multimodal understanding. Our approach outperforms state-of-the-art driving VLMs, rivals domain-specific expert models in 3D perception, and successfully retains its original general reasoning capabilities. This demonstrates that Triplet Incremental Training offers a unified, deployment-ready paradigm for transforming general foundation models into robust driving intelligences.
  </details>

- **[DesignTaste: Benchmarking AI-Generator Visual Design Against Award-Wining Works](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16442)**  `arXiv:2611.16442`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhou Chen, JiaXiang Li, DanYing Pan, Ye Li, TianRui Yang, EnBao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image benchmarks commonly assess text-image alignment or overall preference. Whether models can produce good designs when a brief leaves the appearance open remains less well understood. We introduce DesignTaste, a visual design benchmark that compares generated images with award-winning works. From these works and their publicly available context, we derive 403 design briefs: 100 poster tasks from 100 Beste Plakate 2025 and 303 tasks spanning 20 categories from ADC 2026. Six leading commercial text-to-image models each generate an image from the same brief. A single model judge scores each output and the corresponding award-winning work independently on five evaluation dimensions. On each dataset, only ArtArk achieves a higher mean overall score than the award-winning works. All six models score lower in Creative Originality on both datasets. These results show that producing finished, visually appealing images does not imply that a model can develop good design solutions.
  </details>
