  _Andrei Dumitriu, Aakash Ralhan, Florin Miron, Florin Tatui, Radu Tudor Ionescu, Radu Timofte_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rip currents are a serious and often under-addressed threat to beach safety and the leading cause of coastal drownings worldwide. They are difficult to detect due to their amorphous structure, similarity to the background, and large variability in viewpoints and environments. Despite their dangers and growing interest in automated detection, existing research remains fragmented across datasets, metrics, models, and approaches, leading to an incomplete understanding of model performance. We introduce RipBench, a unified benchmark that enables controlled evaluation of rip current detection spanning multiple tasks, including classification, axis-aligned and oriented object detection, and instance, semantic, and panoptic segmentation, on the same data with standardized splits. This allows direct comparison of model performance on all levels of visual abstraction. Across $304$ videos ($303{,}491$ frames, $51{,}011$ of them annotated for all six tasks) collected from diverse coastlines, our results expose a clear performance gap between coarse recognition and precise spatial understanding: frame-level classification reaches $F_2 = 0.93$, while the best detector and the best instance segmenter reach $0.72$ and $0.66$, with model rankings that change from task to task. Video-level confidence intervals, a blind inter-annotator study of the spatial labels, stratified analyses by viewpoint and visual signature, and video views with temporal baselines (single-seed, short-schedule reference runs) accompany the benchmark. All tasks are supported with carefully curated annotations and evaluated using standard metrics, with a focus on the $F_2$ score to emphasize recall in this safety-critical setting. RipBench, along with multiple baselines per task, is publicly available atthis https URLto support progress in real-world, multi-task vision for beach safety.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Consistency of Vision Tokens: A Vision-Centric Perspective on Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15448)**  `arXiv:2611.15448`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nikola Đukić, Tim Lebailly, Tinne Tuytelaars_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In computer vision, vision-language and self-supervised encoders are two unique paradigms characterized by global language alignment and high-quality vision-only patch representations, respectively. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often use the former because of language alignment, despite the recent rise in vision-centric tasks resembling traditional dense vision tasks that would likely benefit from the high-quality patch representations, raising the question whether self-supervised models could aid MLLMs in vision-centric tasks. To investigate this, we define semantic (in)consistency of patch tokens from language-aligned encoders and identify two sets of tokens, semantically consistent (SCTs) and semantically inconsistent (SITs). Through systematic probing via the removal of SCTs or SITs from the input of modern MLLMs, we show higher reliance on SCTs than SITs, as removal of the former induces larger performance drops. This demonstrates that MLLMs benefit from semantic consistency of vision tokens. With this in mind, we devise a simple yet effective extension of the MLLM training objective that combines traditional language modeling with semantic guidance via minimization of semantic inconsistency. This strategy, named Semantically-Guided Training (SGT), combines the signals of both loss terms to push the vision encoder towards providing semantically consistent tokens, while preserving language alignment. Extensive evaluation shows that SGT outperforms all baselines and MLLMs with specialized vision encoders on vision-centric benchmarks while keeping strong scores on text-centric benchmarks, thus setting state of the art on average across all tasks. Our results highlight the importance of combining language alignment and semantic consistency through the lens of MLLMs rather than independently from them and pave the way towards the design of better MLLM vision encoders.
  </details>

- **[SteinPnP: Non-Parametric Uncertainty Quantification in the Perspective-N-Point Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15437)**  `arXiv:2611.15437`  `cs.CV`  
  _Baptiste Lardinoit, Jongseok Lee, Andres Yubero, Seong Hun Lee, Javier Civera, Rudolph Triebel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose SteinPnP, a probabilistic Perspective- N-Point (PnP) solver that models the full 6-DoF camera pose posterior using a non-parametric set of pose particles. By lever- aging Stein Variational Inference (SVI), SteinPnP combines the expressivity of particle-based representations with the efficiency of optimization-based solvers. However, a naive application of SVI struggles in the PnP problem, often missing alternative pose hypotheses or assigning incorrect probability weights to them. To address these limitations, we propose practical remedies that discover new potential poses, identify distinct hypothesis clusters, and balance their relative probability weights to capture the true underlying distribution. Our experiments across synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that SteinPnP accurately captures complex multimodal uncertainties while maintaining competitive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CaseScope: A Multi-Field Benchmark for Case-Level Pathology Diagnosis with Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15325)**  `arXiv:2611.15325`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenchuan Zhang, Chengyang Zhang, Fengling Li, Ting Yin, Chen Shu, Jie Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathologists rarely diagnose from a single image: they scan a section at low power for architecture, zoom into selected fields for cytology, and integrate the evidence across views and scales. Pathology benchmarks for multimodal large language models (MLLMs), however, mostly ask about one image or require whole-slide pipelines, and the few that show several fields draw nested regions from cancer slides of one public cohort. We introduce CaseScope, a benchmark of diagnosis from several curated fields of one lesion: CaseScope-Teaching, 436 cases from a residency-training image library, reconstructed, de-identified, harmonised against a WHO-based ontology and re-examined by a pathologist wherever an automated pre-review raised a doubt, and CaseScope-Atlas, 800 cases from 88 published atlases (387 of them figure series of one disease), labelled from the captions by two LLMs that must agree. They cover 843 entities across 18 organ systems and pose 4,679 items in six tasks, from open-ended and ten-way diagnosis with ontology-guided hard negatives under circular evaluation to the verification and location of morphological findings. Across twenty-eight open and four proprietary MLLMs, diagnoses are unstable -- the best open and proprietary models are correct under all ten option rotations on 17.9% and 61.7% of the teaching cases -- and wrong answers favour the classic look-alike; open models make little use of the additional fields on teaching cases, where the one-line specimen site matters more, although on atlas cases with three or more fields the fields count about as much as the site; and most open models verify findings barely above chance (median 62.0% vs. 50%, best 81.5%), gaining a median +3.4 points from images; proprietary models reach 83.2-93.2%. Rankings broadly replicate between the sources. The evaluation code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AcrossPlace: Do Vision--Language Models Recognize Places Beyond Their Source Views? A Comprehensive Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15314)**  `arXiv:2611.15314`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenyu Li, Tianyi Shang, Shuhan Dong, Ruotong Xu, Qingyu Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-guided place recognition requires recovering geographic identity across visual observations beyond the image from which a description originates. However, aggregate retrieval scores often entangle observation transfer, gallery competition, and linguistic evidence, obscuring the underlying recognition capability. We introduce AcrossPlace, a provenance-aware benchmark establishing a systematic evaluation standard for bidirectional language--vision place recognition. Built upon GSV-Cities, AcrossPlace comprises 56,726 places across 23 cities, 156,800 automatically generated, rule-filtered descriptions, and 113,404 target images. Each description retains its source-image provenance, while retrieval operates on disjoint target observations. Text-to-image (T2I) and image-to-text (I2T) retrieval share a unified place-identity relevance criterion supporting multiple valid matches. Two geographic protocols evaluate unseen places within represented cities (P0) and generalization to held-out cities (P1). Paired observation comparisons, gallery-controlled evaluations, fixed-query scaling, and blinded annotation auditing systematically characterize retrieval performance. Across three training seeds, place-supervised SigLIP2 T2I Recall@1 decreases from 19.69% on source images to 12.31% on independent targets under P0. For fixed P1 queries, expanding the gallery from 500 to 8,425 places reduces Recall@1 from 14.67% to 2.87%. A 240-description audit further reveals discrepancies between automated acceptance and factual evidence. These findings establish AcrossPlace as a reproducible evaluation standard for assessing transferable place identity across visual observations and geographic environments.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Explicit: Benchmarking and Detecting Implicit Harm in Short Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15306)**  `arXiv:2611.15306`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenyang Li, Qifan Yu, Saihui Hou, Zhaofeng He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Short videos have become a dominant online medium, where harmful content is increasingly conveyed in implicit and fragmented forms. Prior work largely focuses on explicit categories, such as violence and hate speech, and often fails to detect videos that subtly promote misleading values through narrative context, emotional framing, or cross-modal cues, partly due to the lack of dedicated benchmarks for implicit harm. To bridge this gap, we construct DeepHarm-7K, a large-scale harmful short-video dataset comprising 7,110 samples annotated according to a fine-grained harmful-content taxonomy. The dataset systematically covers implicitly harmful videos across diverse real-world scenarios and is constructed through a rigorous multi-stage quality-control process. Building on this dataset, we propose DeepHarm-VL, a multimodal detection framework that integrates visual, audio, and cross-modal reasoning. The framework employs a two-round reasoning strategy to progressively identify surface-level evidence and infer latent harmful implications. Experimental results show consistent improvements over strong baselines and state-of-the-art methods across multiple open-source video-language models, demonstrating its effectiveness in detecting both explicit and implicit harmful short videos.
  </details>

- **[Track4D: Representing Dense 3D Tracking for Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15287)**  `arXiv:2611.15287`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yushi Lan, Zeren Jiang, Kelvin Li, Xingang Pan, Chuanxia Zheng, Andrea Vedaldi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how to repurpose video diffusion models (VDMs), pretrained on millions of videos, for dense 3D point tracking, where training data is scarce. Doing so is non-trivial, as it requires representing 3D tracks as video-like signals that may lie outside the natural-video distribution on which the VDM was trained. We thus systematically assess the effectiveness of two such video representations. The first, Residual 3D Tracking Video (RTV), encodes per-pixel 3D offsets relative to a query frame and allows recovery of 3D trajectories directly from the generated video. The second, Normalized Coordinate Map Video (NCMV), labels each pixel with a canonical coordinate invariant through time. This is closer to a natural video and easier for the VDM to learn, but requires additional post-processing with an off-the-shelf 2D tracker to recover 3D tracks. RTV tracks more accurately, while NCMV shows that a VDM can generate dense correspondence as a video. We instantiate both representations with LoRA adaptation of a frozen Wan backbone, conditioned on point maps from an off-the-shelf 3D reconstructor as a geometric scaffold. Trained with a single dynamic 3D tracking dataset, Track4D achieves competitive zero-shot tracking on four real and synthetic benchmarks. A regression readout that bypasses the video VAE and uses additional training data brings further improvement, demonstrating the potential of this direction for dense 3D tracking. We hope our analysis will inspire future work that leverages VDM priors for dense 3D tracking and 4D reconstruction. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[A General Concept-based Decomposition for Vision-Language Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15195)**  `arXiv:2611.15195`  `cs.CV`  
  _Simone Alberto Peirone, Ortal Senouf, Francesca Pistilli, Giuseppe Averta, Pascal Frossard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models are designed to capture the compositional structure of the world through multi-modal alignment; however, it remains unclear whether their representations truly align with the way a human would naturally describe the same visual input. Prior works attempt to decompose visual embeddings into their concept-level contributions, but their instance-level optimization is inherently local and does not capture the model's global semantic structure, which is essential for compositional generalization across different inputs, tasks, and domains. We introduce GRACE, a method that learns this concept-based structure at the feature space level, producing semantic and general concept decompositions. To achieve this, we propose a novel training objective that preserves the geometric relations of different samples in the concept space, enabling a better understanding of how concepts compose and relate to each other. We validate GRACE on several image and video benchmarks, showing that its concept decomposition is aligned with human perception and achieves faithful decompositions across different tasks and domains. With minimal overhead, GRACE makes existing Vision-Language Models more interpretable, while preserving most of their original performance.
  </details>

- **[OT-BLS: Optimal-Transport-based Backbone Layer Selection for Frozen DINOv3 in Cross-Domain Few-Shot Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15187)**  `arXiv:2611.15187`  `cs.CV`  
  _Natsuo Okamoto, Kazuki Kobayashi, Yoshio Kato, Shuhei Tarashima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-domain few-shot segmentation (CD-FSS) aims to segment a target class in images from a previously unseen domain using only a few annotated support examples. Training-free CD-FSS methods, which typically leverage vision foundation models such as DINOv3, achieve competitive performance without parameter updates. However, the optimal DINOv3 layer for mask prediction varies across support--query episodes. Although several layer-selection criteria have been proposed, they do not explicitly evaluate whether the predicted query foreground and background labels agree with the corresponding support labels. To address this limitation, we propose a novel layer-selection criterion, Optimal-Transport-Based Backbone Layer Selection (OT-BLS). Our key insight is that an effective layer-selection criterion can be derived from the optimal-transport correspondence between query and support patch-token features. Based on this insight, we first estimate the transport plan using a well-established optimal transport algorithm and then compute a layer-selection score by aggregating the transported mass according to the foreground--background partition defined by each candidate query mask. The resulting score reflects how well the predicted foreground and background of the query correspond to those of the support. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of OT-BLS. It outperforms the conventional last-layer baseline in 12 of 16 dataset/shot settings. Furthermore, OT-BLS improves the average mIoU from 55.72 to 57.62 and achieves a 2.99-point improvement over the best-performing existing layer-selection criterion while running 3.7--14.6$\times$ faster.
  </details>

- **[CineMME: From Dialogue Grounding to Narrative Reasoning in Multimodal Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15161)**  `arXiv:2611.15161`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jin Liu, Dawei Du, Yexiang Liu, Sijie Zhu, Fan Chen, Junxian Duan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Narrative video understanding requires more than recognizing visible events: models must extract evidence from dialogue, bind it to speakers, and use it to infer narrative meaning. Existing video benchmarks evaluate aspects of this problem, including video question answering, audio-visual reasoning, and temporal grounding, but rarely test whether these abilities form a coherent dialogue-to-plot evidence chain within the same clips. We introduce \textbf{CineMME}, a human-verified benchmark designed to diagnose dialogue-grounded plot understanding, supported by approximately $1,600$ expert person-hours of annotation. CineMME contains $975$ curated short narrative video clips and two linked tracks: \textit{CineMME-Grounding}, with $13,554$ timestamped speech segments and $33,083$ speaker face boxes for temporal dialogue localization, transcription, and active-speaker grounding; and \textit{CineMME-Reasoning}, with $1,600$ multiple-choice and $316$ open-ended questions spanning perception, audio, narrative, social, and scene-presentation understanding. Our main evaluation compares $20$ MLLMs on reasoning and $9$ omni-modal models on grounding. The results reveal a pronounced who-what-when decoupling: Gemini-3 Pro localizes dialogue in time ($68.34\%$ tIoU) and transcribes it with a WER of $28.99\%$, yet achieves only $7.74\%$ vIoU for active-speaker grounding while achieving $67.66\%$ MCQ accuracy on questions with extractable answers. Modality ablations further show that vision is not uniformly beneficial: it improves performance on perception-heavy questions but can hurt dialogue-centric reasoning. CineMME provides a compact diagnostic testbed for evaluating how multimodal video models connect dialogue, speakers, visual evidence, and narrative meaning.
  </details>

- **[Toward Cross-Rubric Surgical Skill Assessment via Joint Embedding Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15125)**  `arXiv:2611.15125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiajun Cheng, Haidar M. Abdul-Muhsin, Juha Choi, Shan Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert review of surgical videos is essential for surgical training but labor-intensive, motivating AI-assisted skill assessment to provide feedback at scale. Although surgical assessment rubrics may use different language to describe similar skills, existing automated methods commonly predict predefined skill levels or numerical scores tied to fixed rubrics, limiting their ability to transfer across semantically equivalent assessment criteria. We propose Predictive Rubric Alignment (PRA), a joint embedding prediction approach that scores surgical videos using natural-language descriptions of skill levels. Rather than matching level probabilities, PRA predicts a rater-weighted combination of skill-level description embeddings. Adjacent description features define a score-parameterized path, and the predicted embedding is projected onto this path to read the score. We introduce two variants: \textit{PRA-item}, which uses learned queries for predefined assessment items, and \textit{PRA-cond}, which generates queries from textual item names to enable assessment of unseen items without retraining. On AIxSuture, a dataset of expert-rated suturing videos, PRA outperforms published baselines in skill classification. We further evaluate zero-shot transfer to the smaller surgical skill assessment benchmark JIGSAWS. Trained on only two AIxSuture rubric items, PRA-cond assesses unseen JIGSAWS items without further training, yielding positive score correlations and greater Grad-CAM overlap with instruments and suture than a description-matching baseline. These results support predictive rubric alignment as a promising direction for flexible and explainable automated surgical skill assessment.
  </details>

- **[Keep Images in Sight: Visual Position Re-Anchoring for Long-Context MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15100)**  `arXiv:2611.15100`  `cs.CV`  
  _Juntao Xu, Qichai Zhao, Rui Sun, Zhouyang Jiang, Shiwei Wu, Zhi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context multimodal intelligence requires revisiting earlier images as conversations and documents accumulate, yet retaining an image does not guarantee reliable access to its details. By varying context length, redistributing a fixed text passage around the image, and changing positions without adding text, we identify the positional relation between images and questions as a direct influence on visual access. To address this gap, we propose Visual Position Re-Anchoring (VPR), a training-free method that shifts cached visual-key rotary phases toward a short-context reference before question processing. It operates directly on the existing KV cache, preserving standard attention computation without extracting attention maps or adding model forward passes. We develop static and continuous compensation schedules, using the static schedule by default. Across ten checkpoints and ten benchmarks, static VPR improves 97 of 100 model--task scores. Further analysis shows greater attention to regions containing answer evidence across six checkpoints, with minimal runtime overhead under fixed rotary frequencies. These findings demonstrate an efficient way to sustain visual access through positional adjustment within the cache.
  </details>

- **[SAVE: Less is More in Agentic Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15079)**  `arXiv:2611.15079`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tymoteusz Wilk, Sergey Prokudin, Xi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic video-reasoning frameworks iteratively explore a video, choosing what to observe based on the query and evidence gathered so far. Existing methods are typically evaluated with different backbones, preprocessing pipelines, and computational budgets, making it difficult to determine which components actually drive performance, how much they cost, and whether agentic inference is preferable to native video reasoning in the first place. We conduct a controlled evaluation of a diverse set of recent agentic video-reasoning systems across two strong contemporary backbones, with explicit token and monetary-cost accounting. Across Gemini 3 Flash and Gemma 4, most existing agentic frameworks fail to consistently improve over native video inference and often increase computational cost. We investigate two primary questions: (1) How much agentic framework complexity is necessary for effective video reasoning? (2) When, if at all, is agentic video reasoning preferable to just using the backbone directly? To answer the first question, we introduce a deliberately minimal Active Exploration Baseline and show that it remains competitive with substantially more elaborate agents. We then propose targeted extensions to this baseline, yielding SAVE (Simple Agentic Video Explorer). SAVE outperforms all evaluated prior agentic methods in five of six backbone-benchmark settings. Turning to the second question, our results indicate that the advantage of agentic exploration grows when dense processing would otherwise represent answer-relevant events too sparsely, particularly for long videos and temporally concentrated evidence. Overall, our results suggest that agentic video reasoning should favor simple frameworks over increasingly elaborate pipelines and focus primarily on long-form video, where selective evidence acquisition offers the clearest advantage over native inference.
  </details>

- **[Back to the Question: Tracing Visual Distraction Through Text Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15039)**  `arXiv:2611.15039`  `cs.CV`  
  _Marie Amale Huynh, Regev Cohen, Serena Yeung-Levy, Sivan Doveh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The visual world is cluttered: robust vision-language reasoning requires ignoring irrelevant information. Yet vision-language models (VLMs) can suffer substantial accuracy losses when answering questions about a target image with distractors present. Existing mitigations focus on selecting relevant images or limiting information exchange between images. Through a systematic analysis of three open 7-8B VLMs on five perception benchmarks adapted to include distractor images, we show that distractors reduce accuracy even when models correctly identify the relevant image, suggesting that selection alone is not enough. To understand the mechanism behind this interference, we combine attention analysis with targeted knockouts in Qwen2.5-VL and identify middle-layer distractor-to-text connections as a pathway through which irrelevant visual information affects reasoning. We introduce answer consistency regularization, which encourages consistent answer distributions with and without distractors. Without directly constraining attention, this objective reduces middle-layer attention from question tokens to distractor images, consistent with the pathway our analysis implicates. With five distractors, our approach improves circular accuracy by an average of 16 percentage points over the Qwen2.5-VL baseline across the five benchmarks while preserving single-image performance.
  </details>

- **[RSLiveBench-UAV: A Continually Evolving Vision-Language Benchmark for Aerial Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14953)**  `arXiv:2611.14953`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yue Zhou, Jiawei Liu, Jue Chen, Ziyang Gong, Haojia Wei, Jingxiang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial spatial intelligence (ASI) requires models to understand objects and spatial relationships across changing viewpoints and scales. Evaluating these abilities as models evolve calls for fresh physical observations, yet producing new aerial spatial questions requires repeatedly establishing entity identities, geometric references, and cross-view correspondences. We introduce \textbf{RSLiveBench-UAV}, a continually evolving vision-language benchmark driven by recurring acquisition of real UAV imagery. Its update protocol admits images captured after the preceding release and delays answer disclosure. An entity-centered production workflow uses geometry-assisted association to link repeated observations of physical vehicles, reuse human-verified attributes, and combine them with view-specific annotations and spatial references for traceable question generation. All tasks use deterministic scoring without model judges. We evaluate 30 general-purpose, remote-sensing, and embodied models on 35,491 question records from ten scenes, covering 17 tasks across 2D perception, 3D perception, cross-view perception, and reasoning. All 18 tested embodied models outperform the three selected remote-sensing comparators in Overall, although the strongest embodied model scores 43.29 versus 59.88 for GPT-6-Astra. Fine-grained vehicle recognition remains challenging, and performance varies across heights and camera pitches. Retrospective scene-group comparisons and rolling-window replay characterize score-pattern consistency under scene renewal. RSLiveBench-UAV connects fresh physical observations, entity-centered answer production, and systematic model evaluation to support continual assessment of ASI. Benchmark will release atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Reality Perception Gap: Are AI-Manipulated Video Detectors Ready for the Real World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14941)**  `arXiv:2611.14941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangrui Liu, Xinmiao Luan, Xinyuan Wang, Zhiyuan Fang, Chi-Yao Huang, Douglas C. Montgomery, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-manipulated video detectors are typically evaluated on clean, curated datasets. In deployment, however, videos have usually passed through repost compression on social-media platforms and sometimes were captured by imperfect hardware. To measure detection under these conditions, we introduce Reality Perception Gap (RPG), a benchmark of 10,000 videos from frontier generators and partially synthetic edits, processed with cross-platform repost compression and paired with real footage that carries camera-side optical artifacts. RPG also includes socially fragile content, such as crime and wartime footage, where fabricated videos can cause severe social harm. Across 18 detectors, none catches more than 15% of fakes at a 1% false-positive rate. A positive control and a re-encoding sweep separate two sources of failure: generator and content shift degrades detectors before any compression, and repost compression erases the low-level traces that the artifact-based detectors relies on. Existing detectors therefore struggle when deployment conditions strip away the generation artifacts they rely on. These complementary failures suggest that robust detection requires training under deployment conditions and combining low-level, temporal, and semantic evidence, where passive detection may have reached its limits.
  </details>

- **[Paired Geometric Supervision for Generalizable Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14851)**  `arXiv:2611.14851`  `cs.CV`  
  _Luke Collins, Chang-Tsun Li, Duc Thanh Nguyen, Victor Sanchez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OrthoPairGTL, a geometric training framework for cross-domain deepfake detection, built on a partially frozen DINOv3 backbone ($\sim$300M parameters), and make three contributions: (1) We re-evaluate six recent transfer-learning detectors (Forensics Adapter, Effort, three GenD variants and FakeSTormer) using the authors' released checkpoints under a single preprocessing pipeline across 17 cross-domain datasets (2018-2025), so that the reported gaps reflect method rather than per-method pipeline tuning. (2) We model the transformation between paired real and fake samples, training Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) adapters with an orthogonal pair decomposition, hyperspherical regularisation, and (in the GSD-LoRA variant) a set-level coherence objective. (3) Under LoRA, this regularisation gives a more stable optimisation profile: GSD-LoRA's post-peak validation degradation is $4.2\times$ shallower than plain LoRA at the same parameter footprint, and the time-averaged validation AUC across training is 1.82 pp higher. On the 17 datasets, GSD-LoRA matches or exceeds every re-evaluated baseline (92.82% vs. 91.58% for the strongest; paired Wilcoxon signed-rank $p=0.015$, 13/17 per-dataset wins), with the largest gains on modern reenactment-heavy benchmarks (DSv1.1, DSv2). Under strict parameter budgets, LayerNorm-only tuning ($\approx$0.03% of the backbone) is the most stable configuration and matches the strongest baseline with a mean of 91.97%.
  </details>

- **[Neural Compression for Scientific Particle Data: Perspectives on How Learning Can Help](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14832)**  `arXiv:2611.14832`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tian Qiu, Carolina Cuesta-Lazaro, Sheng Di, Haewon Jeong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As particle-based simulations grow in scale, efficient compression of scientific data becomes critical. Neural point cloud compression (PCC) methods excel on computer vision (CV) benchmarks, but do they transfer to scientific particle data? We highlight that scientific particles differ from CV point clouds in fractal dimension: CV point clouds are locally planar, but particles are not. This key difference limits the effectiveness of the octree data structure that underpins most PCC methods. Neural codecs achieve only marginal bitrate reductions over the traditional G-PCC baseline on scientific data, revealing a significant performance gap. Supported by an evaluation spanning domains of cosmology, molecular dynamics, and plasma physics, we outline where the structure of scientific data limits current neural approaches and where learning can help in the future.
  </details>

- **[RECALL: Real-Time Recalibration of Multi-Camera Rigs via Learned OEM-Anchored Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14763)**  `arXiv:2611.14763`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mario Alberto Tapia de la Cruz, Charalambos Poullis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-camera rigs ship pre-calibrated from the manufacturer, but their calibration goes stale each time the rig is repositioned, and drifts continuously through wear, thermal cycling, and vibration. Recovering it on-the-fly is hard: classical bundle adjustment needs a fresh multi-view capture and a global optimization whose cost grows with the rig, ruling out real-time use. In this paper we present RECALL, a feed-forward method that recalibrates a movable multi-camera rig in a single pass. From the nominal OEM calibration as an anchor and a brief observation of a known object, it predicts each camera's current intrinsics and extrinsics, written as a shared global rotation of the rig composed with a small per-camera drift. Instead of regressing the calibration in one shot, RECALL refines its estimate with a learned, rig-coupled recurrent operator that reprojects through a differentiable projection and applies attention across the cameras at each step, and solves for the shared rotational gauge in-network with a learned Gauss-Newton step rather than regressing it. Because the ground truth is synthesizable, we train entirely on data generated on-the-fly and need no captured dataset. On a synthetic 10-camera benchmark, RECALL is more accurate than per-camera and rig-coupled joint bundle adjustment ($0.26^\circ$ vs $0.47^\circ$ rotation, $5.4$ vs $8.9$ px focal) and runs about $10^5\times$ faster at about $0.12$ ms per rig. On real data (TotalCapture, CMU Panoptic) it matches or beats offline rig-coupled bundle adjustment on absolute and relative rotation while running four-to-five orders of magnitude faster, and on the Panoptic surround rig it also matches the strongest image-only foundation models (VGGT, VGGT-$\Omega$, $\pi^3$) on relative pose while staying metric and about $15\times$ more accurate in focal length. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SyntheIce: A Synthetic Radargram Annotation Benchmark for Englacial Layer Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14728)**  `arXiv:2611.14728`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Behroze Hassan, Bayu Adhi Tama, Sanjay Purushotham, Vandana P. Janeja_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Englacial layers in radio-echo-sounding radargrams (subsurface ice images) encode the climate and flow history of ice sheets and glaciers. However, large-scale extraction is hindered by time-consuming manual annotation and semi-automated detectors that produce fragmented rather than continuous horizons. We address two challenges: reconstructing complete layers from partial detections across radargram profiles and validating layer continuity across adjacent tile boundaries. We introduce a slope-aware greedy fragment-linking algorithm that reconstructs full-width layers using row-similarity gating and an exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) slope predictor. Each reconstruction receives a content-based hash ID for stable cross-radargram identification. For quantitative evaluation, we generate SyntheIce, a synthetic dataset of 600 radargrams spanning multiple complexity levels and realistic noise conditions, including 150 wide radargrams divided into four adjacent tiles. All samples include exact ground-truth annotations. We evaluate the dataset using XCheck, a consistency-based validation framework, extending its original two-image setting to four consecutive tiles and enforcing continuity at every boundary. This demonstrates the dataset's utility for developing and evaluating real-world annotation algorithms. Our findings extend beyond ice science: when spatial features are fragmented but retain consistent local geometry, trajectory-based linking is more reliable than appearance-based matching. Evaluation against expert-labeled real radargrams demonstrates high precision, recall, F1-score, and mean IoU, supporting the method's transfer to real-world data.
  </details>

- **[UniFlow-RS: Unified Generative Prediction for Heterogeneous Remote-Sensing Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14281)**  `arXiv:2611.14281`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jianlong He, Liang Liao, Jing Xiao, Chaofeng Chen, Gui-Song Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote-sensing foundation models increasingly unify representations across sensing modalities and downstream tasks, yet their prediction mechanisms remain largely task-specific. Separate decoders, prediction heads, and objectives leave prediction fragmented across tasks. This naturally raises a question: can heterogeneous remote-sensing tasks share the prediction process? UniFlow-RS answers this question with a single latent flow-matching predictor shared across tasks. This requires addressing two key challenges: unifying heterogeneous prediction spaces and harmonizing joint optimization across tasks. First, we cast heterogeneous task outputs into structured visual targets in a common latent space, allowing them to be modeled by the same pretrained flow predictor. Task-dependent conditioning further accommodates differences in input configurations. Second, we optimize all tasks with a common Flow Matching objective, eliminating task-specific prediction losses. To mitigate optimization mismatch across tasks and modalities, we introduce Task-Modality Adaptive Timestep Sampling (TMAS), which dynamically allocates training timesteps according to the online learning state of each task-modality group. Using a single shared model, UniFlow-RS handles six tasks and thirteen datasets, surpassing recent task-specific state-of-the-art methods on several benchmarks while remaining competitive on the others. Beyond these benchmarks, the same checkpoint demonstrates strong zero-shot transfer across unseen datasets, task settings, occlusion conditions, and near-infrared false-color imagery, without target-specific adaptation. Codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RF3DGS: Reflection-Free 3D Gaussian Splatting with Self-Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13772)**  `arXiv:2611.13772`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiang Wen, Guotao Meng, Junhong Ruan, Hongyu Liu, Chenyang Lei, Qifeng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D reflection removal is crucial in applications demanding high-fidelity visualization, such as museum exhibitions. In this work, we propose Reflection-Free 3D Gaussian Splatting (RF3DGS), a novel method for superior reflection removal. RF3DGS exclusively leverages a widely-adopted photometric prior for reflection removal: that the minimum pixel intensity across multi-view images corresponding to the same 3D point strongly correlates with reflection-free information. However, generalizing this principle to a 3D Gaussian Splatting within a multi-view context is non-trivial. To address this, RF3DGS first optimizes a vanilla 3DGS model using a geometric prior to estimate a coarse, reflection-free depth map for each view. Leveraging these depth maps and photometric prior, a 3D warping process aggregates reflection-free information from auxiliary views to construct a clean transmission pseudo-label for a given reference view. This pseudo-label provides a robust self-supervised signal for optimization. We further introduce an Adaptive Twin Rendering (ATR) loss to synergistically utilize this pseudo-label while preserving high-frequency details from the original, reflection-degraded images. By adeptly fusing these internal 3D self-priors with the ATR loss, RF3DGS demonstrates state-of-the-art reflection removal performance across both synthetic and real-world scenarios. Furthermore, we establish the first 3D reflection removal benchmark, based on the Unity rendering engine, for rigorous quantitative evaluation.
  </details>

- **[DisasterVLG: Vision-Language Grounding and Evidence-Based Reasoning for Disaster Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12144)**  `arXiv:2611.12144`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ali Diba, Aisha Al-Mohannadi, Yin Yang, Muhammad Imran, Ferda Ofli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose vision-language models struggle with crisis imagery due to its inherent noise, ambiguity, and high variability across disaster events - yet timely, accurate scene understanding is critical for humanitarian response. To address this, we introduce DisasterVLG, a large-scale multi-task dataset containing 1.24 million disaster images from 142 countries paired with 33 million structured annotations spanning dense captioning with localization, incident assessment, relational scene descriptions, and crisis-grounded visual question answering (VQA) across diverse hazard types and scene contexts worldwide. Unlike prior disaster benchmarks that focus narrowly on classification, DisasterVLG unifies recognition, grounded reasoning, and uncertainty-aware interpretation. A distinguishing feature is its evidence-grounded annotation design: each VQA instance includes supporting evidence text that links visual observations to answers, alongside positive and negative question pairs, multiple answer formats, and difficulty labels. Visual grounding layer links caption phrases to image regions and relationship statements to subject-object boxes, with explicit labels for visual supports and clues. To support rigorous evaluation, we construct a human-verified test set of 38K images (1.15M annotations) spanning broad geographic regions and disaster types. Experiments with domain-adapted vision-language models demonstrate strong performance across disaster captioning, incident recognition, and grounded VQA, while also exposing the limitations of general-purpose models in crisis-specific reasoning. DisasterVLG establishes a scalable, reproducible benchmark for vision-language models that must reason reliably under real-world uncertainty. We will publicly release the dataset, trained models, and code.
  </details>

- **[Interactive Visual Gym: Evaluating Visual Interaction Models with Simulated Users in Interactive Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08286)**  `arXiv:2611.08286`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenhao Zheng, Zixian Ma, Kai Li, Kanchana Ranasinghe, Jieyu Zhang, Artemis Panagopoulou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual interaction models that support users during ongoing physical tasks require evaluation in closed loop, where assistant responses can change subsequent user behavior and task outcomes. Existing benchmarks largely rely on offline replay and therefore cannot capture these effects. We introduce Interactive Visual Gym, a framework for closed-loop evaluation and training of visual interaction models through joint simulation of executable task environments and human users. We contribute three simulators that recreate everyday tasks and human-aligned action spaces across cooking, household manipulation, and mobile-interface domains, together with a persona-conditioned user simulation harness that collaborates with the assistant while incorporating controlled execution errors and other out-of-plan behaviors. We instantiate the framework as IVG-Bench, a benchmark with 315 interaction scenarios. Evaluating state-of-the-art VLMs as visual interaction models reveals substantial limitations: the best-performing model, Claude Opus 5.5, completes only 53.5% of tasks within the time limit, exhibits significant weaknesses in factual grounding and actionable guidance, and is often too slow to prevent errors when response latency is considered. We verify our evaluation protocol with a human study which finds 78.5% agreement between our interaction-quality metric and participant preferences. We further show that the environments provide useful learning signals: in a proof-of-concept GRPO experiment, closed-loop training improves both task success and interaction quality for Qwen3.5-4B model in our cooking environment.
  </details>

- **[Anchored Rank-Adversarial Fine-Tuning of Pretrained Visual Place Recognition Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05286)**  `arXiv:2611.05286`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mostafa Kira, Bader Rasheed, Manuel Mazzara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual place recognition (VPR) localises a camera by retrieving similar geo-tagged images, and current foundation-model VPR models are accurate across cities, seasons and illumination, but not robust to adversarial perturbations: under a white-box attack on the ranking at an imperceptible budget of 1/255, SuperVLAD and BoQ keep at most 0.2% top-1 recall (R@1) on four benchmarks. We propose anchored rank-adversarial fine-tuning (ARAFT), which fine-tunes a released model with a listwise rank loss on each adversarial query's hardest positive and an anchor to the clean descriptor of the frozen pretrained model. Checkpoints are selected under a clean-accuracy budget fixed in advance, and the full trade-off is reported. Within five validation R@1 points of clean cost, plain adversarial training has no admissible checkpoint, whereas ARAFT raises robust R@1 from zero to 47.3 on SuperVLAD and 55.3 on BoQ; plain adversarial training exceeds this only at about three times the clean cost. Against unbudgeted plain adversarial training on the test sets, ARAFT matches its robust R@1 at 2/255 with about ten more clean R@1 points. At 4/255, plain adversarial training is 6-9 robust R@1 points ahead, while ARAFT still keeps its ten-point clean advantage. Developed on SuperVLAD, ARAFT transfers to BoQ without re-tuning, and its robustness holds against stronger optimisers, with no sign of gradient masking.
  </details>

- **[Blueprint2CAD: Benchmarking Foundation Models on Engineering Drawing-to-CAD Render Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02433)**  `arXiv:2611.02433`  `cs.CV`  
  _Alexander Valverde, Danny Xie-Li, Zhichao Wang, Marcos V. Conde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Engineering drawings specify mechanical parts through orthographic projections. Recognizing which part a drawing depicts underlies part search, design reuse, and digitization, yet no public benchmark measures whether vision models can do it. We introduce Blueprint2CAD, a benchmark for drawing-to-render correspondence: given an orthographic line drawing and a shaded render, usually from another viewpoint, decide whether they show the same part. Its data layer, BlueCAD-30K, applies an open drafting pipeline to 29,925 Zero-to-CAD models, adding view-aligned renders, clean and dimensioned drawings from six viewpoints, and surface-type metadata. Frozen encoders retrieve the matching part from a 10,000-model gallery with up to 65% Recall@1 when viewpoints are known but 43% when they are not, and the four encoders we test lie within six points of each other: viewpoint, not backbone, is the bottleneck. Six zero-shot vision-language models reach only 0.52-0.67 verification accuracy, with model-specific Yes/No biases that chain-of-thought shifts but does not remove; a quick LoRA fine-tuning on in-domain pairs improves verification accuracy by a few points but sharpens these biases and leaves the viewpoint failure intact.
  </details>

- **[SPARE: Shared Perception and Adaptive Retrieval of Evidence for Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14671)**  `arXiv:2610.14671`  `cs.CV`  
  _Senbin Xu, Zijun Zhang, Xinhua Wu, Li He, Jubo Feng, Li Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video understanding requires answering questions from the observed video, with evidence drawn from recent observations or earlier events. Historical context can improve recall but may interfere with judgments based on recent frames. Its usefulness depends on both when it is introduced and which clips are selected, while relevance alone does not guarantee sufficient evidence for an answer. We propose SPARE, a framework that combines adaptive historical retrieval with visual encoder sharing. A lightweight gate predicts whether recent observations provide sufficient evidence to answer the question. When additional evidence is predicted to be necessary, a multimodal embedding model retrieves relevant historical clips to supplement the current context. LoRA instruction tuning trains the understanding model to use recent observations and retrieved history jointly and abstain when the evidence remains insufficient. To reduce redundant visual parameters, the understanding and retrieval branches share a visual encoder while retaining separate language backbones. Four linear adapters align the shared features with the retrieval model through distillation at the visual token and final retrieval embedding levels. With visual-only inputs, SPARE achieves 77.78% on the evaluated Real-time and Backward task groups of OVO-Bench and 73.31% on StreamingBench. Compared with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct evaluated at 2 fps, SPARE improves accuracy by 20.68 percentage points on OVO-Bench and 14.15 percentage points on StreamingBench.
  </details>

- **[When Does an Image Determine the Answer? Benchmarking Visual Answerability across Charts and Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34480)**  `arXiv:2609.34480`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sungguk Cha, Mintae Kim, Youngsub Han, Byoung-Ki Jeon, Sangyeob Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable visual question answering requires correct answers when evidence is sufficient and abstention when it is not. We introduce a benchmark of 3,000 question groups: 1,000 each from PlotQA charts, CLEVR rendered scenes, and GQA photographs. Each group keeps the question fixed across original, answer-preserving, and evidence-removing images; charts and CLEVR also include answer-changing edits. Models receive each image independently. A group succeeds only if every supported answer and every required abstention is correct. For charts and CLEVR, executable witnesses establish ambiguity under the declared task semantics: two admissible complete charts or scenes give different answers but yield the same masked observation. GQA scoring compares source labels with a permissive rule that also credits masked-image answers compatible with the full-image reference. Across 72,000 responses from six model configurations, the best complete-question success is 57.0% on charts, 43.5% on CLEVR, and 33.7% on GQA under source labels. On charts, the strongest configuration makes correct answer-or-abstain decisions on 96.2% of individual images and on all five images of 83.5% of questions. Requiring correct answers lowers success to 57.0%: in 26.5% of all chart questions, every decision is correct but at least one answer is wrong. Adding answer correctness also reverses a chart ranking based on decision accuracy. Evaluating all response obligations together reveals failures that per-image decision accuracy conceals.
  </details>

- **[Geometry-Preserving Blind Watermarking for Raw 3D Point Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32222)**  `arXiv:2609.32222`  `cs.CV`  
  _Rungui Zhou, Chuanzhi Zhou, Ruihuan Wang, Peng-Shuai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Raw 3D point clouds are a core geometric representation. Establishing their ownership is challenging because point sets are irregular, unstructured, and frequently altered by resampling and geometric preprocessing. We present a blind watermarking framework that operates directly on xyz coordinates and supports both object-level shapes and scene-scale scans. At verification time, the embedded message is recovered from the observed point cloud alone, without access to the original point cloud, color, normals, or mesh connectivity. The method jointly learns watermark embedding and extraction through a feed-forward octree-based architecture, enabling efficient multi-scale geometric reasoning on large point sets. During training, a stochastic transformation layer exposes the decoder to common geometric perturbations, while progressive pose alignment improves robustness to pose changes.Experiments on object-level and scene-level benchmarks demonstrate reliable message recovery under common geometric processing while maintaining low geometric distortion. Qualitative comparisons further show that the learned perturbations are less visually conspicuous and less spatially structured than those of handcrafted alternatives.
  </details>

- **[GlyphAnchor: Enhancing Visual Text Rendering via Position-Anchored Glyph Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02349)**  `arXiv:2609.02349`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiang Xiang, Shuang Sun, Binglei Li, Yibo Chen, Xu Tang, Yao Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rendering accurate text remains difficult for image generation and editing models, especially when the target contains long, complex, and densely arranged text or rare characters. Existing approaches either improve native text rendering through stronger backbones and data-centric training without explicit glyph priors, or incorporate glyph priors through specialized designs that remain insufficiently accurate and robust under challenging scenarios. We introduce GlyphAnchor, a novel text-rendering enhancement method for both text-to-image and image-editing diffusion transformer models. GlyphAnchor enhances the backbone with lightweight glyph patch conditions whose positions are anchored to the target image through the model's native positional encoding. We train this capability with staged supervised finetuning and further refine it with text-aware post-training to improve robustness. We also introduce InfoTextBench, a benchmark for evaluating text-rich visual text rendering in both generation and editing settings. Experiments across multiple backbones and benchmarks, including long, complex, and densely arranged text and rare character scenarios, show that GlyphAnchor consistently improves text fidelity while preserving overall image quality.
  </details>

- **[CORAL: A Benchmark for Structure-aware and Brain-wide Neuron Reconstruction in Light Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30768)**  `arXiv:2608.30768`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zekang Yang, Jiamin Li, Zhenghua Li, Jiaqi Fan, Zengcai Guo, Xiaolin Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic neuron reconstruction from light microscopy images is a central problem in computational neuroanatomy. While recent methods have achieved encouraging results on local image blocks, it remains unclear whether such progress translates to reconstruction that is both structurally accurate and scalable to the whole-brain scale. We present CORAL, the first benchmark for structure-aware evaluation of automatic neuron reconstruction from light microscopy images at both local and whole-brain scales. Built on a high-quality whole-brain fMOST dataset with carefully curated annotations, CORAL establishes two progressive tasks: block-level reconstruction, which evaluates reconstruction methods under limited spatial context, and brain-wide reconstruction, which assesses complete neuron reconstruction at the whole-brain scale. To account for topological correctness beyond geometric distance similarity, we introduce a structure-aware metric based on fiber prediction. To further achieve complete neuron reconstruction across the entire brain, we develop a brain-wide neuron tracing framework that extends arbitrary local reconstruction methods to the whole-brain scale through an iterative local-to-global process. Using this benchmark, we provide the first structure-aware comparison of mainstream methods for local neuron reconstruction and further evaluate their performance in brain-wide reconstruction. Our results underscore the importance of structure-aware evaluation and the need for more robust methods for complete neuron reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[ChessQueries: Toward Better Chess Board Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30762)**  `arXiv:2608.30762`  `cs.CV`  
  _Joël Seytre_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chess board recognition is the task of mapping the image of a chess board to the information of which piece is on which square. So far this task has two established benchmarks: ChessCog is synthetic, and ChessReD comes from smartphone pictures of a single chess board setup. We introduce ChessQueries, a new method combining a ViT encoder with a DETR-style decoder, which outperforms existing methods. On the ChessReD benchmark, we improve the state of the art from 15.3% to 99.2%, and demonstrate strong capabilities on out-of-distribution datasets. Our method saturates the task on the two datasets, with an average 0.01 wrong squares per board (vs. SotA: 3.4 / 0.15 respectively). We also share a new, harder public dataset, parsed from broadcasted top-level chess tournaments. Code, model weights and the SLCC data have been released.
  </details>

- **[Disambiguating 2D-3D Correspondences in Gaussian Splatting-based Feature Fields for Visual Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07351)**  `arXiv:2605.07351`  `cs.CV`  
  _Miso Lee, Sangeek Hyun, Yerim Jeon, Jae-Pil Heo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Gaussian Splatting-based Feature Fields (GSFFs) have shown promise for visual localization, this paper highlights that photometrically optimized GSFFs are inherently not suitable for 2D-3D matching. The volumetric extent of each Gaussian induces many-to-one pixel-to-point mappings that destabilize PnP-based pose estimation, while photometric optimization gives rise to superfluous Gaussians devoid of multi-view consistency. To address these issues, we propose SplitGS-Loc, a GSFFs construction framework for localization, which disambiguates 2D-3D correspondences by exploiting Gaussian attributes. Our key design, Mixture-of-Gaussians-based splitting, decomposes each Gaussian into smaller Gaussians, replacing ambiguous many-to-one with precise one-to-one correspondences. In parallel, we exploit composition weights from GS rasterization to select Gaussians that significantly and consistently contribute across multiple views and aggregate discriminative features through strong pixel-Gaussian associations. The resulting compact yet discriminative feature fields enable stable PnP convergence, achieving competitive performance on localization benchmarks. Extensive experiments validate that SplitGS-Loc extends the utility of photometric GSFFs to accurate and efficient localization by exploiting Gaussian attributes, without per-scene training for feature fields or iterative pose refinement. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Common Principal Component Networks for Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05136)**  `arXiv:2605.05136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu-Hsi Chen, Abd-Krim Seghouane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain Generalization (DG) aims to learn representations that generalize to unseen target domains under out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts. Despite recent advances in invariant representation learning, explicitly capturing shared structures across source-domain distributions remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we integrate Common Principal Component Analysis (CPCA) into deep learning to identify a common orthogonal basis across multiple domains, proposing two complementary end-to-end differentiable frameworks. First, we introduce Common Principal Component Network (CPCNet), which unrolls the iterative Flury-Gautschi (FG) algorithm into fully differentiable neural layers, enabling covariance-based joint diagonalization within end-to-end representation learning. Second, to address rank deficiency arising from mini-batch covariance estimation in small-sample regimes, we develop Projection Pursuit-based Common Principal Component Network (PP-CPCNet), which directly optimizes a common orthogonal basis without explicit covariance estimation. Experiments validate the joint diagonalization capabilities of both methods, while evaluations on four standard DG benchmarks demonstrate competitive zero-shot generalization performance with favorable computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[A Lightweight Global-Target Framework for Multi-Domain No-Reference Image Quality Assessment in UAV Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13112)**  `arXiv:2604.13112`  `cs.CV`  
  _Koffi Titus Sergio Aglin, Anthony K. Muchiri, Celestin Nkundineza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable image quality assessment is essential in applications where large volumes of images are acquired automatically and must be filtered before further analysis. In many practical scenarios, a pristine reference image is unavailable, making no reference image quality assessment (NR-IQA) particularly important. This paper introduces Multi-Metric Image Quality Assessment (MM-IQA), a lightweight multi-metric framework for NR-IQA. It combines interpretable cues related to blur, edge structure, low resolution artifacts, exposure imbalance, noise, haze, and frequency content to produce a single quality score in the range [0,100].MM-IQA was evaluated on five benchmark datasets (KonIQ-10k, LIVE Challenge, KADID-10k, TID2013, and BIQ2021) and achieved SRCC values ranging from 0.647 to 0.830. Additional experiments on a synthetic agricultural dataset showed consistent behavior of the designed cues. The Python/OpenCV implementation required about 1.97 s per image. This method also has modest memory requirements because it stores only a limited number of intermediate grayscale, filtered, and frequency-domain representations, resulting in memory usage that scales linearly with image size. The results show that MM-IQA can be used for fast image quality screening with explicit distortion aware cues and modest computational cost.
  </details>

- **[Open-Set Supervised 3D Anomaly Detection: An Industrial Dataset and a Generalisable Framework for Unknown Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01171)**  `arXiv:2604.01171`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hanzhe Liang, Luocheng Zhang, Junyang Xia, HanLiang Zhou, Bingyang Guo, Yingxi Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although self-supervised 3D anomaly detection assumes that acquiring high-precision point clouds is computationally expensive, in real manufacturing scenarios it is often feasible to collect a limited number of anomalous samples. Therefore, we study open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection, where the model is trained with only normal samples and a small number of known anomalous samples, aiming to identify unknown anomalies at test time. We present Open-Industry, a high-quality industrial dataset containing 15 categories, each with five real anomaly types collected from production lines. We first adapt general open-set anomaly detection methods to accommodate 3D point cloud inputs better. Building upon this, we propose Open3D-AD, a point-cloud-oriented approach that leverages normal samples, simulated anomalies, and partially observed real anomalies to model the probability density distributions of normal and anomalous data. Then, we introduce a simple Correspondence Distributions Subsampling to reduce the overlap between normal and non-normal distributions, enabling stronger dual distributions modeling. Based on these contributions, we establish a comprehensive benchmark and evaluate the proposed method extensively on Open-Industry as well as established datasets including Real3D-AD and Anomaly-ShapeNet. Benchmark results and ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of Open3D-AD and further reveal the potential of open-set supervised 3D anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[MMEarth-Bench: Global Model Adaptation via Multimodal Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06285)**  `arXiv:2602.06285`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lucia Gordon, Serge Belongie, Christian Igel, Nico Lang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent research in geospatial machine learning demonstrates that models pretrained with self-supervised learning on Earth observation data can perform well on downstream tasks with limited labeled data. However, most benchmark datasets have few data modalities and poor global representation, limiting the ability to evaluate multimodal pretrained models at global scales. In order to fill this gap, we introduce MMEarth-Bench, a collection of five new environmental tasks with 12 modalities, globally distributed data, and both random and geographic test splits. We benchmark a diverse set of pretrained models and find that while (multimodal) pretraining tends to improve model robustness in limited data settings, geographic generalization abilities remain poor. Moreover, a simple randomly initialized multimodal model is competitive given enough labeled data. Although data is abundant, models can currently only make use of the modalities on which they were pretrained. To solve this problem, we propose using all the modalities available at test time as auxiliary tasks for test-time adaptation. Our model-agnostic method for test-time training with multimodal reconstruction (TTT-MMR) can improve performance across all models and tasks on both test splits. Furthermore, geographic batching leads to a good trade-off between regularization and specialization during TTT, which can be beneficial for long-tail distributions. Our dataset, code, and visualization tool are linked on the project page.
  </details>

- **[Do Pathology Foundation Models Encode Disease Progression? A Pseudotime Analysis of Visual Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21334)**  `arXiv:2601.21334`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pritika Vig, Ren-Chin Wu, William Lotter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision foundation models trained on discretely sampled images achieve strong performance on classification benchmarks, yet whether their representations encode the continuous processes underlying their training data remains unclear. This question is especially pertinent in computational pathology, where we posit that models whose latent representations implicitly capture ordinal disease progression may better reflect underlying biology, support more robust generalization, and enable quantitative analyses of features associated with disease transitions. Using diffusion pseudotime, a method developed to infer developmental trajectories from single-cell transcriptomics, we probe whether foundation models organize disease states along coherent progression directions in representation space. Across four cancer progressions and six models, we find that all pathology-specific models recover trajectory orderings significantly exceeding null baselines, with pathology vision-only models achieving the highest average fidelities. Model rankings by trajectory fidelity on reference diseases predict few-shot classification performance on held-out diseases ($\rho = 0.91$), and exploratory analysis shows cell-type composition varies along inferred trajectories in patterns consistent with known stromal remodeling. Together, these results suggest that vision foundation models can implicitly learn to represent continuous processes from independent static observations, and that trajectory fidelity provides a complementary measure of representation quality beyond downstream performance. While demonstrated in pathology, this framework could be applied to other domains where continuous processes are observed through static snapshots.
  </details>

- **[ReflFlow: Learning Geometry-Guided Ray Tracing for Dynamic Specular Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10095)**  `arXiv:2512.10095`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiachen Tao, Junyi Wu, Haoxuan Wang, Zongxin Yang, Dawen Cai, Yan Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present ReflFlow, a novel framework for high-fidelity rendering of dynamic specular scenes by addressing two key challenges: precise reflection direction estimation and physically accurate modeling. To achieve this, we propose a Residual Material-Augmented 2D Gaussian Splatting representation that models dynamic geometry and material properties, allowing accurate reflection ray computation. Furthermore, we introduce a Dynamic Environment Gaussian and a hybrid rendering pipeline that decomposes rendering into diffuse and specular components, enabling physically informed specular synthesis via rasterization and ray tracing. Finally, we devise a coarse-to-fine training strategy to improve optimization stability and promote physically meaningful decomposition. Extensive experiments on dynamic scene benchmarks demonstrate that ReflFlow outperforms prior methods quantitatively and qualitatively, producing sharper and more realistic specular reflections in complex dynamic environments.
  </details>

- **[Robust Image Emotion Classification via Multi-Level Affective Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23115)**  `arXiv:2511.23115`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zibo Zhou, Zhengjun Zhai, Huimin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image emotion classification (IEC) has been studied for many years and has recently attracted increasing attention with the rapid development of deep learning. Although recent studies have benefited from knowledge embedded in pre-trained visual models, the performance is still limited by the "affective gap" between low-level visual features and high-level affective semantics. Psychological studies suggest that language can convey fine-grained and emotion-rich information. Inspired by this observation, we attempt to narrow the "affective gap" by introducing affective text. Specifically, we design a multi-level contrastive loss to detect emotional concepts from images and introduce an emotional-attribute-guided large language model approach to generate affective sentences. Then, a pre-trained language model is employed to combine emotional concepts with affective sentences for IEC. In addition, we introduce a contrastive loss with a semantic-similarity-based sampling strategy to address large intra-class differences and limited inter-class differences in affective datasets. Moreover, unlike many previous studies, we explicitly consider images containing embedded text. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks show that the proposed method helps narrow the "affective gap" and achieves competitive performance.
  </details>

- **[D2D: Detector-to-Differentiable Critic for Improved Numeracy in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19278)**  `arXiv:2510.19278`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nobline Yoo, Olga Russakovsky, Ye Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models have achieved strong performance in semantic alignment, yet they still struggle with generating the correct number of objects specified in prompts. Existing approaches typically incorporate auxiliary counting networks as external critics to enhance numeracy. However, since these critics must provide gradient guidance during generation, they are restricted to regression-based models that are inherently differentiable, thus excluding detector-based models with superior counting ability, whose count-via-enumeration nature is non-differentiable. To overcome this, we propose Detector-to-Differentiable (D2D), a novel framework that transforms non-differentiable detection models into differentiable critics, leveraging their superior counting ability to guide numeracy generation. Specifically, we design custom activation functions to convert detector logits into soft binary indicators, which are then used to optimize the noise prior at inference time with pre-trained T2I models. Our extensive experiments on SDXL-Turbo, SD-Turbo, and Pixart-DMD across four benchmarks of varying complexity (low-density, high-density, and multi-object scenarios) demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements in object counting accuracy (e.g., boosting up to 13.7% on D2D-Small, a 400-prompt, low-density benchmark), with minimal degradation in overall image quality and computational overhead. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Optimized Minimal 4D Gaussian Splatting for Efficient Dynamic Scene Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03857)**  `arXiv:2510.03857`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minseo Lee, Byeonghyeon Lee, Lucas Yunkyu Lee, Eunsoo Lee, JunYoung Jeong, Sangmin Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  4D Gaussian Splatting has emerged as a new paradigm for dynamic scene representation, enabling real-time rendering of scenes with complex motions. However, it faces a major challenge of storage overhead, as millions of Gaussians are required for high-fidelity reconstruction. Compressing explicit 4D Gaussians is challenging because they exhibit heterogeneous redundancy across static and dynamic regions, and their temporal attributes make direct quantization unstable. In this work, we present OMG4 (Optimized Minimal 4D Gaussian Splatting), a framework that constructs a compact set of salient Gaussians capable of faithfully representing 4D Gaussian models. Our method progressively reduces Gaussians in three stages: (1) Gaussian Sampling to identify primitives critical to reconstruction fidelity, (2) Gaussian Pruning to remove redundancies, and (3) Gaussian Merging to fuse primitives with similar characteristics. In addition, we integrate implicit appearance compression and extend Sub-Vector Quantization (SVQ) to 4D representations with a staged quantization scheme, further reducing storage while preserving quality. Extensive experiments on standard benchmark datasets demonstrate that OMG4 achieves a favorable rate-distortion trade-off over recent state-of-the-art methods, reducing model sizes by over 60% while maintaining reconstruction quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of OMG4 as a practical framework for compact and high-fidelity 4D scene representation. Our source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The SAGES Critical View of Safety Challenge: A Global Benchmark for AI-Assisted Surgical Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17100)**  `arXiv:2509.17100`  `cs.CV`  
  _Deepak Alapatt, Jennifer Eckhoff, Zhiliang Lyu, Yutong Ban, Jean-Paul Mazellier, Sarah Choksi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in artificial intelligence (AI) for surgical quality assessment promise to democratize access to expertise, with applications in training, guidance, and accreditation. This study presents the SAGES Critical View of Safety (CVS) Challenge, the first AI competition organized by a surgical society, using the CVS in laparoscopic cholecystectomy, a universally recommended yet inconsistently performed safety step, as an exemplar of surgical quality assessment. A global collaboration across 54 institutions in 24 countries engaged hundreds of clinicians and engineers to curate 1,000 videos annotated by 20 surgical experts according to a consensus-validated protocol. The challenge addressed key barriers to real-world deployment in surgery, including achieving high performance, capturing uncertainty in subjective assessment, and ensuring robustness to clinical variability. To enable this scale of effort, we developed EndoGlacier, a framework for managing large, heterogeneous surgical video and multi-annotator workflows. Thirteen international teams participated, achieving up to a 17% relative gain in assessment performance, over 62% reduction in calibration error, and a 17% relative improvement in robustness over the state-of-the-art. Analysis of results highlighted methodological trends linked to model performance, providing guidance for future research toward robust, clinically deployable AI for surgical quality assessment.
  </details>

- **[Parsed, Plausible, Wrong: Output Format Controls Silent Errors in Small-Model Semantic Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17169)**  `arXiv:2611.17169`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Umar Ahmed, Maxim Dunaev, Mohammad Nour Al Awad, Sergey Ivanov, Ivan Khodnenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A small fine-tuned parser that emits a well-formed but wrong tree fails silently: nothing downstream can tell. We ask whether the output format, a free design choice, controls how often this happens. On the PIZZA ordering benchmark we fine-tune two sub-billion models (Qwen3.5-0.8B and LFM2.5-350M) with LoRA on six lossless encodings of the same target tree, from 44.3 down to 10.9 tokens per order, including two matched-length pairs that vary surface form at fixed length. Across 300 runs (5 training pool sizes, 5 seeds) with a hierarchical bootstrap and Holm correction fixed before the results were read, shortening alone never helps, but writing the target in ordinary words does. The word encoding is the most accurate format on both models (87.5% vs. 85.8% exact match for the native s-expression on Qwen) while emitting 4.1x fewer tokens, and it cuts the silent error rate by 2.3x on Qwen and 1.8x on LFM2, separating at every pool size. Error analysis locates much of the gain in negation. All runs also learn to refuse off-menu input (held out: 95.9-99.0% handled) and to apply in-utterance self-corrections. Strict refusal has a measured price: 2.1-10.1% of real, mostly polite, orders are refused.
  </details>

- **[ShadowBench: Exposing Lexical Anchoring and the Illusion of Forgetting in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15925)**  `arXiv:2611.15925`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Sujan Maharjan, Lu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as primary interfaces for factual retrieval, yet current evaluation paradigms rely almost exclusively on explicit entity names. In this work, we demonstrate that LLM knowledge is fundamentally lexically anchored: models possess extensive factual information but find it significantly difficult to retrieve without explicit name tokens. To quantify this limitation, we introduce ShadowBench, a rigorously hardened, shortcut-resistant benchmark designed to evaluate Latent Entity Association via a novel Dual-Trait Association (DTA) task. Our evaluation reveals a pervasive "Shadow Gap" across all model scales$\unicode{x2013}$up to the frontier models GPT-5.4 and Claude-Sonnet-4.6$\unicode{x2013}$where removing lexical anchors causes performance drops of over 20%. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this lexical dependency exposes a critical vulnerability in AI safety. Applying ShadowBench to Machine Unlearning, we find that state-of-the-art algorithms (e.g., Gradient Difference, NPO) achieve only superficial lexical erasure. While unlearned models fail on direct queries, our novel Latent Entity Leakage Rate (LELR) metric reveals that for popular entities, models explicitly reconstruct the "forgotten" entity in their internal reasoning traces in over 74% of cases, utilizing residual shadow knowledge to solve associative tasks. Conversely, for niche entities, models retain high shadow accuracy (60-90%) via implicit attribute correlation despite 0% LELR. Ultimately, ShadowBench indicates that current unlearning paradigms create an "Illusion of Forgetting," demonstrating that existing methods act as superficial output filters and are not yet reliable for achieving true parametric erasure.
  </details>

- **[SO-Arena: Benchmarking the Capability Spectrum and Reliability of System One Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15777)**  `arXiv:2611.15777`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`
