  _Lichen Li, Ziping Dong, Sicheng Feng, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One models promise fast, general-purpose decision-making without lengthy token generation. However, understanding their capability spectrum and decision reliability requires more than aggregate performance on current benchmarks. Existing evaluations often provide limited insight into the breadth of model capabilities and the reliability of their decisions. We introduce SO-Arena, a benchmark characterizing the capability spectrum and decision reliability of System One models across 11 domains and five capability families. For the capability spectrum, SO-Arena offers evidence-grounded evaluation through reliable sources and manual verification of reference labels against model-visible inputs; fine-grained capability assessment through domain-specific difficulty criteria; and realistic interactive evaluation, where models iteratively execute actions in sandboxes. Reasoning-intensive tasks further enable system one-two comparison. Beyond accuracy, SO-Arena assesses decision reliability through two complementary measures: decision stability under controlled option-set changes and missing-answer recognition when no valid option is available. Evaluations across 21 models reveal that the benefits of scaling and additional reasoning vary substantially across domains. More importantly, strong overall performance does not necessarily imply reliable decision-making: the best-performing System One model ranks only seventh in reliability. Together, these findings highlight the need to characterize capability spectrum and decision reliability as complementary dimensions, providing a systematic view of the strengths and limitations of System One models.
  </details>

- **[AutoOntic: Automatic Ontology Induction from Text for Knowledge Graph Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15467)**  `arXiv:2611.15467`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Alla Chepurova, Aydar Bulatov, Mikhail Burtsev, Yuri Kuratov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation depends on the quality of the knowledge graph (KG) it retrieves over, yet schema-free LLM-based extraction pipelines typically produce flat graphs: deduplicated entities and relations with no type hierarchy and no domain/range constraints. We present AutoOntic, a pipeline that automatically constructs an ontology from text by canonicalizing open-vocabulary entity types and relations, inducing a hierarchy over the canonicalized types, and mining relation-level domain and range constraints. Its hierarchy induction procedure prioritizes types using corpus-derived relation-argument profiles and places them through local, LLM-adjudicated parent/child checks, allowing newly inserted types to trigger reassignment of existing parent-child links. The resulting hierarchy constrains entity deduplication and supports the induction of relation-level domain/range constraints. For intrinsic evaluation, we introduce a reference-based protocol that classifies induced ontology types as exact, broader, narrower, or unrelated to reference domain and range classes. On the Wikidata-TekGen and DBpedia-WebNLG subsets of Text2KGBench, over 90% of AutoOntic's induced endpoint types are semantically aligned with the reference classes across two LLM backbones: about half match exactly, while most remaining differences concern granularity. On MINE, knowledge graphs organized using an ontology built by AutoOntic achieve 88% retrieval accuracy. AutoOntic is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge in the Harness, Reliability in the Weights: Teacher-Free Co-Evolution of an LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15268)**  `arXiv:2611.15268`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yujia Zhang, Xinqi Ren, Shixiong Zhou, Jia Meng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small open models suit private customer-service deployments but trail frontier models in two ways: they miss what the written policy requires in a given situation, and they act inconsistently on what they know. We study whether such a model can close both gaps from its own deployment logs and their reference outcomes, without a stronger teacher model. Our starting point is a Gemma 4 12B agent that reads its domain policy from on-demand skills rather than from its system prompt. In each round, the deployed system serves a fresh batch of tasks; the base model diagnoses failed conversations and writes rules into the skills, and a rule survives only if it passes code checks, counterexample self-review, a paired retest, and a noise-calibrated validation gate. Reinforcement learning (GRPO) then trains a LoRA adapter on complete multi-turn, tool-using conversations. On the retail and airline domains of $\tau^2$-bench, without training or selecting on official tasks, the agent after two rounds improves on this starting point by 12.5 pass$^1$ points in retail and 19.5 in airline, and by 18.4 and 22.0 pass$^4$ points. It also exceeds the benchmark's reference agent, which runs the same model with the whole policy in its system prompt, by 11.8 and 8.0 pass$^1$ points and by 22.8 and 16.0 pass$^4$ points, closing most of the gap to three frontier models under the same protocol. A $2\times2$ ablation shows that the rules rescue tasks the agent never solved and carry policy knowledge that RL with the rules in context does not internalize, whereas the trained weights make solved tasks reliable.
  </details>

- **[NaijaFacts: A Framework for Disentangling Language from Knowledge in Facts about Nigeria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06504)**  `arXiv:2611.06504`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zainab Afolabi, Nikita Kozodoi, Jack Butler, Babs Khalidson, Sabastine Dekaa Francis, Christiana I. Ezeilo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual benchmarks for African languages mostly translate generic questions into local languages, measuring linguistic competence but not whether models know facts about Africa. We introduce NaijaFacts, a benchmark of factual and statistical questions about Nigeria, authored by native speakers and grounded in authoritative official statistics, with every question available in English, Nigerian Pidgin, Yorùbá, Igbo, and Hausa. Answering similar questions for the UK and US yields a country by language matrix that disentangles a language effect (the model knows the fact but degrades in a low-resource language) from a knowledge effect (the fact is absent in every language). Across eight frontier and Nigeria-adapted models, the knowledge effect dominates: English-query accuracy is 49-50% for the US/UK but 20% for Nigeria. The language effect is smaller and tracks web representation, degrading from English to Yorùbá (9 points on Nigeria, up to 24 on US/UK questions). We find scale does not help: the strongest models reach 66-79% on the UK and the US but none exceed 31% on Nigerian statistics. Extending to all 54 African countries with our best performing frontier model shows the same profile continent-wide, 96% on culture and 98% on politics against 26% on statistics, so the gap is not specific to Nigeria.
  </details>

- **[An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093)**  `arXiv:2611.03093`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lakshmi Narayanan P, Narenkarthik K, Naren Kumar C_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support. In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Continuous Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02665)**  `arXiv:2610.02665`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhihan Yang, Wei Guo, Jean-Marie Lemercier, Simon Welker, Mohammad Mahdi Kamani, Yonggan Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the success of discrete diffusion language models (dLMs) for fast parallel decoding, their non-smooth, high-dimensional space hinders trajectory steering for reasoning and inference acceleration. To overcome this, we present Nemotron-Labs-Sigma, the first family of large-scale (3B/8B) continuous dLMs built on steerable, low-dimensional ODE/SDE latent trajectories. Trained blockwise via likelihood optimization, Nemotron-Labs-Sigma jointly denoises Gaussian-corrupted token embeddings while learning an optimal embedding geometry, and leverages pretrained autoregressive (AR) weights for warm-starting. At inference, we identify classifier-free guidance and score temperature as essential for high-fidelity reasoning and coding. Against state-of-the-art discrete counterparts (masked dLMs and AR baselines), Nemotron-Labs-Sigma outperforms them on standard benchmarks after pretraining (e.g., GSM8K, Minerva, HumanEval, MBPP) and on challenging reasoning tasks after supervised fine-tuning (e.g., MATH-500, AIME), with the largest consistent gains on coding. Beyond accuracy, we exploit two structural properties unique to continuous dLMs. First, smooth trajectories degrade gracefully at low NFEs and enable efficient few-step distillation: distribution matching proves strongest among the methods we study, giving roughly 8x acceleration on math and 4x on coding (8 and 4 tokens per forward pass) with negligible accuracy loss and preserved diversity. In addition, the one-step speculative decoding on the (pre-SFT) distilled 8B model yields a further ~3.5x throughput gain over the AR counterpart (on GB200), outperforming masked dLM baselines. Second, embedding-space steering controls the quality--diversity trade-off, yielding strong pass@$k$ that keeps improving as $k$ grows. Together, these results establish continuous dLMs as a promising paradigm for efficient language generation.
  </details>

- **[Discovering Effective Agent Memory Designs through Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17068)**  `arXiv:2611.17068`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinheng Ding, Seyyed Saeid Cheshmi, Ziyang Zhang, Tairan Chen, Wenya Xie, Yu Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Program evolution provides a promising approach to discover agent memory designs and study the choices underlying their effectiveness. We introduce MAPE, a framework that evolves whole memory programs from minimal seeds through a standardized lifecycle interface and an iterative diagnosis-proposal loop. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval yield an absolute accuracy gain of up to 12% over manually designed baselines at 35% of the serving cost, with further evaluations supporting transfer across serving backbones and benchmarks. Empirical analyses and ablations identify and validate two recurring design patterns: retaining raw conversations as retrievable evidence preserves details omitted from extracted memories, while composing retrieved evidence according to query intent helps the answering model use it effectively. Transplanting the composition module into five existing memory systems improves accuracy across all five. Beyond these patterns, the search objective shapes where computation goes: optimizing serving cost alongside accuracy shifts computation from LLM-based memory construction to selective processing at query time while retaining competitive accuracy. Together, these findings offer empirical lessons for agent memory design. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Lara Mini Base: Language Diversity as a Resource, Not a Trade-off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16956)**  `arXiv:2611.16956`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Translated, Maria Pia Adinolfi, Isabelle Andrieu, Grazia Fiorella Anfuso, Melania Annunziata, Daniele Antonini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose language models are trained mostly on English: over open models we review, at most, non-English pretraining data makes up only a 12% share. Models built for European languages give them far more, but score well below general-purpose models of the same size. A common concern is that, in small models, languages compete for capacity. We present Lara Mini base, a model trained from scratch on 6T tokens in English and 44 other languages, which together cover all 24 official languages of the European Union. As training progresses, the 44 other languages together come to outweigh English. To give these languages enough capacity at low cost, Lara Mini base uses a mixture of experts architecture with 7.2B total and 0.6B active parameters. Among open base models with up to 2B active parameters, it achieves the highest average score across standard English, multilingual, math, and code benchmarks, ahead of models trained on 4 to 6 times as many tokens. Its margin is largest on multilingual benchmarks in the languages it supports and on math benchmarks. It supports contexts of up to 128k tokens. We find that our MoE architecture reduces capacity competition between languages. For a single user on one GPU, without quantization, it generates text at least 1.8 times faster than any model with a higher average score. We release the weights of Lara Mini base under the CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 license. In addition, we will release T-HellaSwag, a higher-quality machine translation of the commonsense reasoning benchmark HellaSwag into 110 language variants.
  </details>

- **[SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16929)**  `arXiv:2611.16929`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alex Jiang, Didier Schwab, Chuyuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.
  </details>

- **[MuSeToM: Multi-Sensory Evaluation of Belief Revision with Textual Cues Across Six Senses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16662)**  `arXiv:2611.16662`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunkit Chan, Kawai Chung, Sze Heng Douglas Kwok, Yuxuan Liu, Yauwai Yim, Tianshi Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to infer mental states in others, is pivotal for human social cognition. Existing evaluations of ToM in large language models (LLMs) mainly focus on static belief attribution and false-belief reasoning, leaving a critical aspect of social reasoning underexplored: can LLMs decide whether perceptual evidence is sufficient to justify another person's belief revision? To address this gap, we present MuSeToM, a human-validated, text-based benchmark for evaluating epistemic belief revision in ToM reasoning. MuSeToM contains 2,415 instances adapted from BigToM and OpenToM, with textual cues spanning six sensory categories, and tests whether models can determine when a protagonist should revise a belief and infer the resulting updated belief state. Using MuSeToM, we systematically evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs under direct and chain-of-thought prompting, revealing a pronounced dissonance: while models often detect that a belief may change, they struggle to identify the updated belief and frequently over-update on perceptually salient but epistemically insufficient cues.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Compressing Everything: Contrastive Memory Learning for Selective Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16384)**  `arXiv:2611.16384`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sikui Zhang, Haowei Liu, Ziyun Gan, Chunfeng Yuan, Guangze Gao, Nian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) advance toward increasingly long inputs, soft context compression offers a promising approach to efficient inference by converting long inputs into compact memory slots. Under limited memory budgets, effective context compression remains challenging because task-relevant evidence varies substantially across queries, ranging from localized evidence to multiple distant spans or information dispersed throughout the full document. Instead of compressing everything indiscriminately, we propose CoMem, a query-guided selective context compression framework that learns memory representations conditioned on both the document and the query. CoMem develops Contrastive Compressor Learning to achieve query-sensitive yet context-preserving memory representations. To inject a strong prior over query-dependent evidence distributions, we devise a same-context hard negative mining strategy by pairing a single document with multiple queries, requiring the compressor to selectively retain information according to each query's distinct evidence needs. To address the restricted information flow imposed by causal masking, we further enable bidirectional attention among memory slots. Experiments demonstrate that CoMem consistently outperforms existing context compression methods across compression ratios on question answering and RULER-NIAH benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Label-Efficient Document-Grounded Factuality Detection via Cross-Paraphrastic Invariance Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16264)**  `arXiv:2611.16264`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Chen, Shanshan Lin, Dongsheng Hong, Sibo Ju, Wenjie Yang, Sihong Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent claims that are unsupported by a given source document, motivating accurate and efficient document-grounded factuality detection. Existing LLM-based evaluators often require repeated inference and incur high computational cost, whereas compact factuality detectors are efficient at inference time but commonly rely on large labeled or synthetically generated corpora. We propose CPIL (Cross-Paraphrastic Invariance Learning), a two-stage Siamese framework that converts a small labeled set into relational supervision without acquiring additional factuality labels. CPIL constructs positive pairs from distinct label-preserving paraphrastic views of the same document-claim instance and negative pairs from opposite-label examples associated with the same source document. It further introduces quantile-aware semi-hard negative mining, which derives a boundary from the positive-pair similarity distribution and balances informative negatives with broader coverage. In the two-stage training procedure, Stage 1 learns an encoder from these task-aware pairs, and Stage 2 jointly fine-tunes the encoder and a linear classification head for binary factuality prediction. At inference time, CPIL requires no paraphrase generation, pair construction, or Siamese comparison and uses only one encoder-classifier forward pass. On the 11-task LLM-AggreFact benchmark, CPIL-2% and CPIL-3% achieve macro-F1 scores of 81.04 and 81.60 while using only 2-3% of the FactCG-released labeled pool. Both settings outperform all compared compact detectors and two vanilla LLM references. Ablations and sensitivity analyses support the contributions of task-aware pair construction, dynamic negative mining, and multi-pivot paraphrasing.
  </details>

- **[Indic-Task Gains and Regressions after Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16228)**  `arXiv:2611.16228`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sutanshu Saksena Raj, Daksh Maan, Avinash Benki, Parijat Chatterjee, Pasunuti Prasanjith, Purusharth Malik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We run reinforcement learning (RL) with GRPO across four environments, starting from a supervised fine-tuned 30B-A3B mixture-of-experts model covering English and eight Indic languages. We compare the model before and after RL across 34 benchmarks in one evaluation campaign. MATH improves by 36.07 points, and most Indic benchmarks also improve. Yet XQuAD-Indic F1 falls 19.32 points as English answers rise from 8.7% to 91.7%; IndicQA falls 8.64, while English XQuAD gains 11.71 F1. A second RL run from the same supervised initialization adds an Indic environment together with language and repetition rewards. Compared with the released model, it improves both passage-answering and instruction-following benchmarks, along with preserving MATH performance. But it loses ground on reasoning and coding benchmarks. Several training choices changed together, so we cannot isolate the effect of any one of the reward terms. Script compliance remains unresolved. Adding a language reward did not ensure use of the requested script. Passage answering and script compliance therefore need direct evaluation, even when other benchmark scores improve.
  </details>

- **[Data-Efficient LLM Safety Alignment Guided by Interpretable Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16183)**  `arXiv:2611.16183`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyu Li, Zhan Yu, Zhe Zhao, Kimmo Yan, Xiong Kuang, Renfen Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing large language models typically rely on massive fine-tuning datasets for safety alignment, which results in higher costs for safety alignment and often leads to the problem of over-refusal. We propose Less is Safer (LIS), a compact yet highly effective dataset for safety alignment, constructed through interpretable feature-guided selection and adversarial synthesis. Specifically, we leverage Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) activations to efficiently select prompts that cover diverse and interpretable safety features. Then, we employ a multi-agent adversarial synthesis framework to generate harmless prompts that nevertheless elicit unnecessary refusals. LIS contains only 2.4K samples, yet outperforms much larger datasets, e.g., TRIDENT-Core (26K), TRIDENT-Edge (19K) and WildJailbreak (262K), in overall performance on four safety and five over-refusal benchmarks. Through ablation experiments, we demonstrate that neither safety data nor over-refusal data alone can achieve optimal results. We also used safety features from SAE, annotated by an LLM, for data filtering, but the results were inferior to those obtained using the pipeline proposed in this paper.
  </details>

- **[Show Your Work in Swahili: Reinforcement Learning for Native-Language Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16104)**  `arXiv:2611.16104`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jessica Lundin, Katherine Rosenfeld, David Adelani, Geoffrey Mosoti Nyakiongora, Halfan Ngowo, Sloan Coffin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Fikiria, a two-stage SFT+GRPO recipe for Swahili mathematical reasoning. Its reward multiplies a format gate by a weighted sum of answer accuracy and the ChrF\texttt{++} similarity of the Swahili reasoning to an in-language reference chain of thought, so that training targets the surface fidelity of the reasoning trace and not only the final answer. We train on a 2.3k-problem Swahili dataset created by translating GSM8K with GPT-4.1 and filtering with SSA-COMET. On AfriMGSM Swahili, Fikiria-AfriqueQwen-14B reaches 79.2\%. Under the same fixed prompt and strict answer-format scoring, GPT-4o reaches 68.0\%, partly because it often fails the required answer format; IrokoBench reports 78.8\% for GPT-4o using a best-per-model prompt, a figure numerically close to ours but not directly comparable. In a small human evaluation with co-author annotators, GPT-4o's outputs were preferred overall. ChrF\texttt{++} measures surface fidelity, not the validity of individual reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Outside the Purview of the Reward: Capability Drift in Multilingual Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15717)**  `arXiv:2611.15717`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sutanshu Saksena Raj, Purusharth Malik, Pasunuti Prasanjith, Daksh Maan, Parijat Chatterjee, Avinash Benki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training can improve rewarded behavior while weakening capabilities users still need. We study two GRPO branches from a shared supervised checkpoint in a multilingual mixture-of-experts model. The released branch gains 36.07 points on MATH but loses 19.32 F1 points on XQuAD-Indic, while XQuAD-English improves under the same scoring rule. An independently trained sibling adds Indic instruction following and language-aware shaping. It recovers the regressing Indic task scores and preserves MATH, but does not restore script compliance and trades off competition mathematics and code. In a later round, judge training is followed by a sharp extraction decline from the Indic branch; reward-free distillation from its earlier checkpoint restores most of the lost F1. A MATH score decomposition associates most of the measured gain with increased boxed-answer emission; accuracy among boxed answers changes little. These results show why benchmark scores should be read alongside the response forms they reward.
  </details>

- **[Faithful in Steps: Improving Generalization and Citation in RAG via Query Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15538)**  `arXiv:2611.15538`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Liu, Zhongying Ru, Shimin Di, Jipeng Zhang, Ruiyuan Zhang, Xiaofang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augment generation is a prevalent strategy to mitigate hallucinations of LLMs. The attributable RAG (RAGQ) generates quotes for its answers. The quotes indicate which input contexts support the RAG to derive the answers, enhancing the answer's verifiability and trustworthiness. However, existing RAGQs exhibit significant degradation when dealing with questions that require multi-hop reasoning and multi-modal understanding, suffering from over-citation, implicit entity identification failure, and poor generalization. In this paper, we propose a novel RAGQ framework, namely QDRAG. QDRAG breaks down the input question into atomic subquestions to identify the implicit entities. Then, the reranker prunes context distractors to eliminate the downstream over-citation. To facilitate query decomposition, we propose two zero-shot approaches: QD-C and QD-R, which guide the QD MLLM to decompose the question based on context knowledge and retrieval rewards, respectively. One interesting finding is that finetuning on the QD task shows better generalizability compared to directly finetuning on the downstream RAGQ task. Experiments on four multi-modal QA benchmarks demonstrate QDRAG's efficacy in grounding answers and generating faithful citations. The framework significantly outperforms all the baselines on both in-domain and out-of-domain tests, even surpassing Gemini-Pro.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Multilingual Document Understanding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14758)**  `arXiv:2611.14758`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Matteo Gabburo, Daniela Baiamonte, Stefano Simonazzi, Silvia Gardini, Sean Cesare Lazzeri, Andrea Zugarini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) perform strongly on multimodal tasks, Document Understanding (DU) among them, but the benchmarks that measure them mostly cover English documents and narrow problems. How well current VLMs generalize across languages, tasks, styles and layouts therefore remains unclear. We introduce MDU, a multilingual DU benchmark of 23146 examples spanning 13 tasks and five languages: English, French, German, Italian, and Spanish. The tasks isolate complementary capabilities, from surface-level text and Markdown extraction through summarization, classification and named-entity recognition to layout-sensitive passage and layout extraction. We evaluate 45 state-of-the-art open-weight VLMs from 1.7B to 235B parameters, plus one commercial system under the same protocol. The benchmark is far from saturated: the best average is 69.0, only eight of the 45 evaluated systems reach 65, and the two best results come from 27B models rather than from the largest systems we evaluate. Performance is uneven in ways that scale does not fix. The gap between the strongest and weakest language does not close as models grow, and it survives controlling for the domain composition of each language. Capabilities also do not improve together: once we partial out overall model quality, per-task scores are largely uncorrelated, and transcription ability in particular trades off against semantic extraction.
  </details>

- **[MITRA-Swarm: Injecting Explicit Linguistic Structure into Foundation Models for Classical Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14675)**  `arXiv:2611.14675`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Kayshav Bhardwaj, Pragun Seth, Kush Bhardwaj, Vijay Nagasamy, Kurt Keutzer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical Sanskrit poses three challenges unresolved by single-call monolithic models: morphophonemic fusion Sandhi, dense nominal compounding Samāsa, and discourse-level zero-anaphora forced by poetic meter. We present MITRA-Swarm, a multi-agent architecture injecting explicit linguistic structure into an otherwise monolithic pipeline: a Sandhi-splitting agent, a parallel array of generalist gemma-4-26B-A4B-it and domain-specialized gemma-2-mitra-it translators, and a discourse-aware Ellipsis Restorer that consults corpus-derived register tags to avoid inventing active-voice subjects in impersonal legal and philosophical registers. Two separated judges evaluate quality: a routing judge ($J_{\text{route}}$, same model family as the generators) driving an internal critique loop, and an independent reporting judge ($J_{\text{report}}=$gpt-4o-mini, no shared vendor lineage) scoring every output exactly once. On a stratified benchmark of 1,000 verses across five registers (23,990 scored verdicts, 12-condition pre-registered ablation), the full swarm reaches $85.90$ MQM against a monolithic baseline of $85.27$ ($\Delta=+0.63$, $p_{\text{adj}}=0.0018$, 95% CI $[+0.30,+0.97]$), and isolated discourse restoration alone accounts for $+0.44$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$), outperforming a call-count-matched self-refinement baseline by $+0.51$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$). Severe per-verse failures (MQM$<80$) fall from $9.0\%$ to $7.6\%$, at roughly $9\times$ the per-verse inference cost of the baseline. Additionally, $J_{\text{route}}$ scores its own family's outputs $2$-$7$ MQM higher than $J_{\text{report}}$ on every one of the 12 conditions, empirically motivating the two-judge split; and a preflight screen of a 192,151-entry retrieval index against all 12 evaluation works verifies 0 reference leaks across 269 runtime checks. These are small gains on a metric saturating near 85-86.
  </details>

- **[An AI Assistant for Automating Anti-Corruption Expertise of Draft Legislation Based on the TRACE Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14244)**  `arXiv:2611.14244`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Serhii Zabolotnii, Alina Tarasova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an AI assistant for automating anti-corruption expertise of draft legislation based on the TRACE architecture, designed to screen bills for corruption risks: pinpoint provisions an expert would criticise and name the regulatory defect in each. The pipeline combines a deterministic parsing core, model-guided single-provision qualification, rule-based factor assignment, and external human oversight. To evaluate the assistant, we constructed an open benchmark from the published conclusions of Ukraine's National Agency on Corruption Prevention (NAZK), comprising 132 draft laws with 474 model-extracted findings. A system flag agrees with a finding when it targets a compatible provision and an LLM judge rates the risk as the same (strict) or partly the same (lenient). On 100 draft laws, the pipeline matches 0.28 of the findings within its top ten flags using the local Gemma 4 31B model, 0.30 with Sonnet 5.5, and 0.43-0.46 across all flags (non-blind; the frozen blind baseline achieves 0.21, 95% CI 0.16-0.26). Across all runs, most misses occur on provisions that the system located but qualified as a different risk, and none of the 32 legal-collision findings was matched. On 21 acts held out from tuning, the pipeline outperforms a monolithic prompt containing the full methodology under the strict measure, though not under the lenient one, and Opus 5.5 adds nothing over Sonnet. The reported scores reflect agreement with model-extracted findings rated by an LLM judge whose two access routes agree on 73 of 74 labels, without human expert validation.
  </details>

- **[Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14019)**  `arXiv:2611.14019`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahao Wang, Yuqi Xiong, Qing Yang, Zhipeng Xu, Zhenghao Liu, Zulong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills have emerged as an effective means of enabling LLM agents to solve complex, interactive tasks by encoding reusable knowledge without modifying model parameters. Recent approaches further improve skill adaptation by distilling reusable experience from agent execution trajectories and evolving skills based on downstream task feedback. However, existing methods primarily represent skills as natural language documents, intertwining procedural logic with descriptive knowledge and making skills difficult to maintain and evolve. To address this limitation, we propose PASE (Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution), a program-aided framework that iteratively evolves skills by associating them with executable programs. Unlike prior approaches that encode trajectory-derived experience primarily in natural language, PASE separates reusable procedural logic into editable programs, enabling targeted updates to executable components while reducing the need to repeatedly revise natural language skills. Extensive experiments across diverse models and benchmarks demonstrate that PASE consistently outperforms all baselines, by achieving over 2.8% improvements. Further analyses show that PASE exhibits greater evolutionary stability and generalisation capability, highlighting the critical role of programmatic knowledge in skill evolution. We will release all data and code via GitHub.
  </details>

- **[ScrapeBench: Evaluating Legal Compliance of AI Agents in Website Scraping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11306)**  `arXiv:2611.11306`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Joseph Marvin Imperial, Daniel Slate, Noam Kolt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents deployed on the open web are becoming more capable of performing actions that may breach contractual provisions and violate computer security laws. In this paper, we introduce ScrapeBench, the first agentic benchmark for empirically evaluating the legal compliance of AI agents with website scraping policies, namely terms of service (ToS) and robots$.$txt directives. Across widely used closed-weight and open-weight AI agents, we find that most agents exhibit low compliance rates (below 5%) when instructed to scrape websites in our sample of 1,626 popular websites that explicitly prohibit scraping. In addition, we observe that even where AI agents actively check the applicable ToS and robots$.$txt, they continue to exhibit low compliance rates. Conversely, in our sample of 269 websites that do not prohibit scraping, we observe that agents sometimes refuse to scrape despite it being lawful, such as Claude Opus 4.6 exhibiting an over-refusal rate of 98.5%. Our human baseline experiments (n = 180 participants) suggest that humans exhibit compliance rates comparable to AI agents but over-refuse at much higher rates.
  </details>

- **[Jais 3: Introducing Arabic-centric Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10224)**  `arXiv:2611.10224`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Team IFM, Haonan Li, Hadi Abdine, Nikhil Ranjan, Mohamed Anwar, Ahmad Chamma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Jais 3, a fleet of six open, Arabic-centric agentic language models ranging from 0.9B to 375B parameters. Jais 3 is built to complete tasks for Arabic speakers, not only to answer questions in plain text: it is trained to plan multi-step tasks, call tools and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, operate a terminal, search the web, and read, edit, and produce PDF, spreadsheet, and presentation files, while communicating with the user in Arabic. Instead of pretraining from scratch, we adapt each model of the fully open K2 Horizon fleet to Arabic through continual pretraining, followed by supervised fine-tuning (SFT), final token preference optimization (FTPO), and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We combine natural Arabic text with translated, transliterated, and synthetic Arabic, and replay the base model's own training data to keep its reasoning, agentic, and English abilities. Jais 3 covers Modern Standard Arabic and the major regional dialects, follows instructions with Arabic-specific constraints, and inherits the 512K-token context window of K2 Horizon (all models except the 0.9B). To our knowledge, its 375B model is the largest open Arabic-centric language model to date. For evaluation, we revisit how Arabic models are assessed, moving to fully generative evaluation on audited Arabic benchmarks. Across model sizes, Jais 3 matches or outperforms open-weight models of comparable size on Arabic and agentic benchmarks, with particularly strong agentic capabilities and Arabic instruction following, and far fewer hallucinations. We release the weights and the checkpoints from every training stage of all six models, together with samples of the training data, the data recipes and training configurations, the training logs, the audited benchmarks, and the evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[A Scaled-Up Empirical Study of Syntactic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16837)**  `arXiv:2610.16837`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Pengcheng Wang, Zhexuan Zhang, Chao Lou, Kewei Tu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Syntactic language models (SLMs) that generate sentences along with their syntactic tree structures have shown promise in both syntactic generalization and downstream tasks. Prior studies, however, have mostly evaluated smaller models trained on less pretraining data and tested on narrower task suites. In addition, the design space of tree linearization and attention masks remains underexplored. We conduct a large-scale empirical study of 12 controlled configurations---one terminal baseline and 11 SLM variants spanning different linearizations and masks---at scales up to 1B parameters and 100B training tokens, evaluated across 11 downstream tasks and two syntactic benchmarks. The experimental results show that SLMs with standard causal attention match or exceed structural-mask variants across downstream and syntactic benchmarks. They require no architectural modifications and remain compatible with FlashAttention. We also observe that when scaling up to the 1B-parameter scale, the top-performing SLM variant no longer shows a clear advantage over a compute-matched non-structural baseline, suggesting that SLMs may become less competitive with scale. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Failures Propagate: Causal Failure Attribution in Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20627)**  `arXiv:2608.20627`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Lauren Pothuru, Spurthi Setty, Natan Vidra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) interleaves retrieval, reasoning, and answer generation across multiple hops. A retrieval error at hop 1 can surface only as a wrong answer at hop 3, while later retrieval can also repair the trajectory. This paper introduces AgenticRAG-FP, an interventional benchmark for causal failure attribution in agentic RAG. The benchmark injects a certified fault at a specified hop, re-executes the downstream trajectory, and evaluates diagnosers against the known intervention site. Its central question is whether a post-hoc trace still identifies the injected hop after the suffix changes. In the dense Claude Haiku 4.5 structural sweep on three-hop MuSiQue questions, coverage-based diagnosis is 0.91 at hop 1 and 0.00 at hops 2 and 3 (n=43,36,21 failed trajectories), but it predicts hop 1 in 96% of failures, an earliest-hop rule; at hops 2 and 3, an LLM judge and frozen-hop repair localize only 0.25 to 0.48 of failures. In larger content-corruption runs, depth-2 exact-hop accuracy is 0.00 for coverage, 0.49 for an LLM judge, 0.59 for frozen-hop repair, and 0.14 for suffix regeneration (n=51); the judge and frozen-hop repair do not significantly differ (exact McNemar p=0.33).
  </details>

- **[Prompt-Activation Duality: Improving Activation Steering via Attention-Level Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10664)**  `arXiv:2605.10664`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Diancheng Kang, Zheyuan Liu, Ningshan Ma, Yue Huang, Zhaoxuan Tan, Meng Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering controls language model behavior by adding directions to internal representations at inference time, but standard residual-stream steering can fail in stateful dialogue. We identify KV-cache contamination as a key failure mode: steered token states are stored and repeatedly reused, turning a local perturbation into cumulative coherence degradation. To address this challenge, we propose Gated Cropped Attention-Delta steering (GCAD), which extracts steering signals from system-prompt contributions to self-attention and applies them with token-level gating. Across persona-steering experiments, GCAD preserves trait control while substantially improving long-horizon coherence. On the main multi-turn benchmark, GCAD improves average coherence drift from -18.6 to -1.9 and raises turn-10 trait expression from 78.0 to 93.1. These results suggest that activation steering becomes more reliable when interventions follow the prompt-mediated pathways that models already use for behavioral control.
  </details>

- **[SLIDERS: Evidence Synthesis with Autonomous Database Reconciliation for Systematic Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22294)**  `arXiv:2604.22294`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Harshit Joshi, Priyank Shethia, Jadelynn Dao, Monica S. Lam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systematic reviews requires comprehensive evidence synthesis from large document corpora to answer targeted research questions and are foundational in finance, social sciences, and other technical fields. Manual construction of evidence tables is labor-intensive, and recent LLM-based assistants relying on embedding or keyword search often fail to meet the coverage standards of systematic reviews. We introduce SLIDERS, a novel LLM-based methodology for automatically assembling evidence tables tailored to research questions for systematic reviews. SLIDERS extracts full-text excerpts that serve as direct evidence or as provenance for structured data. Core to SLIDERS is an autonomous evidence reconciliation coding agent that iteratively writes SQL to analyze and reconcile extracted evidence, bringing together information fragmented across documents, resolving inconsistencies and removing duplicates across records. We evaluate SLIDERS on BrowseComp+, achieving 90% accuracy, and show that it outperforms all baselines on additional benchmarks within the LLM's context window. To assess scalability, we introduce two new evidence-synthesis benchmarks, WikiCeleb100 (3.9M tokens) and FinQ100 (36M tokens), which include follow-up questions beyond the original research query. Although SLIDERS synthesizes evidence knowing only the original question, it answers 78.9% and 55.2% of all questions correctly on WikiCeleb100 and FinQ100, respectively
  </details>

- **[LongReasonArena: A Long Reasoning Benchmark for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19363)**  `arXiv:2508.19363`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayu Ding, Shuming Ma, Lei Cui, Nanning Zheng, Furu Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing long-context benchmarks for Large Language Models (LLMs) focus on evaluating comprehension of long inputs, while overlooking the evaluation of long reasoning abilities. To address this gap, we introduce LongReasonArena, a benchmark specifically designed to assess the long reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Our tasks require models to solve problems by executing multi-step algorithms that reflect key aspects of long reasoning, such as retrieval and backtracking. By controlling the inputs, the required reasoning length can be arbitrarily scaled, reaching up to 1 million tokens of reasoning for the most challenging tasks. Extensive evaluation results demonstrate that LongReasonArena presents a significant challenge for both open-source and proprietary LLMs. For instance, even GPT-5 achieves only 27.8% accuracy on Level 3. Further analysis also reveals that the accuracy exhibits a linear decline with respect to the logarithm of the expected number of reasoning steps. Our code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MoralBench: Moral Evaluation of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04428)**  `arXiv:2406.04428`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianchao Ji, Yutong Chen, Mingyu Jin, Wujiang Xu, Wenyue Hua, Yongfeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the rapidly evolving field of artificial intelligence, large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for a myriad of applications, from natural language processing to decision-making support systems. However, as these models become increasingly integrated into societal frameworks, the imperative to ensure they operate within ethical and moral boundaries has never been more critical. This paper introduces a novel benchmark designed to measure and compare the moral reasoning capabilities of LLMs. We present the first comprehensive dataset specifically curated to probe the moral dimensions of LLM outputs, addressing a wide range of ethical dilemmas and scenarios reflective of real-world complexities.The main contribution of this work lies in the development of benchmark datasets and metrics for assessing the moral identity of LLMs, which accounts for nuance, contextual sensitivity, and alignment with human ethical standards. Our methodology involves a multi-faceted approach, combining quantitative analysis with qualitative insights from ethics scholars to ensure a thorough evaluation of model performance. By applying our benchmark across several leading LLMs, we uncover significant variations in moral reasoning capabilities of different models. These findings highlight the importance of considering moral reasoning in the development and evaluation of LLMs, as well as the need for ongoing research to address the biases and limitations uncovered in our study. We publicly release the benchmark atthis https URLand also open-source the code of the project atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning When Diffusion Language Models Should Regenerate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17320)**  `arXiv:2611.17320`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minhae Oh, Jeonghye Kim, Jinwoo Koo, Yeongoon Kim, Jimin Seo, Minsik Seol, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remasking allows diffusion language models to revisit generated text, but effective correction requires knowing when and what to revise. Our diagnosis on Sudoku reveals two gaps. Hidden states contain error information that token confidence does not reliably expose, and remasking an isolated error usually reproduces it when the surrounding context remains fixed. These findings motivate DiVR , a verifier that predicts whether a partially generated sequence will reach a correct answer. Trained on the final correctness of the model's own trajectories, DiVR decides when to regenerate a block and which alternative to retain, while the generator itself stays unchanged. On five reasoning benchmarks with LLaDA-8B-Base, DiVR raises the average accuracy of the fine-tuned generator from 35.3\% to 43.4\% at $1.9$-$3.5\times$ the cost of greedy decoding, above search baselines that use $3.8$-$7.4\times$. It also complements post-training such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and generalizes to other generators, including Dream.
  </details>

- **[Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16843)**  `arXiv:2611.16843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.
  </details>

- **[EvenVLM: A Text and Multimodal Dataset for the Even Language with Vision-Language Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16500)**  `arXiv:2611.16500`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniil Makovetskii, Ivan Bondarenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present EvenVLM, the first text and multimodal (VQA) dataset for Even (Lamut), an endangered Northern Tungusic language spoken by approximately 6,800 people in Siberia. Our dataset comprises 50,521 structured Even-Russian entries extracted from dictionary and phrasebook sources via GPT-4o Mini-based parsing, from which we synthetically generate 107,543 training examples spanning translation, grammar, reading comprehension, and semantic classification. To address tokenization inefficiency on Even's agglutinative morphology, we propose a three-step tokenizer expansion algorithm that adds Even-specific graphemes (eng, oe, o-umlaut), whitelisted vocabulary, and BPE merges, reducing average tokens per word by 27% (4.99 to 3.64). Using LoRA fine-tuning of Qwen3-VL-4B, we adapt the model for text tasks (0.53% trainable parameters, rank 32) and separately for visual question answering (rank 64, frozen vision encoder). Our best configuration achieves 11.4% exact match and a 13.6 weighted score on text benchmarks, 0.1283 chrF for sentence translation, and 65% accuracy on VQA (up from 0% for the base model and text-only fine-tuning). We release our datasets, evaluation benchmarks, and training pipeline to support further research on Even and other low-resource languages.
  </details>

- **[daVinci-Prover: Agent-Native Mid-Training for Formal Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16433)**  `arXiv:2611.16433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenhong Zhu, Xuefeng Li, Jiashuo Zhang, Yu Chen, Tiantian Mi, Qipeng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in large language models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming formal theorem proving. Yet further progress remains constrained by the lack of a transparent and effective data recipe for systematically improving theorem-proving models. In this work, we argue that effective agentic provers require strong mathematical skill priors to ground theorem proving and agentic trajectories to enable long-horizon, interactive proof construction. To this end, we synthesize an agent-native 33.7B-token mid-training corpus covering decomposition, search, self-verification, and revision, together with verifier-grounded agentic trajectories. We further introduce the daVinci-Prover harness, which enables models to construct proofs and interact with a Lean verifier in an isolated workspace, and use it to collect approximately 36K high-quality agent trajectories for supervised fine-tuning. Our mid-training recipe yields an average improvement of 13.7 points over the matched Qwen3-8B SFT-only baseline across MiniF2F, FATE-M, and ProofNet. When applied to the Qwen3.5 series, our approach also yields strong performance across theorem-proving benchmarks and achieves near-saturating MiniF2F performance with memory compaction. The results highlight agent-native mid-training as an effective foundation for developing stronger interactive theorem-proving agents.
  </details>

- **[From Large Language Models to Efficient System One Models via Adaptive Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16321)**  `arXiv:2611.16321`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minh-Anh Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transferring LLMs into System One decision models typically replaces the language modeling head with a decision head while retaining the full backbone, making every decision pay for all layers. Yet it remains unclear how much depth such decisions require. We find that decision information is concentrated in intermediate layers, with early layers building essential features and the final six layers removable without loss, while independently trained smaller models largely mimic the full model rather than developing specialized decision capabilities. These findings suggest that efficiency requires not simply smaller backbones, but shared computation across different depths. Building on this, we propose \method{}, which jointly trains a full model with prefix sub-models that share its final layers and decision head through self-distillation. A lightweight gate reads the hidden state at each exit to determine whether additional computation is needed, allowing one model to operate at multiple layers. Across seven decision benchmarks, our approach matches or outperforms independently trained models at every depth, while achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs through adaptive computation.
  </details>

- **[Low Language Transfer Speech-to-Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16260)**  `arXiv:2611.16260`  `cs.CL`  
  _Husein Zolkepli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A multilingual speech recogniser has heard much speech in its own languages. We ask how much of that it brings to a language it was never trained on, when it is fine-tuned on the little that language has. We score 28 open multilingual models untouched on Low-Language-TTS, 50 low-resource languages in 11 writing systems, and fine-tune 24 of them on at most 1,000 clips a language, 82 hours in all, with a dev set choosing every epoch. Fine-tuning lowers every model's macro character error rate (CER), by 16.6 to 53.1 points, to 9.5 at best, and a word language model takes three CTC models 1.2 to 1.4 points lower; WER is still 34.4 at best. On the 12 languages that no model lists, every model starts at 46 or worse and the best ends at 11.7. The same architectures from random weights stay 60 points or more behind, so the gain is transfer, and an encoder left untouched already gives a Whisper or Qwen decoder most of what it needs. It is the language, not the corpus: on FLEURS recordings that ours do not share, the median model keeps 79% of its gain. It costs the languages the models knew, 5.7 points of CER on English, Spanish, French and German in the median model. Low-rank adapters on 4% of the weights come within 1.1 points of tuning everything, and on 2 of 3 models forget less. Seq2seq and language-model decoders are done within three to five epochs, while a new CTC head is still improving at twenty or thirty. Data beats epochs: 25 clips a language take Qwen3-ASR 0.6B from 60.9 to 39.3, and at equal clips presented, ten times the data is worth 9.7 points. A model that first learned forty of the languages learns the other ten no faster. On a public Malay benchmark, untouched Whisper large-v3 scores 8.9 when it detects the language and 14.9 when told the clip is Malay, because it then translates the English that speakers mix in; trained on the Scicom telephony dataset it scores 6.0.
  </details>

- **[We Are What We Remember: Mental-State-Driven Memory for Long-Term Character Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16223)**  `arXiv:2611.16223`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhicheng Jia, Shuchen Wu, Zhuoshi Tong, Haisen Liao, Sicheng Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Shared history in human dialogue is not merely a repository of facts, but the foundation of evolving relationships and mutual understanding. However, mainstream conversational memory systems rely on passive semantic similarity over external fact stores, frequently retrieving topically similar records while missing relationally pivotal experiences. Inspired by cognitive psychology's Self-Memory System (SMS), we propose MindMem, a mental-state-driven memory framework that asynchronously consolidates shared experiences into source-linked mental states of the character, the user, and their relationship. At each turn, a specialized mental-state reasoner (Mind) contextualizes these persistent states to anticipate latent user needs, form proactive intentions, and recover supporting episodic evidence that surface-level matching overlooks. To evaluate history-sensitive adaptation beyond factual recall, we introduce MemReal-Bench, a longitudinal benchmark grounded in month-long real-user interactions across dyadic and group scenes. By pairing Direct Question Answering (DQA) with Situated Memory Realization (SMR), it tests whether agents bridge the gap between knowing past facts and naturally using shared history, world rules, and relational trajectories. On MemReal-Bench, MindMem reaches 59.03% SMR and 82.58% DQA, exceeding the strongest memory baseline by 24.19 and 9.19 percentage points, respectively, and outperforming oracle target-memory injection on SMR. Code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can Agents Relive Past Experiences? Diagnosing Event-Affect Decoupling in Affective-Episodic Retrospection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15655)**  `arXiv:2611.15655`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tingna Xie, Ming Wang, Shi Feng, Yongkang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As memory-augmented agents increasingly support long-term and personalized interactions, reliable recall of emotional experiences while preserving their associated affect becomes necessary. However, existing evaluations usually assess emotion accuracy and factual memory grounding separately, overlooking event-affect correspondence. In this work, we formulate Affective-Episodic Retrospection (AER) as a task that explicitly evaluates the recovery of past events, associated affects, and their correspondence, and define Event-Affect Decoupling as a failure mode where the correct emotion is recalled but associated with the wrong episode. To diagnose Event-Affect Decoupling, we propose EM-TRACE, a stage-wise framework that decomposes AER into retrieval, affect recovery, and realization. On a controlled GoEmotions-based benchmark, Emotion-only retrieval achieves 28.65% affect recovery but retrieves the target episode for only 4.44% of queries, yielding a UAR of 24.21%. Further analysis reveals that event retrieval and affect assignment introduce distinct binding failures. These findings show that reliable AER requires grounded recall of emotional experiences, where episode retrieval, affect recovery, event-affect binding, and generation quality should be evaluated jointly. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ChJGBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Chinese Legal Judgment Generation with Diagnostic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15639)**  `arXiv:2611.15639`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yinghao Hu, Linggang Kong, Qian Xiao, Yuting Huang, Yunlong Zhang, Yujie Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid development of LLMs, increasing attention has been paid to whether they can reliably generate complete and legally valid judicial documents. This calls for a benchmark capable of evaluating whether models can accurately apply statutes and resolve outcome-critical issues, including claims, charges, penalties, and monetary obligations. However, existing benchmarks typically focus on isolated legal tasks or coarse-grained evaluation. We introduce ChJGBench, a benchmark for generating the reasoning and dispositive sections of Chinese judgments across civil, criminal, and administrative cases. Built from 3,462 real-world judgments, ChJGBench provides a case-type-aware diagnostic evaluation framework comprising 13 metric types and adopts a hybrid paradigm. Experiments reveal no uniform advantage for legal-domain models over general-purpose models, with model strengths varying across diagnostic dimensions. Performance is generally stronger on criminal cases, while civil and administrative cases continue to present substantial challenges.
  </details>

- **[Can We Make an LLM Dumber, or Is It Just Playing Dumb? An Empirical Study of Believable Performance Throttling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15568)**  `arXiv:2611.15568`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chengguang Gan, Yunhao Liang, Hanjun Wei, QingHao Zhang, Shiwen Ni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A benchmark score is commonly read as a proxy for model capability. We study the inverse problem: can a model be driven to a target score, and do its failures resemble those of a naturally weaker model? We operationalize believable performance reduction along three axes: target calibration, item-difficulty structure, and error-distribution similarity to a real weak reference. We introduce CapLadder, an IRT-calibrated suite of 1,985 items spanning mathematics, code, multiple-choice reasoning, and tool use. A simple weak-teacher LoRA dial reaches unseen target levels on reasoning with a 1.9-point held-out calibration error, whereas prompting, temperature, and weight noise cannot combine comparable range and precision. Yet on multiple choice, the dial converges to an answer-token attractor: up to 97% of its newly introduced errors land on one letter, with the attractor changing from D in Qwen to A in Llama. Logit interventions identify a dominant near-constant shift: subtracting the estimated shift on held-out items recovers 52 to 59% of lost accuracy and removes the dominant attractor, while option permutation confirms that the bias tracks token position rather than option content. With high inter-annotator agreement ($\kappa=0.94$), human-coded error-category distributions nonetheless match the weak reference on reasoning (JS = 0.00), showing that local plausibility and aggregate detectability differ. Structured tool calls exhibit more natural degradation in the Qwen family (JS = 0.09), while a globally calibrated dial transfers poorly across benchmarks (14.2-point error on two usable benchmarks). Precise score control, therefore, does not imply a portable or believable reduction in skill.
  </details>

- **[Towards Better Legal Reasoning LLMs: Signal Balancing and Reward Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15346)**  `arXiv:2611.15346`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yinghao Hu, Qian Xiao, Yuting Huang, Kaisong Song, Yiquan Wu, Ang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the legal domain, LLMs can improve reliability and interpretability by generating explicit reasoning traces. However, training LLMs to produce high-quality reasoning traces remains challenging. Existing supervised fine-tuning methods struggle when reasoning traces are significantly longer than final answers, as the learning signal for the answer becomes diluted. Meanwhile, reinforcement learning methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization also have several limitations, including costly reward design, performance plateaus, and reward hacking. We therefore propose a two-stage training framework. In Stage I, JurisCoT-SFT employs a length-normalized dual objective to balance learning signals between reasoning traces and final answers. In Stage II, Lifecycle-Aware Backtrackable Policy Optimization dynamically evaluates and retires auxiliary reward signals based on their impact on final evaluation objectives, enabling efficient reward utilization. Our framework substantially improves legal reasoning performance on relevant tasks selected from multiple legal benchmarks, including ChJGBench, LawBench, and LegalBench. Experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in both reasoning trace quality and final answer accuracy, while our models remain competitive with larger models.
  </details>

- **[FIBER: Bayesian Fusion of Fast-DetectGPT and IRT-Informed Detector Evidence for LLM-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15058)**  `arXiv:2611.15058`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Li, Guangsheng Bao, Zhipeng Chen, Guandong Zhang, Yanbin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting large language model (LLM)-generated text remains challenging under domain and generator shifts. Supervised detectors may generalize poorly, while zero-shot methods often rely on individual statistical signals without leveraging historical detector behavior. We propose FIBER (Fast-DetectGPT with IRT-informed Bayesian Evidence Recalibration), which integrates continuous Fast-DetectGPT scores with auxiliary detector outputs through Bayesian evidence fusion. Item response theory (IRT) models historical detector-correctness patterns, providing class-conditional detector-output likelihoods through marginalization over latent text representations. These likelihoods are combined with Fast-DetectGPT score densities and a historical class prior for prediction. When historical Fast-DetectGPT scores are unavailable, a variant called FIBER-Stream estimates class-specific score distributions from an unlabeled batch. On a 32,000-text multi-domain benchmark, FIBER achieves the highest accuracy (0.620) and improves mean AUC over Fast-DetectGPT (0.664 versus 0.600) under five-fold cross-validation. FIBER also consistently improves AUC across four individual domains. Under out-of-distribution transfer, FIBER-Stream achieved an AUC of 0.911, compared with 0.889 for FIBER and 0.922 for Fast-DetectGPT alone. These results demonstrate the potential of integrating complementary detector evidence and historical information to improve detection performance across heterogeneous settings.
  </details>

- **[ToolTurns: Benchmarking LLM Agents in Multi-Turn Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14925)**  `arXiv:2611.14925`  `cs.CL`  
  _Milad Gholami, Brendan King, Jeffrey Flanigan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language models are increasingly deployed in conversational settings, yet most large-scale tool-use benchmarks focus on isolated requests. We introduce ToolTurns, a benchmark of 2,620 problems spanning 1,827 tools and 11,004 API endpoints across 47 categories. Each dialogue begins with an intentionally underspecified request and continues through two context-dependent follow-ups, testing clarification, tool use, and retention of conversational context. We also introduce an open-source user simulator that reveals withheld context through dialogue and generates follow-up requests, with a checker--fixer pipeline for improving its turns. Across nine language models, the best agent resolves 69.6% of individual requests but completes only 39.8% of full dialogues. Under this evaluation protocol, every evaluated model receives lower success scores on follow-up requests than on the initial request, and dialogue-quality rubric scores and task success can rank models very differently. Paired turn-level evaluation shows that the checker--fixer reduces detected dialogue defects, and blind human evaluation favors the revised turns. ToolTurns provides a testbed for evaluating whether agents sustain task success across multi-turn dialogue with tools.
  </details>

- **[Is Jev a Good Multilingual Judge?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14861)**  `arXiv:2611.14861`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wei Liu, Yi Fan, Xiyan Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative large language models are widely used for multilingual text evaluation, yet whether decision models that directly output judgment probabilities can serve as multilingual judges has received limited attention. We evaluate Jev against generative LLM judges on four multilingual benchmarks with human annotations, covering up to 47 language varieties and three evaluation formats: binary decisions, pairwise comparisons, and graded ratings. At approximately $1/31$ to $1/61$ of GPT-6.1's evaluation cost, Jev is competitive on most multilingual judging tasks, but falls behind GPT-6.1 on knowledge-intensive answer verification. Further analysis shows that Jev is more likely to accept incorrect answers in low-resource languages than in English and that its probability outputs help identify errors across tasks. Based on these findings, we offer recommendations on evaluation instructions, confidence-based selection, and model routing, providing empirical guidance for using Jev as a multilingual judge.
  </details>

- **[LLM Validation Makes Minimal-Pair Benchmarks Easier for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13788)**  `arXiv:2611.13788`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guofang Tang, Zheng Qu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Minimal-pair benchmarks are only as good as their labels, and LLMs are an obvious substitute for costly human validation. We test it on BLiMP (English) and QFrBLiMP (Quebec French), the two public minimal-pair datasets releasing every forced-choice human judgment. Three LLMs validate by forced choice, by rating, and as a panel, scored against human panels of one, three and five judges in two pre-registered phases. On BLiMP, forced-choice validators agree with held-out human majorities more often than five-judge panels, yet catch far fewer pairs whose label humans reject than one judge does. By point estimate, no validator is worth more than one judge. The errors are not random. On both datasets, across 10 open LMs, human-supported pairs that the pre-registered LLM filter flags are 18.9 points harder than those it accepts, in all 70 LM-validator combinations, and harder than pairs a single human judge flags. Filtering thus removes items on which LMs fail but humans do not, raising accuracy more than random removal. A protocol frozen before any output replicates the gap and the lift on Dutch BLiMP-NL. LLM validation makes minimal-pair benchmarks easier for the models they are meant to test; we release code, validator outputs and item-level scores.
  </details>

- **[OpticalRAG: Pixel-Space Compression for Token-Efficient Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13675)**  `arXiv:2611.13675`  `cs.CL`  
  _Senhao Liu, Yuheng Zhang, Chunyu Wei, Yueguo Chen, Xinran Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is bottlenecked by the token budget of large language models: feeding long documents into the context window is expensive, while aggressive textual compression discards fine-grained information that is irreversibly lost in a one-dimensional token sequence. We argue that this bottleneck is not fundamental to information density but to the choice of compression \emph{modality}. Modern visual encoders can pack the contents of a rendered text page into roughly a hundred visual tokens with little semantic loss, opening a new compression-fidelity tradeoff that purely textual methods cannot reach. We present \textbf{OpticalRAG}, a framework that renders documents as page images, encodes them with a frozen vision encoder, and distills the retrieved visual tokens into as few as $16$ injected tokens before passing them to the LLM. Two ingredients make this work without expensive cross-modal training: (i) \emph{Encoder-Bridged Retrieval}, which projects text-query states into the visual feature space of the frozen encoder, through a projection shared with the compressor, to retrieve visual tokens from a text query, with no contrastive training; and (ii) \emph{Query-Driven Distillation}, a lightweight cross-attention compressor that condenses the retrieved visual tokens into a small set of query-conditioned prefix tokens. On LongBench-v2, BAMBOO, and LooGLE-v2, OpticalRAG achieves the best compression-aware accuracy (CAP) on all three benchmarks while injecting only $16$ tokens, and improves the closed-book Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone by $5.17$ points on LongBench-v2. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[YRC-Bench: Benchmarking Culturally Aware Machine Translation for Historical Heritage via Tri-Level Semantic Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10555)**  `arXiv:2611.10555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Huang, Aobo Li, Jiayi Wang, Huidong Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translating historical cultural heritage poses profound challenges for machine translation systems due to the acute semantic asymmetry across eras and cultures, where terse classical syntax frequently obscures domain-specific cultural knowledge. While Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable prowess in generic translation benchmarks, they struggle to negotiate Culturally-Specific Items and diachronic shifts in domain-rich historical texts, such as Yellow River cultural records. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce YRC-Bench, the first human-curated, multi-parallel benchmark dedicated to historical heritage translation across Classical Chinese, Modern Chinese, and nine target foreign languages. YRC-Bench establishes a tri-level parallel corpus incorporating modern explanatory glosses as explicit semantic bridges and operationalizes translation strategies through paired conserved versus substituted CSI variants categorized by cultural translatability. Systematic evaluations of nine leading LLMs across bidirectional translation directions reveal that leveraging modern Chinese glosses - especially human-curated ones - dramatically elevates both lexical fidelity and semantic adequacy. Furthermore, multidimensional human preference audits by native experts unveil distinct cross-cultural preferences between Sinographic-tradition and non-Sinographic language groups.
  </details>

- **[SAG2: Embedding-Free Multi-Hop Retrieval with BM25 and Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22588)**  `arXiv:2610.22588`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuchao Wu, XingCheng Liang, Guanxian Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop retrieval must find passages that become useful only after intermediate evidence is retrieved. We present SAG2, an embedding-free retriever whose only persistent structure is a SQLite lexical index, built without an LLM. Where SAG and HippoRAG 2 rely on a generative LLM for graph extraction and selection, SAG2 uses a decision model. Given the question and a shared context of selected retrieved passages, the decision model scores whether each candidate supplies an answer-chain fact. Terms activated from the top-scoring passages retrieve unvisited passages through the lexical index, which holds a passage-term hypergraph implicitly. SAG2 reaches Recall@10 of 86.62%, 97.43%, and 99.00% on 1,000-question subsets of MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and HotpotQA, the highest among the compared systems. With a decision model priced at about a fifth of SAG's LLM, indexing and retrieval on MuSiQue cost USD 4.33 against USD 14.85 for SAG. On EnterpriseRAG-Bench, SAG2 reaches 81.12% document Recall@10. As the benchmark's nested corpus grows 447.5-fold, SAG2's Recall@5 lead over BM25 alone widens from 7.28 to 14.76 points, at a nearly constant model cost per question. Because indexing makes no model call, SAG2 suits corpora that change often or must be searchable as soon as they are indexed.
  </details>

- **[Memory Consolidation Flattens the Temporal Shape of User Facts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36457)**  `arXiv:2609.36457`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sugam Panthi, Muhaiminul Yeamin, Siyan Luo, Rabab Abdelfattah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents with long-term memory store conversations as short notes that later models read. A note can turn progressive "I am working at Corvida" into simple-present "The user works at Corvida," dropping the cue that the job may be temporary and that the stored fact may go stale. We call this aspectual flattening and measure it with LAPSE, a benchmark of matched user statements that differ only in temporal form. Three writer models flattened the progressive statement but kept its simple-present match in 210 of 381 pairs, never the reverse. This one-way loss held in all 11 models tested and in the agent memory systems mem0, Graphiti, and Letta. In exploratory tests, reader models judged a fact less likely to still hold when only its stored verb was progressive. In a planned test, the reader GLM-5.2 acted without asking the user on 64% of "works" notes but 40% of "is working" notes. Gemini 3 Flash showed a smaller gap, one reader added later showed the reverse, and others showed none. In a further exploratory test, free-text notes erased this difference and notes that kept the user's words restored it.
