Agent memory systems should keep the user's wording as evidence for later readers.
  </details>

- **[DeepStress: Stress-Testing Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13920)**  `arXiv:2607.13920`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ismael Rousseau, Geraldine Damnati, Frederic Bechet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While search agents demonstrate impressive capabilities in multi-step question answering, their robustness to poor-quality evidence remains under-explored. Such evidence is rare in benchmark corpora, and resulting failures are hard to reproduce. We propose DeepStress, a dynamic stress-testing framework that subjects agents to degraded evidence at controlled rates. It replaces the retrieval module with a synthetic environment where documents are generated dynamically for each query, under independent control of their trustworthiness, relevance, and factuality. To characterize behavior under stress, we go beyond binary accuracy with a five-way classification of final-answer outcomes and two new reliability-aware metrics, RAS and CoPRAS, which credit cautious behavior and account for computational cost. Evaluating 12 systems on questions from HotpotQA and BrowseComp-Plus, we find that several open-weight agents answer confidently when sources contain obvious credibility red flags, and that many HotpotQA questions are answered from parametric knowledge even when all returned documents are factually wrong.
  </details>

- **[Improving General Role-Playing Agents via Psychology-Grounded Reasoning and Role-Aware Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27025)**  `arXiv:2606.27025`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenhua Xu, Dongsheng Chen, Jian Li, Yitong Lin, Zhebo Wang, Jiafu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building general-purpose role-playing agents that faithfully portray any character from a natural-language profile remains challenging. Explicit reasoning can help models deliberate before responding, but generic reasoning does not necessarily preserve character fidelity. We therefore propose Psy-CoT, a psychology-grounded chain-of-thought framework that decomposes pre-response reasoning into three role-specific steps: Interaction Perception, Psychological Empathy, and Logical Construction, so that the model thinks dynamically from the profile rather than merely mimicking surface patterns. While structured reasoning provides a foundation, it alone is insufficient; reinforcement learning is essential to further align the model with character fidelity. However, we observe that under LLM-based reward models, both generic phrases that hack the reward model and genuinely role-specific phrases receive identical gradient signals. This hacking accumulates over training, misleading the model into treating both as equally optimal choices. To address this, we propose Role-Aware Policy Optimization (RAPO), which uses profile-induced entropy differences to weight gradients asymmetrically, amplifying role-specific tokens under positive advantage while attenuating them under negative advantage. Experiments on CoSER, CharacterBench, and CharacterEval demonstrate that Psy-CoT outperforms existing role-playing CoT methods, and RAPO consistently surpasses GRPO across multiple model scales.
  </details>

- **[Not All Gap Correction Helps: A Geometric View of External Information in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08294)**  `arXiv:2602.08294`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dingzirui Wang, Xuanliang Zhang, Keyan Xu, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che, Yang Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Providing external information beyond the query is a common way to improve large language model (LLM) inference, as seen in in-context learning (ICL), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and memory-based methods (Mem). Existing work increasingly suggests that useful external information should compensate for the gap between LLMs and user queries, implicitly assuming that stronger gap-correction can lead to better performance. In this paper, we challenge this assumption and show that more gap-corrective external information can instead harm performance. We analyze this phenomenon in Transformers through the lens of reasoning error, defined as the difference between the predicted answer vector and the ground-truth answer vector. Our analysis reveals that external information can be not only under-corrective but also over-corrective, where excessively strong correction increases reasoning error. We further show that the induced correction vector is jointly determined by attention and the complementarity between the external information and the query, and derive conditions under which information is properly corrective in both direction and magnitude. Experiments on four mainstream LLMs and seven reasoning benchmarks across ICL, RAG, and Mem validate our theory and support a theory-guided information selection method.
  </details>

- **[Divide and Conquer: A Hybrid Strategy Defeats Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16555)**  `arXiv:2412.16555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanxu Mao, Peipei Liu, Tiehan Cui, Zhaoteng Yan, Congying Liu, Datao You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied in various fields of society due to their powerful reasoning, understanding, and generation capabilities. However, the security issues associated with these models are becoming increasingly severe. Jailbreaking attacks, as an important method for detecting vulnerabilities in LLMs, have been explored by researchers who attempt to induce these models to generate harmful content through various attack methods. Nevertheless, existing jailbreaking methods face numerous limitations, such as excessive query counts, limited coverage of jailbreak modalities, low attack success rates, and simplistic evaluation methods. To overcome these constraints, this paper proposes a multimodal jailbreaking method: JMLLM. This method integrates multiple strategies to perform comprehensive jailbreak attacks across text, visual, and auditory modalities. Additionally, we contribute a new and comprehensive dataset for multimodal jailbreaking research: TriJail, which includes jailbreak prompts for all three modalities. Experiments on the TriJail dataset and the benchmark dataset AdvBench, conducted on 13 popular LLMs, demonstrate advanced attack success rates and significant reduction in time overhead.
  </details>

- **[AstroMemBench: A Benchmark for Memory Systems in Embodied Spacecraft Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16590)**  `arXiv:2611.16590`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _You Wu, Guhong Zhang, Hongwei Li, Xinyue Hong, Yuan Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term spacecraft autonomy requires embodied agents to use operational history under communication delays, time-critical operations, environmental uncertainty, complex physical interactions, power and computational constraints, and safety and reliability requirements. We introduce AstroMemBench and a Scenario-Challenge-Capability design method that maps these requirements to executable memory tests. The benchmark covers 16 memory subchallenges through 140 templates and 2240 instances across nine tasks in planetary surface operations, on-orbit servicing, and deep-space exploration. A deterministic environment scores actions for task completion, state changes, safety, and resource use. Ablations under nine conditions show that the benchmark is sensitive to how accurately agents recover and use historical information and to differences in memory-system design. Main experiments compare six configurations. Mem0, Chain-of-Memory, LightMem, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improve same-session mean scores over native Pi Agent, with no clear advantage of the three memory systems over RAG. After a session restart, Mem0 outperforms RAG by 12.25 points. Benefits favor instruction retention and result reuse over ongoing state tracking and energy reservation. AstroMemBench supports the design, development, and selection of memory systems for embodied spacecraft agents. AstroMemBench 1.0 and its evaluation code are publicly available:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where Autonomy Belongs: A Separation of Powers for Agentic Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15309)**  `arXiv:2611.15309`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhengyang Ai, Tong Liu, Jingxian Tang, Xiaobing Du, Tao Xiao, Fengshi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven program evolution rests on three decisions: govern directs the search from accumulated evidence, propose constructs the next candidate, and judge settles validity and fitness. All three appear in every system, and existing work splits into two lines over who makes each. Controller-centric systems strengthen govern and neglect the autonomy of propose, which caps what evolution can reach. Agent-centric systems let a coding agent take over both govern and propose, at a cognitive and computational cost. Neither line protects the independent authority of judge, so benchmark reliability suffers. These failures share one root: autonomy is misallocated across the three decisions. We address this problem with EvoTrias, a separation-of-powers architecture in which each decision has one owner: Structured Governance for what spans evolution rounds, Open Proposal for construction inside a task, and Protected Judgment behind a private evaluator. These owners exchange typed contracts only, and runtime hooks reinforce the boundaries. This separation also lets EvoTrias run as a plugin that turns an existing coding agent into an evolution system. EvoTrias sets a new state of the art on mathematical, systems, and algorithm-design benchmarks. Three further results show what each owner buys: separating Governance from Proposal cuts token cost by 64%, Open Proposal extends evolution to a 680K-line industrial repository, and Protected Judgment keeps benchmark scores faithful to transfer.
  </details>

- **[DrugCoding: A Scientific Programming Agent for Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13651)**  `arXiv:2611.13651`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Junwei Hu, Zhuohao Lin, Kun Li, Hengnan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drug discovery involves multiple interconnected research stages, including molecular design, property prediction, and virtual screening. These processes are characterized by complex workflows, interdependent computational tasks, and diverse specialized tools, placing substantial demands on the coordination of scientific models and computational resources. However, existing large language model (LLM)-based scientific agents still face challenges in autonomous planning of complex tasks, adaptive selection of specialized models, and reliable execution of multi-stage scientific computations, limiting their effectiveness in automating complex drug discovery workflows. To address these challenges, we propose DrugCoding, a scientific programming agent system that integrates scientific task planning, adaptive model routing, multi-agent collaboration, and programmatic tool execution. DrugCoding enables autonomous task planning and dynamic model adaptation for complex research objectives. It can flexibly invoke specialized scientific models, autonomously generate and execute code according to task requirements, and leverage multi-agent collaboration to support efficient execution and reliable validation of complex scientific tasks. To evaluate DrugCoding's performance in scientific tool invocation and multi-stage task execution, we conduct multi-turn tool-calling experiments across diverse language models and scientific tool interfaces. Experimental results demonstrate that DrugCoding achieves an average session-level Pass@1 of 97.35% on the full benchmark, outperforming the strongest baseline, Codex, by 9.24%. On the challenging subset, DrugCoding achieves 97.92%, exceeding the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[AgentSSL: Can MLE Agents Leverage Unlabeled Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17323)**  `arXiv:2611.17323`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ethan Lin, Akanksha Sarkar, Ziang Liu, Kristin Branson, Jennifer J. Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Applying machine learning systems to new domains introduces bottlenecks in both labeled data acquisition and manual pipeline engineering. While recent MLE agents show promise in automating pipeline design, they have been studied almost exclusively in fully supervised settings, leaving them untested in realistic regimes where labeled data is scarce but unlabeled data is abundant. In this work, we introduce Agentic Semi-Supervised Learning (AgentSSL), framing SSL as a program search problem and studying how agents navigate this space under limited supervision. We find that even under weak feedback and matched compute, agents can synthesize code that combines and adapts established SSL techniques, exceeding the strongest human-engineered baselines in 8 of 12 settings by up to +21.8%. However, our results identify program selection as a critical bottleneck in AgentSSL, where performance drops by as much as 47% when selecting based on the agent's feedback metric. To systematically study this bottleneck, we introduce ProgramSelectBench, a large-scale benchmark with a combined total of 16,977 agent-generated and human-engineered SSL programs, and evaluate both unsupervised and active program selection methods. We further evaluate AgentSSL on real-world ecology detection tasks, highlighting the opportunities and challenges of transferring agent-discovered strategies beyond controlled benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Classical Energies Learn to Reason 1,000x Faster than Looped Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17309)**  `arXiv:2611.17309`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Benjamin Hoover, Hugh Blayney, James C. R. Whittington_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking AI risk behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16318)**  `arXiv:2611.16318`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Luhan Mikaelson, Robert Adragna, Emmett Brosowsky, Hayley Clatterbuck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We evaluate LLM risk propensities across a range of contexts and task demands. We evaluate models for how risk averse or risk tolerant they are in various conditions, as well as how stable and consistent their behavior is across conditions. We present a toolkit for developing model risk profiles that can be used to predict model behavior and which can be adapted to include new evaluations of risk propensities. We also examine how these properties have changed across model generations and families. We observe that 2026 state-of-the-art models tend to be more stable, more consistent, and more risk neutral than older models. These trends appear to be related; newer models are more stable and consistent because they are more likely to use expected value maximization as a decision procedure.
  </details>

- **[SAGE-QA: Semantic-Aware, Grounded Evaluation for Closed-Context Question Answering on SQuAD 2.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16300)**  `arXiv:2611.16300`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chandra N Sekharan, Vishakan Umapathy, Abhishek Phadke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-context question answering (QA) requires a system to generate an answer strictly supported by a supplied passage, or to abstain when the passage lacks adequate evidence. SQuAD 2.0 is a widely used benchmark for this setting, scored by Exact Match (EM) i.e., character-level agreement with human reference spans after lexical normalization. For generative large language models (LLMs), however, an EM failure may reflect a valid paraphrase or an alternative passage-supported answer rather than a hallucination. The best prior leaderboard EM is approximately 91%, but evaluating generative models requires separating lexical divergence from factual error. We introduce SAGE-QA (Semantic-Aware, Grounded Evaluation for Question Answering), a multi-tiered framework combining reference-based Semantic Equivalence Scoring (SES) and passage-based Re-Validation Scoring (RVS). On a fixed set of 10,000 SQuAD 2.0 validation examples, we compute EM and decision-matrix F1 from TP, TN, FP, and FN counts for zero-shot closed-weight frontier models and for open-weight models, untuned and fine-tuned. Zero-shot, Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.1 Pro reach 85.21 and 86.66 EM, outperforming untuned Llama 3 8B (56.88), gpt-oss-20b (66.28), and Llama 3.3 70B (71.39). Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adds 21.19 to 34.06 EM points, raising these models to 90.94, 87.47, and 94.24 EM, respectively. Auditing the frontier models with SAGE-QA and an external LLM judge yields dataset-wide SES scores of 92.81 for Claude Opus 4.6 and 91.67 for Gemini 3.1 Pro, and hybrid RVS scores, which combine benchmark credit with passage-based judge assessments, of 97.96 and 98.02. Fine-tuned open-weight models perform best, with fine-tuned Llama 3.3 70B surpassing the previous best known EM.
  </details>

- **[Reward-Driven Collider Physics Challenges for Self-Improving Agentic AI via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15885)**  `arXiv:2611.15885`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Alexander Yue, Qibin Liu, Julia Gonski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in agentic AI have demonstrated the potential to automate complex high-energy physics (HEP) workflows. We focus on the regime of HEP tasks where frontier models still struggle to provide correct and complete solutions, and introduce an open-ended optimization task format with continuous, automatically evaluated rewards. Unlike conventional benchmarks focused on task completion, these tasks target reinforcement learning (RL) environments to generate agentic improvement on state- of-the-art HEP challenges. We benchmark frontier reasoning models on several HEP tasks and demonstrate an initial proof of concept in which a small language model improves through RL using automated feedback without human intervention. By connecting challenging HEP problems with verifiable scientific rewards and RL, we aim to advance agentic AI from reproducing established workflows to autonomously discovering better scientific solutions, paving the way toward self-improving scientific agents.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Task Self-Evolving Harnesses with the Agent as Its Own Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38372)**  `arXiv:2609.38372`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qiankai Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A harness is the code around a language-model agent that organizes prompts, calls tools, manages context, and controls execution. As models grow stronger, recent work has begun to let agents improve their own harnesses, a line of work known as self-evolving harnesses. In most existing methods, a separate proposer running on a human-designed harness modifies the solver's harness, and a separate harness is evolved for each benchmark. Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and the evaluation of a harness should cover a diverse range of tasks. We propose a framework close to recursive self-improvement: the same frozen model, on the same version of the harness, first solves tasks as the solver and then, as the proposer, reads the complete run records and directly edits the harness that runs it. Each evolution batch draws tasks from five benchmarks in different domains. To measure generalization, training and held-out tasks are strictly separated, and we additionally evaluate on five out-of-distribution benchmarks never used during evolution. We frame the evolution process as deep-learning training with two stages, multi-task pretraining and continual training. Starting from a 49-line seed harness, the harnesses evolved by GLM-5.3 and GPT-5.6 Sol gain about 5 points on the in-distribution benchmarks, matching or exceeding Claude Code and Codex, and the GPT-5.6 Sol harness is on par with Codex on five out-of-distribution benchmarks. Continued evolution on Claw-Eval in the second stage then lifts GPT-5.6 Sol and GLM-5.3-Flash above Codex and Claude Code. We also provide an in-depth analysis of the mechanisms that emerged during evolution, including output truncation, history compaction, completion checks, independent review, and visual inspection. Code and the evolved harnesses are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Submodular Benchmark Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02209)**  `arXiv:2605.02209`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Alexander Smola_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating a new language model means running it on a large, growing collection of benchmarks, at considerable cost. Much of this cost is redundant: arranged as a matrix of models by benchmarks, scores are strongly correlated and concentrate in a few dimensions, so a few well-chosen benchmarks largely determine the rest. This allows us to run a new model on a few benchmarks and predict the rest with a simple Gaussian model that copes with missing scores. The key question is \emph{which few to run}. An answer comes from sensor placement, which chooses the observations that carry the most information, measured by entropy or mutual information. We argue that the objective should instead reward a selection for how well it predicts every benchmark. This leads to two coverage objectives: explained variance, the predictions' accuracy if the estimated correlations were exact, and facility location, which credits each benchmark with its best proxy among the selected ones. On eleven score matrices built from public evaluation results, the coverage objectives predict the scores of held-out models best wherever the correlations can be estimated. The information objectives fall behind because they favor benchmarks that the selection predicts poorly (entropy) or that the others predict well (mutual information), not benchmarks that predict the others. By contrast, facility location predicts practically as well as explained variance, holds up on the addition of newer models and under real costs, has classical guarantees (even under a budget), and gives readable selections.
  </details>

- **[Safety as a Budget: Calibrating the Control Channel of Constrained GRPO for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16628)**  `arXiv:2611.16628`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Bo Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning can teach vision-language models (VLMs) to decline harmful image-text requests, but how safe the result becomes is set indirectly, through reward weights found by trial runs. Stating safety as budgets on the rate of harmful assistance and the rate of over-refusal, enforced by Lagrange multipliers inside GRPO with an off-the-shelf scorer as the cost, is the natural fix, and it fails twice. The multiplier compares the scorer's mean with the budget, and scorers built for ranking get the mean wrong: a fixed-point analysis shows that the mean bias, not the budget, then decides whether the multiplier settles, saturates or switches off. And standard GRPO standardizes the penalized reward within each group, which cancels the multiplier before it reaches the gradient. Two-channel constrained GRPO repairs both: the base reward keeps the raw score for the within-group comparison, the penalty and the constraint estimate pass through a monotone map fitted on judge-labeled training responses, and the advantage keeps the multiplier. On a Qwen3-VL-2B policy with a training-free readout of an open 4B model as the scorer, held-out harm on 1,037 adversarial prompts falls from 8.9% under standard GRPO to 4.8%, lower in every seed, where the uncalibrated budget gives 7.6%; over-refusal stays at most 2% on 400 benign queries and MMBench is unchanged. The gain is largest where the intent sits in the image (10.1% -> 5.1%) and grows on the unseen HADES benchmark (28.9% -> 12.8%). Order-preserving distortions of the scores reproduce each predicted controller state in every seed, and the results hold under a map fitted on another judge's labels, an image-reading judge and a human audit.
  </details>

- **[SkillEarth: Teaching General-Purpose Agents to Act on Earth via Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15481)**  `arXiv:2611.15481`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Haojun Tang, Wenda Zhao, Xiang Zhou, Qiwei Lin, Lida Huang, Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently developed remote sensing (RS) agents execute real-world tasks with complex workflows through multi-step tool invocation. These agents, however, typically rely on predefined expertise or parameter updates. They thus require updating expert knowledge or retraining models whenever new tools, tasks, or execution patterns emerge, limiting their continual adaptability in dynamic Earth environments. In this work, we seek to equip general-purpose agents with RS tool-call knowledge that can be automatically extracted from demonstrated trajectories without expert intervention or parameter updates. Specifically, we propose SkillEarth, a self-evolving skill extraction method that externalizes RS tool-call knowledge as reusable, retrievable, and evolvable skill files and refines them through observation-grounded evolution. Unlike existing automatic skill extraction methods that rely on outcome-based reflection to refine skills holistically, SkillEarth grounds skill evolution in real tool-chain execution and combines dual-judge feedback with a grounded observation routing graph for precise component-level refinement. The resulting skills are generalizable, reliable, and transferable: they improve overall RS tool-call performance across three frontier LLMs and four open-source LLMs, are refined through real tool execution feedback, and support cross-model reuse without retraining. Experiments on OpenEarth-Bench and Earth-Bench demonstrate that SkillEarth achieves better tool selection, argument correctness, execution-order consistency, and task completion than existing RS agents and skill extraction methods.
  </details>

- **[Emergent Vision-Shaped Thinking in Text-Only Reasoning through Multimodal Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14942)**  `arXiv:2611.14942`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Zhang, Bohan Wang, Qianru Sun, Hanwang Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does multimodal training shape how a language model thinks, even when the input is text only? We test this with targeted causal ablations across 10 multimodal configurations from 6 model families, with matched text-only backbones and cross-family transfer controls. We contrast spatial-imagery tasks that invite mental scene construction with symbol-manipulation tasks solvable by symbolic rules alone. We identify a vision-language axis in hidden-state space and ablate its projection at inference. In Chameleon-7B, this ablation drops accuracy on BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) navigate by 27.6 pp while leaving boolean expressions intact (+5.6 pp), a 33.2 pp double dissociation. The effect is direction-specific and layer-localized, extends to mental-simulation tasks such as mental rotation, paper folding, and perspective taking, and is not explained by task difficulty, reasoning depth, or sequential update load. Together, these results reveal emergent vision-shaped thinking (EVT): text-only reasoning that causally recruits representations introduced or strengthened by vision-language training, without assuming an image-like internal format. The measured effect is spatially concentrated at 7-8B and broadens to non-spatial visual attribute binding in the tested 32B configuration. In dual-path unified models (BAGEL, Janus-Pro), the causal handle localizes to the generation pathway as a compact one-dimensional carrier. The understanding pathway also encodes spatial information in a higher-rank, probe-decodable form, but is not the causal handle. To separate decodability from causal use, we analyze concentration, alignment, and semantic location. These diagnostics explain why behavior, probing, and intervention can diverge. Multimodal training thus reshapes the geometry of text-only reasoning, not just how visual input is processed.
  </details>

- **[LOLPlay: Benchmarking Real-Time Visual Agents in League of Legends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03685)**  `arXiv:2611.03685`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaojian Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents need to coordinate reasoning and action while their environment evolves during inference. We introduce LOLPlay, a benchmark in native League of Legends with 36 champion-specific tasks spanning resource management, combat, cooperation, and objectives. Agents interact through screenshots and mouse-keyboard actions; automated initialization and 82 executable subgoal checks distinguish partial progress from completion. Across 324 task evaluations of three models at three reasoning-effort settings, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate of 25.0% at low and medium effort, and the highest subgoal success rate of 40.3% at medium effort. However, high effort improves neither metric over low effort for any model. For Astra, longer requests leave fewer interaction rounds, suggesting a cost to additional reasoning in real-time control. Trajectories reveal incomplete subgoal composition and actions executing after the observed scene changes, motivating agents that combine visual reasoning with timely action.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Supervision and Capability Retention in LoRA Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17072)**  `arXiv:2611.17072`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Saleh Ashkboos, Dhruv Desai, Lavinia Ghita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning foundation models on financial data has a wide range of applications, including identifying event types from news, monitoring risk and compliance language, and answering questions over filings and earnings calls. In this setting, the main challenge is to improve task performance without degrading the generalization capabilities of the model. In this paper, we present an extensive set of ablations for fine-tuning Qwen3-8B with low-rank adaptation (LoRA). We compare three training regimes: (1) response-only LoRA, where the supervised response is the label alone; (2) single-adapter reasoning, where reasoning traces from a teacher model are included alongside the labels in the training loss; and (3) dual-adapter composite training, where separate LoRA adapters are trained on traces and labels. Response-only LoRA improves in-domain performance and leaves general capabilities near those of the frozen model. The in-domain change is largest for calibration and argmax accuracy. Under single-adapter reasoning, the in-domain metrics remain behind those of the response-only regime, while MMLU-Pro can rise slightly and the other retention benchmarks stay within ordinary variation. Dual-adapter composite training remains on the same side of this comparison and yields no further gain. Finally, we compress the teacher traces by summarizing them before training, and show that shorter summaries move calibration toward the response-only regime without reaching it.
  </details>

- **[Cleaning an Agent's History Hurts When the World Still Holds the Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14654)**  `arXiv:2611.14654`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Egor Pakhomov, Anurag Koul, Hongjun Liu, Bo Pang, Shafiq Joty, Erik Nijkamp_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language-model agents accumulate histories of their own mistakes, and a system that compacts, repairs or hands off such a history decides what it says about those mistakes while their effects stay in the world. Prior work cleans the history at a fixed world, where showing a corrected step and saying it is done move together. We ask whether the same correct content helps or hurts depending on how the history states it and what the world holds. We freeze an agent mid-task with its exact world state, edit the history or the world, and let a receiver model continue. On AgentCE-Bench, after the agent commits a wrong value, we hold the world fixed and add the correct value by two natural edits: a truthful note beside the preserved error or the agent's own completed action. The note raises recovery, the share of remaining targets the receiver gets right, from 51% under a matched neutral note to 66%. The completed action lowers it below the unedited history, from 50% to 44%, so the value's presence alone does not predict the effect. On AppWorld, after a database write, we vary whether its receipt reports success or failure and whether its effect is present. A success receipt lowers whole-task success from 45% to 28% while the effect is absent and raises it from 38% to 49% once present. The two effects differ by 27 points pooled over two receivers, a difference positive in six of eight receivers at 97.5% and seven at 95%. A history edit's effect thus depends on the world it describes, and a history made to look clean while the world still holds the error can leave an agent worse off than no edit at all.
  </details>

- **[How Should Planning Agents ReSHAPE Their Search Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16466)**  `arXiv:2611.16466`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yilin Zhang, Xinran Zhao, Tong Chen, Ziming Yuan, Tongshuang Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning agents and benchmarks treat a user's initial specification as fixed. Yet the requests users write exclude plans they would gladly take, since they rarely discover the possible trade-offs they would accept until seeing the options. Agents already revise requests through interaction, but whether this helps planning is unknown. We formalize this step as the ReSHAPE task, which judges a revision by whether its search space matches the acceptable plans and a planner finds one there. Using ReSHAPE Planter, which builds instances from planning benchmarks with a human-validated simulated user, we analyze 5,800 agent-revised specifications in travel and shopping. We find that reshaping matters: one correct trade-off makes the same planner reach an acceptable plan up to 4.6x as often, and search space quality predicts what the planner finds. Current agents, however, admit at most 32% of the acceptable plans, where that trade-off alone admits over 52%, because they ask about preferences instead of showing plans; in travel, showing plans raises F1 by 9.7 points over asking, with two model families. Planning agents should thus treat a request as a hypothesis to test against the user's reactions to concrete plans to recognize user trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Science Prediction Universe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15049)**  `arXiv:2611.15049`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zengji Tu, Xiang Liu, Jiarui Sun, Ruoyu Wu, Zhihui Qi, Chengda Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanity has long sought to explain natural phenomena. Reliable prediction of experimental outcomes is a central test of our understanding of the processes that produce them. If artificial general intelligence (AGI) is to surpass human scientific capabilities, it must also predict experimental outcomes more reliably than humans. Such capabilities could accelerate scientific discovery across disciplines, help address humanity's most pressing challenges, and improve human well-being. We introduce Science Prediction Universe (SPU), a benchmark of hidden experimental outcomes with 100 public verified tasks from chemistry, materials science, biology and medicine, and 16 private tasks from unpublished wet-lab experiments. Given an experimental setup and candidate conditions, an agent works with any resources it needs to rank the candidates by a measured outcome.We evaluate thirteen agent configurations spanning twelve frontier models across two harnesses (Codex and Claude Code). Current agents remain far from saturating wet-lab outcome prediction, with substantial performance disparities across models: on the public benchmark, configurations separate into two distinct tiers, with task-adjusted Spearman correlations ranging from 0.227 for the lowest-performing configuration to 0.478 for the best. Analysis of 1,155 scored public trajectories (227,103 tool calls) suggests that the bottleneck lies in scientific judgment rather than tool execution: agents rely on shared monotone priors, agreeing far more with one another than with experimental reality (0.52 vs. 0.33), and frequently compute trivial physical or chemical proxies that do not influence the final outcome.
  </details>

- **[Termination Lag: Coding Agents Solve Early but Stop Late](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14960)**  `arXiv:2611.14960`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Musa Cim, Burak Topcu, Mahmut Taylan Kandemir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can solve a task well before they stop working on it. We characterize \emph{termination lag}--the number of steps between an agent's first correct patch and the end of its run--across five models, two agent frameworks, and three SWE-bench datasets by recording and testing every distinct repository patch generated during execution. Our analysis reveals that 38--82% of successful runs achieve a correct solution on their initial source-code edit. However, agents continue to expend substantial effort after correct patches, with post-solution steps accounting for a median of 35--37% for Qwen3-Coder and 54--60% for Laguna-XS.2 in successful runs on SWE-bench Verified and Multilingual. Agents spend most of these additional steps verifying their work and exploring the repository. In a small fraction of cases (1--8%), this continued activity leads agents to undo their earlier correct patch, leaving the final solution incorrect.
  </details>

- **[AGORA: Reliability-Aware Aggregation of LLM Judges on Small Evaluation Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05556)**  `arXiv:2611.05556`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junhyung Ahn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge is a common substitute for costly human evaluation, and aggregating several judges is the standard remedy for any single judge's bias. Judge pools mix judges of very different reliability, while the human-labeled sets that validate them hold a few hundred items or fewer. In this regime the per-item mean weights weak judges as heavily as strong ones, and confusion-table models such as Dawid-Skene must estimate a per-judge error table from too few items. We propose AGORA (Aggregation Of Reliability-weighted Assessments), an item-response-theory aggregator that places every item on one latent quality scale and describes each judge by a sensitivity parameter and ordered rating thresholds, so judge reliability is learned from all items jointly and without human labels. Across three benchmarks, AGORA matches or exceeds Dawid-Skene on large evaluation sets, and its advantage widens as the set shrinks toward a few hundred items. It remains accurate as pools grow more uneven, a label-free dispersion statistic indicates in advance when trust weighting helps, and its learned trust weights track per-judge human validity. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LiveGeo: Benchmarking Geographic Agents in Continuously Evolving Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17267)**  `arXiv:2611.17267`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengcheng Zhou, Xuanyu Liu, Yanchen Yin, Tianjie Ju, Bobo Li, Shengqiong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GeoWorld, a controllable and continuously evolving geographic environment platform for LLM agents, and LiveGeo, a benchmark for evaluating reliable decision-making in real-world geographic environments using GeoWorld. Unlike existing evaluations that largely reason and plan over fixed geographic states, GeoWorld is grounded in real-world geographic structure and allows external world states to evolve along their own temporal processes while preserving verifiable state histories. LiveGeo places geographic tasks within this evolving environment, requiring agents to actively acquire task-relevant information, verify whether the evidence supporting prior decisions remains valid, and revise their plans when necessary. Our results reveal that even frontier LLMs struggle with static tasks, with no model consistently excelling across tasks, while strong static performance does not ensure dynamic reliability. Further analysis identifies a key bottleneck: agents struggle to acquire and translate relevant evidence into valid decisions and maintain their correctness as the world evolves. By releasing LiveGeo together with its GeoWorld platform, we aim to provide the community with a flexible and extensible infrastructure for studying the next generation of agents capable of reliable action in evolving geographic worlds.
  </details>

- **[Skill Adapter: Personalized Modification and Online Routing of General-Purpose Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17007)**  `arXiv:2611.17007`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Yanyi Gou, Wenbin Ma, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents use external skills to reuse task procedures and experience with tools. General-purpose skills describe shared execution procedures, while specific tool choices, operating practices, and output requirements vary across users. In long-horizon tasks, subgoals, available inputs, and environmental states change as execution progresses. Agents must select currently applicable skills from an expanding skill pool and prepare candidates for subsequent steps.We propose Skill Adapter, which enables personalized modification and online routing of general-purpose skills. The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Compared with SkillRL, it improves task success rates by an average of 11.43 percentage points and reduces mean execution time on all three benchmarks. Skill Adapter retains its performance advantage when the skill pool grows to 1,000 skills.
  </details>

- **[AnalogLayoutWorld: An Open Benchmark for Agentic Analog Layout Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16991)**  `arXiv:2611.16991`  `cs.AI`  
  _NovaSilicon Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in software development, yet their ability to tackle long-horizon engineering tasks governed by physical constraints remains less understood. We study this question through analog circuit layout, which demands domain knowledge, geometric reasoning, and iterative interaction with design and verification tools. We introduce \textbf{AnalogLayoutWorld}, an open-source agent benchmark with 40 expert-reviewed tasks for end-to-end layout generation from fixed transistor-level netlists. It combines an integrated design environment with independent physical and post-layout electrical verification, as well as objective layout quality metrics co-designed with analog layout experts. Across 13 model--harness configurations, 91.1\% of attempts produce GDS layouts, but only 23.0\% satisfy the benchmark's acceptance criteria. We also evaluate Novara, an internally developed agent system, which achieves the highest reported aggregate score. Our analysis links performance differences to test-time scaling under fixed budgets, suggesting that reasoning efficiency and effective use of verification feedback help sustain design quality as task complexity grows.
  </details>

- **[The Value of Thinking: Learning When to Reason in Hybrid Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16961)**  `arXiv:2611.16961`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengxu Yu, Ke Xu, Meng Fang, Haitham Ammar, Weilin Luo, Jun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing adaptive-reasoning methods shorten the explicit reasoning a model writes, or skip it by switching modes; either way the answer is still written out token by token. \emph{Rather than deciding how much to think}, we explore a different paradigm, \emph{zero-or-$T$ thinking}: a single model either answers from the implicit thinking inside one forward pass, generating no token, or performs full autoregressive reasoning at a cost of $T$ tokens when that is worth paying. We realise it in a \emph{hybrid decision model} (HDM), where one frozen backbone serves both modes and a lightweight \emph{self-routing block} chooses between them. The block estimates the \emph{value of thinking}, the utility the slow mode is expected to add over the fast one, from what that implicit thinking has already produced, and the slow mode runs only where that value exceeds a price $\lambda$ set per request. Where no per-decision label exists, as in episodic agentic tasks, we introduce \emph{Calibrated Decision Policy Optimisation} (CDPO), a reinforcement-learning recipe that trains the block from episode-level returns. We evaluate HDM on MMLU-Pro, AGIEval, GPQA-Diamond, JevBench, BANKING77 and MiniWoB. Specifically, on JevBench HDM-4B scores $0.857$ against Jev's $0.851$ ($+0.7\%$) at a median $0.179$\,s against $0.652$ ($-72.5\%$). On the agentic venue MiniWoB HDM-27B cuts token cost by $80.1\%$.
  </details>

- **[S1-Evo: Experience-Guided Evidence Competition Attacks on Typed System-1 Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16937)**  `arXiv:2611.16937`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaixiang Wang, Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed System-1 (S1) decision models are increasingly deployed in LLM agents for routing, moderation, and authorization to cut inference cost, yet untrusted context can still manipulate typed outputs and alter execution branches. Unlike autoregressive System-2 (S2) models, S1 interfaces score dynamic rules and options without token-by-token decoding, mismatching attacks targeting text continuation or explicit override commands. We propose S1-Evo, a self-evolving framework tailored to S1's contract-based option competition and margin feedback. S1-Evo perturbs untrusted fields via contract-aware evidence channels to induce \emph{evidence competition} with original task evidence while preserving fact invariance. Offline on seed tasks, it optimizes candidates via feedback search guided by directional margins and re-verification, distilling verified flips into experience cards. At test time, S1-Evo retrieves relevant cards to construct perturbations for unseen tasks. Across four benchmarks and three S1 models, S1-Evo reaches 71.99\% mean ASR and 57.47\% high-confidence ASR on \texttt{jev-1.14}, and causes up to 24.00\% E2E agent violations.
  </details>

- **[Scale-Induced Semantic Shift in Visual Prompt Injection Against VLMs and Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16744)**  `arXiv:2611.16744`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiheng Zhao, Runze Ji, Chenyu Yi, Yi Yu, Bingquan Shen, Alex Kot, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual content in multimedia systems may be resized, compressed, or re-rendered before it is interpreted by a vision-language model. We study a security failure mode in which these transformations change the machine-readable semantics of an image: a textual instruction is not recovered at a $512\times512$ presentation resolution under our evaluation protocol but becomes recoverable after aggressive downsampling. We formulate this behavior as Scale-Induced Semantic Shift (SISS) and develop a closed-loop black-box framework that combines dual-scale verification, selective self-hardening, shallow-layer low-rank adaptation, and pixel-space noise regularization. On a fixed 300-case benchmark, the third SISS refinement round obtains a $58.75\%$ average attack success rate across four closed-source vision-language models, compared with $41.08\%$ for the strongest generation baseline. A controlled sweep bounds the effect to aggressive compression: average success decreases to $17.68\%$, $6.85\%$, and $5.60\%$ at $128\times128$, $224\times224$, and $336\times336$, respectively. In a downstream web-agent study, the generated images increase hidden-instruction perception, but absolute behavioral compromise remains about $4\%$. Generic input transformations provide little protection in a separate defense protocol, whereas the proposed ScaleGuard consistency detector reduces non-adaptive success from $61.4\%$ to $24.3\%$ and one-round defense-aware success from $42.5\%$ to $15.0\%$, with no observed rejection among matched clean controls. The results characterize a bounded but reproducible semantic inconsistency in multimedia preprocessing and motivate scale-consistent visual-language inference.
  </details>

- **[Mind the Accuracy-Approval Gap: How Users Evaluate Sycophantic AI in Everyday Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16722)**  `arXiv:2611.16722`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wajiha Naveed, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, a tendency to agree with users, flatter or affirm them, and preserve their face, even when independent judgment or critical feedback would be more useful. Prior work has largely evaluated sycophancy through benchmark evaluations, but less is known about how users perceive and respond to sycophantic AI in everyday tasks. We combine benchmark evaluations with two large-scale user studies. We study four personas defined through system prompts: objective (instructed to prioritize accuracy over agreement), soft-sycophantic, hard-sycophantic, and baseline (no persona-specific system prompt). Benchmarks show that even baseline exhibits substantial sycophancy, agreeing with users' stated views 71.7% of the time on NLP Survey, compared with 54.8% for objective and 89.5% for hard-sycophantic. Under conversational pressure on TruthfulQA, accuracy ranged from 81.9% for objective to 59.6% for baseline and 8.7% for hard-sycophantic. Participants were assigned to one of the four personas, and either discussed contested policy topics or collaborated with AI on email writing. Despite its higher accuracy under pressure, objective AI was rated significantly less respectful and satisfying than the other personas, and less trustworthy than baseline. Baseline and sycophantic personas did not differ significantly. Persona did not significantly affect how often participants changed their opinions, although participants who discussed with objective AI had greater odds of reduced confidence. Objective AI prompted greater writing expansion than soft-sycophantic AI, but final writing quality did not differ significantly across personas. These findings highlight a tension between accuracy and user approval: personas with substantially lower accuracy under conversational pressure received more favorable ratings for respect and satisfaction than objective AI.
  </details>

- **[Natural Gradient Learning in Predictive Coding through Lateral Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16666)**  `arXiv:2611.16666`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lhea Beumer, Sander de Haan, Niclas Pokel, Pau Vilimelis Aceituno, Benjamin F. Grewe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predictive coding (PC) trains neural networks through local energy-minimization dynamics without depending on a global error signal that backpropagation (BP) requires. Although PC is a promising alternative algorithm for efficient learning, the computational cost of inference dynamics currently outweighs the resulting learning benefits in practice. In this work, we take inspiration from optimization theory to extract more learning benefit from each inference phase. We introduce Natural PC, which augments standard PC with a lateral circuit, enabling exact and efficient natural gradient learning locally in space and time. Our lateral circuit learns the activity correlations that determine the Fisher information matrix and implicitly computes its inverse, avoiding expensive matrix inversion. Across all tested image reconstruction benchmarks, Natural PC achieves state of the art in terms of sample and compute efficiency compared to existing PC approaches. Most notably, Natural PC scales to ImageNet and matches the performance of BP trained with AdamW and various second-order optimizers at an order of magnitude lower computational cost while also using two orders of magnitude fewer training samples. Our results demonstrate that local natural gradient learning makes PC competitive with BP in both sample efficiency and computational cost in reconstruction tasks.
  </details>

- **[Folio: Recoverable Multi-Resolution Context Folding for Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16627)**  `arXiv:2611.16627`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yubo Ruan, Tao Peng, Jiao Zhang, Chuanguo Ma, Lei Xu, Han Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon language agents accumulate tool results and replies until the history no longer fits the context window. Current agents shrink this history by having a model summarize it, so each compaction rereads and rewrites the history it replaces, and cache-aware triggers decide when to compact while taking the size of the result as given. We present Folio, a context manager that records, folds, and recovers history through one set of handles. While the agent runs, Folio records three linked levels: a short turn note the model writes as part of its ordinary reply (L1), an event manifest of every call written by the runtime (L2), and the captured source records (L3). Because these views already exist, the runtime folds the context programmatically, selecting coarser views for older turns. The fold prints the handles that index L2 and L3, so recovery tools that read bounded pages, compute over full text in files, and keep findings in a persistent dossier reach the evidence the agent needs with little added input. The fold trigger prices an explicit landing point that includes the retained budget. On 75 LOCA-bench tasks with DeepSeek-V4-Pro behind every system, Folio solves 66 tasks against 54 for the same runtime with model-written compaction and 49-59 for Claude Code, Codex, and SoL-Pi, in about half their working time, at 2.4 times lower cost than that runtime; each fold takes a median of 0.6 seconds.
  </details>

- **[Singularity-Bench: Can Agents Build Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16591)**  `arXiv:2611.16591`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Zhou, Krish Agarwal, Zhuoming Chen, Zerui Wang, Zhenyu Zhang, Wenshuang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier AI agents increasingly succeed on realistic, long-horizon tasks and are beginning to improve the systems through which they operate. Yet existing benchmarks often restrict agents to narrow action spaces, use simplified environments, or rely on verification methods that are unreliable for evaluating arbitrary agent-generated systems. We introduce Singularity-Bench, an open-ended benchmark for autonomous AI development with six end-to-end tasks spanning pre-training, mid-training, post-training, inference, the agent harness, and cluster orchestration. Each task requires agents to implement core systems from a minimal starting point and evaluates them against mature human-engineered baselines on identical hardware. Additionally, through extensive red-teaming covering 9 of the 10 categories in the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, we expose reward-hacking vulnerabilities in the conventional Harbor framework and develop Breakwater, an extension of Harbor that blocks all attacks tested in our study. Across 11 agents built from 7 frontier models and 2 harnesses, Opus 5.5 with Claude Code matches or outperforms the human baseline on all six tasks with a Singularity-Index of 1.25, suggesting it may have the skills needed to reach the singularity. The remaining agents average only 0.58. Our vision is for Singularity-Bench to track how much of AI development agents can already automate and when they may transition from assisting to taking end-to-end responsibility for building stronger successors.
  </details>

- **[ReA-Bench: Benchmarking Responsible Agent Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16493)**  `arXiv:2611.16493`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zipeng Ye, Shenyu Dai, Yong Xie, Kun Wang, Jiyang Guan, Zhikai Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agents perform tasks autonomously in local and cloud environments, auditing their execution trajectories can help service providers identify risks and improve agent harnesses and foundation models. Our analysis of production and red-team trajectories shows that some risks are repeatedly missed across auditing configurations. Overall recall obscures which risks are missed and whether higher recall comes with more false positives on legitimate behavior. Existing benchmarks offer limited insight into these failures. Generated trajectories can contain inconsistent tool calls and responses, conspicuous risky actions, or repetitive attack patterns. Grouping results by risk type also makes it difficult to tell whether auditors overlooked evidence, accepted an unsupported conclusion, or lacked information needed to make a judgment.We introduce ReA-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for auditing responsible agent behavior. It organizes auditing challenges into six tiers: internal safety knowledge, contradictions within trajectories, evidence retrieval in long contexts, verification of sources and reasoning, indirect steering, and missing information. It includes challenging benign cases in which actions resemble risky ones but are consistent with the task and its authorization. It also provides paired trajectory versions to test whether auditors detect the same risks more reliably when the trajectory is shortened or missing information is supplied. Most samples are drawn from real production trajectories, with scenarios manually selected and adapted to preserve realistic tasks and risks that could plausibly arise during execution.
  </details>

- **[NIBench: benchmarking autonomous AI agents for execution, skill evolution and scientific judgment in neuroimaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15831)**  `arXiv:2611.15831`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lujia Zhong, Yihao Xia, Jianwei Zhang, Shuo Huang, Xinyu Nie, Mingyang Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous AI agents are increasingly used to automate biomedical research, creating a growing need to benchmark their capabilities on real scientific data and workflows. Their reliability in neuroimaging remains insufficiently characterized, where plausible analytical outputs can conceal errors that affect scientific conclusions. Existing studies have advanced scientific question answering and tool-based automation, but provide limited evidence on workflow reconstruction, acquisition of procedural expertise and evidence-based scientific judgment. Here we present NIBench, a neuroimaging benchmark comprising three complementary evaluations of autonomous agents. First, we benchmark execution and workflow reconstruction using 56 tasks across seven tool ecosystems, covering structural, diffusion and functional magnetic resonance imaging, positron emission tomography and multimodal analyses. Second, we examine whether agents can evolve their procedural skills through distillation of execution trajectories, evaluating the resulting skills on previously encountered tasks, unseen tasks and other models. Third, we assess scientific judgment using 36 claims from published Alzheimer's disease studies, requiring agents to analyse raw cohort data and provide reproducible evidence. We evaluate 19 language models within a shared agent framework, with selected models undergoing skill distillation and claim evaluation. Leading agents execute supplied analysis protocols nearly perfectly, whereas withholding procedural guidance widens performance differences. Distilled skills improve performance on acquisition tasks and transfer between models, but benefits on unseen tasks are inconsistent. Agents reproduce their reported results yet sometimes reach conclusions inconsistent with expert-derived answers, and access to data does not always improve judgment.
  </details>

- **[DISCERN: Preserve at Write, Resolve at Read for Persistent LLM-Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15729)**  `arXiv:2611.15729`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chirag Ahuja, Vladislav Tanov, Ramcharan Kakarla, Jonathan Taws_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise agents accumulate memory across dozens of sessions in which facts contradict, get superseded, and blur as similarly named entities collide in embedding space. The field treats this as a retrieval problem; we show it is not. On ENT-310, a synthetic enterprise sales-agent memory benchmark of 310 queries (210 answerable, 100 unanswerable), a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reader over a memory store already matches strong memory systems on the recall pillars. Retention and currency are near-saturated, roughly 0.93 to 0.98 for every competent system, so recall is essentially solved. What remains unsolved is resolution: which entity a query refers to, which time-state is current, and whether to commit an answer at all or decline when evidence is insufficient. We propose DISCERN (DISambiguation and Cross-session Episodic memoRy Navigation), an agent-memory framework built on a single rule: preserve at write, resolve at read. At write time DISCERN builds a strictly additive bi-temporal provenance graph that never merges confusable entities and never drops superseded values. At read time a zero-LLM tool surface drives an agentic loop that navigates this graph and commits exactly one of four gated terminal actions. A four-judge LLM-as-a-Judge council scores two axes: answer correctness on answerable questions, and the answer-versus-abstain decision as a classification problem. On ENT-310, DISCERN is the best-calibrated system on abstention (F1 0.924 versus at most 0.802 across eight baselines) and leads macro answer correctness under equal pillar weighting (0.861 versus 0.806 for the strongest baseline), a lead driven by entity resolution (0.800, where no baseline exceeds 0.40). Applied with zero tuning to the full LoCoMo corpus, the same pipeline again leads macro answer correctness (0.663 versus 0.638) and stays among the best-calibrated on false-premise abstention.
  </details>

- **[Marginal-Preserving Coupled Diffusion Composition for Long-Horizon Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15585)**  `arXiv:2611.15585`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hengrui Zhang, Qiyu Wang, Yuhu Cheng, Brooks Paige, Xuesong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compositional diffusion planners extend short-horizon trajectory models to long-horizon goal-conditioned planning by generating and stitching multiple local trajectory chunks. However, in multimodal environments, local plausibility is not sufficient for global consistency: independently initialized chunks may denoise toward incompatible overlap modes, producing plans that are individually feasible but incoherent after stitching. Existing remedies typically add search, guidance, pruning, or graph-based structure at inference time, increasing computational cost or relying on task-specific machinery. We instead investigate graph-structured dependence among chunk-wise initial diffusion noises as a lightweight coordination mechanism for compositional trajectory planning. We propose \textbf{M}arginal-Preserving \textbf{C}oupled \textbf{D}iffusion \textbf{C}omposition (MCDC), a training-free inference method for pretrained compositional diffusion planners. Instead of sampling chunk noises independently, MCDC constructs coupled Gaussian initializations whose per-chunk marginals remain exactly standard Gaussian, as required by the pretrained denoiser, while neighboring chunks receive positively correlated noise components. This preserves the standard-Gaussian initialization marginal of each chunk while biasing adjacent chunks toward compatible overlap-mode choices. We prove exact marginal preservation and show, under a simple multimodal overlap model, that positive coupling increases the probability of compatible mode selection. On OGBench long-horizon stitch tasks, MCDC consistently improves over independent compositional diffusion sampling while retaining nearly the same inference time, and achieves a favorable success--cost trade-off compared with search-based composition methods.
  </details>

- **[Verify by Doing: Co-Evolving Agent Execution and Evaluation without External Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15276)**  `arXiv:2611.15276`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Lin, Jianda Chen, Yihao Hu, Pan Wang, Hang Wang, Zhihao Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent self-improvement requires feedback about task success, yet external feedback may be unavailable at test time. Agents must then infer success from observable evidence, and incomplete verification can misguide subsequent updates. We introduce Verify by Doing (VbD), a method that co-evolves Actor and Evaluator harnesses within a single task while keeping model weights fixed. The Evaluator actively inspects artifacts and environment state with tools; its findings ground an internal signal for output selection and guide updates to both execution and verification procedures. Proposed Evaluators undergo comparative review on common candidates, while Actor updates require support from their own fresh outputs. Across AutomationBench, $\tau^3$ Banking, and selected GDPval tasks, VbD achieves the highest overall scores among the compared methods operating without external feedback, with gains of 3.3, 4.1, and 2.3 percentage points over the strongest baselines on the main backbone. Ablations support the contributions of active verification and Evaluator evolution, and selection from fixed candidate pools shows that improved verification can recover successful outputs without generating new ones.
  </details>

- **[Towards Robust Vision-Language Reasoning: A Multi-Stage Optimization Pipeline with Masked Diffusion Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15238)**  `arXiv:2611.15238`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunming Liu, Pei Huang, Yajing Sun, Huaxiao Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete diffusion generation is emerging as a competitive alternative to autoregressive generation by denoising the entire token sequence through iterative refinement. Its global planning and progressive correction capabilities are particularly well suited to autonomous driving. However, transferring this generation paradigm from general-purpose settings to driving introduces substantial domain gaps in both training and inference. To unlock its potential, we propose a multi-stage optimization pipeline for standard vision-language model architectures, without auxiliary action experts or specialized mixture-of-experts Transformer, spanning post-training and inference. During supervised fine-tuning, we optimize the representation of planning inputs to suppress hallucinations in task responses. During reinforcement learning, we introduce metric-guided diversity filtering to identify stronger learning starting points and improve policy exploration. At inference time, we propose Dynamic Autonomous Driving Decoding (DAD), which adaptively adjusts decoding modes according to scene complexity. Based on this pipeline, we develop Masked Diffusion Drive (MD-Drive), improving its performance by 29.7\% over the unoptimized model. Experiments on the NAVSIM closed loop benchmark and the nuScenes and DriveCoT open loop datasets show that MD-Drive outperforms autoregressive VLM baselines with the same architecture, while achieving a more favorable balance between performance and computational cost than systems with different architectures through discrete diffusion training and inference.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225)**  `arXiv:2611.15225`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunming Liu, Jianyi Peng, Cheng Li, Lu Hou, Pei Huang, Yajing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.
  </details>

- **[WordInferBench: Can Agents Infer How to Edit from Word Document Context?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15189)**  `arXiv:2611.15189`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayu Ding, Yupan Huang, Jinyoung Park, Yilin Jia, Wenshan Wu, Tengchao Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document editing is among the most common knowledge-work tasks, yet existing benchmarks for evaluating autonomous agents on Word document editing provide fully specified attribute-modification instructions. They do not evaluate agents' ability to infer the appropriate attributes from document context, a capability essential in real-world editing where users expect consistency without spelling out every attribute. We introduce WordInferBench, a benchmark comprising 280 base document-editing instances, each paired with three instruction conditions: descriptive (the instruction explicitly lists every attribute change), referential (the instruction points to another object as an exemplar), and inferential (the agent must examine document context to infer what attributes to apply). To evaluate contextual attribute inference, each base instance is constructed so that the target formatting is recoverable from an in-document exemplar. The instances are derived from real Word documents collected from Common Crawl and cover 13 object types and 81 attributes. We evaluate six recent coding-agent systems and find that the best-performing evaluated system, Claude Code with Opus 4.6, achieves a 62.9% task-level pass rate on descriptive variants but only 40.7% on inferential variants, indicating that current systems complete tasks less reliably when the required formatting changes must be determined from document context.
  </details>

- **[HyperSkill: Training-Free Omnimodal GRPO via Hypergraph-Indexed Skill-Library Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15178)**  `arXiv:2611.15178`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Luo, Shangkai Lin, Jinyang Wu, Xinliang Zhou, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning sharpens LLM reasoning, but parameter updates are expensive, forgetful, and opaque; training-free libraries swap gradients for a textual store but still use trajectory-level, correlation-based admission and flat structure. To address both, we propose HyperSkill, a Training-Free Omnimodal GRPO framework that keeps the backbone frozen and replaces gradient updates with auditable edits to a Hypergraph-Indexed Skill-Library, where skills are advantage-bearing nodes and compositions are hyperedges. It has three components: training-time distillation admits counterfactually checked skills and learns a protocol skill from failures; gated hypergraph retrieval injects advantage-ranked skills where a per-dataset gate admits them; and an execution protocol makes the frozen backbone commit to an answer. On 11 text, vision-language, and omnimodal benchmarks, HyperSkill is best or tied on every dataset and beats the best baseline by 13.0 average F1 points while keeping the backbone frozen. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Advancing Weighted Partial MaxSAT Solving via Differentiable GPU Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15091)**  `arXiv:2611.15091`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mingju Liu, Curie Kim, Steve Dai, Cunxi Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a GPU-CPU hybrid framework for Weighted Partial MaxSAT (WPMAX-SAT) that complements state-of-the-art CPU solvers with differentiable GPU optimization. The GPU stage directly optimizes instance-specific categorical decision parameters without a training dataset or a pretrained predictive model. The central challenge in extending differentiable optimization to WPMAX-SAT is the hard/soft clause asymmetry: hard clauses represent inviolable feasibility constraints while soft clauses carry weights. We introduce a hierarchical differentiable loss that prioritizes hard clause satisfaction before optimizing the weighted soft objective, ensuring that GPU gradient descent leans towards the constraint structure of WPMAX-SAT. Our framework transforms the GPU-optimized variable logits to high-confidence assignments and transfers them to CPU solvers via two complementary modes: partial guidance, which injects the most confident perturbed partial variable assignments as soft clauses into $N$ parallel solver instances, and phase initialization, which guides the CDCL solver's internal branching polarity with the full GPU assignment as a warm start to preserve the problem's originality. We evaluate on all 229 instances of the MSE 2024 (most recent due to cancellation in 2025) anytime weighted benchmark with the top 2 winners as baselines; our hybrid framework improves the average solution score by up to 4.9% over tt-open-wbo-inc-Glucose and 4.8% over SPB-MaxSAT-c-FPS at a 300-second time limit, with the potential to obtain better solutions surpassing the best known solution recorded in the competition evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Same Task, Different Statement: When and How Reformulation Helps LLM Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14997)**  `arXiv:2611.14997`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Xiaoyu Zhang, Chengwei Liu, Yutao Hu, Tao Zhang, Junsheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When people face a hard problem, they often restate it in a clearer way before solving it. We study whether the same habit helps large language models by rewriting the statement of a problem. A legal rewrite must keep the meaning, the constraints, and the correct answer unchanged. We test three kinds of rewrites. Decomposition makes hidden subgoals explicit. Typed Reformulation casts the task into a more suitable solving frame. Concept Reduction removes surface detail and exposes the task structure. We evaluate them on eight math, logic, and code benchmarks with eight models, all under the same chain-of-thought solving prompt. The effect is large but depends on the problem type. An oracle that picks the best rewrite after the fact raises accuracy by +27.6% on average. Yet no single rewrite helps every problem, and a mismatched rewrite can hurt. Still, the effect follows clear patterns. Each kind of rewrite fits certain problem types. A simple fixed rule learned from one set of problems transfers part of the gain to new problems. It gains +11.0% on the problems it chooses to rewrite, while blind rewriting loses 5.6% on a similar share of problems. Rewriting is therefore not a universal way to solve problems. The question is when and how to rewrite.
  </details>

- **[Early Signals, Strong Decisions: Prefix-Guided Sampling for Parallel Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965)**  `arXiv:2611.14965`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Ren, Jonathan Rosenfeld, Neil Thompson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel test-time scaling (TTS) has been shown to enhance reasoning in large language models by generating and aggregating multiple independent reasoning paths. Internal confidence scores can further improve this process by identifying paths of varying quality. However, existing methods remain computationally inefficient, as low-quality paths are fully expanded before being evaluated. In this work, we aim to improve the use of internal confidence signals for better model performance and computational efficiency. We begin by offering a new perspective on the role of confidence in aggregation, showing that it is most impactful for questions near the decision boundary. Notably, when amplified by multi-sample aggregation, even small confidence signals derived from short prefixes can meaningfully influence outcomes in these boundary cases. Building on this insight, we introduce Prefix-Guided Sampling (PreG), a method that reallocates computation by first generating short prefixes, ranking them using internal confidence scores, and then completing only the most promising candidates. This strategy reduces token usage while maintaining gains in path quality. We provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that, under a fixed compute budget, PreG strengthens the effective signal by widening the decision boundary margin. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms standard parallel TTS approaches across benchmarks, while significantly improving token efficiency and reducing overall computation.
  </details>

- **[RSI-PDEbench: A Benchmark for Self-Improving Agents in PDE Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14949)**  `arXiv:2611.14949`  `cs.AI`  
  _Binghang Lu, Lang Cao, Changhong Mou, Yige Sun, Cindy Xiangrui Kong, Zheyuan Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
