  Identifying and solving partial differential equations (PDEs) from sampled solution data often requires substantial expert effort in equation discovery, coefficient estimation, and numerical solver configuration, especially under noise and distribution shifts. Can large language model (LLM) agents automate this workflow accurately and efficiently? To investigate this question, we introduce \bench{}, a benchmark of 1,001 cases spanning 42 equation templates, including both classical and fractional PDEs. It provides three evaluation settings: Full evaluates performance on noise-free solution data; Noisy tests robustness to Gaussian noise with an RMS equal to 50\% of the clean-signal RMS; and Hard-OOD tests challenging out-of-distribution generalization to unseen equation templates and shifted coefficient ranges. We further introduce RSI-PDE, a recursively self-improving agent harness that shortlists candidate equations by residual loss, uses relative $L^2$ feedback to refine coefficients and numerical solvers, and applies a validation gate to policy and memory updates. We compare RSI-PDE with single-run baselines and native agent harnesses (Claude Code or Codex) across four frontier models. Across the three settings, RSI-PDE improves the pass rate averaged across the four models by 106.5--181.9\% relative to single-run baselines and by 3.0--19.6\% relative to native harnesses, while reducing total recorded model calls by 46.9\% across the 12 model--setting pairs. These results highlight the potential of validated recursive self-improvement in LLM agents to advance scientific computing.
  </details>

- **[Provably Optimal HBT Legalization in Seconds: Certified Optima Reveal Where the Headroom Really Lies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14928)**  `arXiv:2611.14928`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kadhir Parani, Manjunath Bezawada, Divya M, Kesana Jathin, Kolli Sarath, Sm Zobaed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid bonding terminal (HBT) legalization assigns every terminal of a face-to-face 3D IC to its own pitch-grid site with minimum total Manhattan displacement. Open3DBench reports that a reinforcement-learning (RL) legalizer reduces displacement by 19-22% relative to a sliding-window (SW) heuristic, but none of its legalizers is publicly released. We ask how much displacement any legalizer can still recover. We build an open benchmark of 21 design/pitch instances from Open3DBench placement data, with reconstructed greedy, SW (ViaLegal-style) and BTAssign-style baselines, and GEO-Exact, an exact solver that runs successive shortest augmenting paths over lazily generated spatial candidate edges. An independent dual certificate proves its assignment optimal on all 21 instances (relative primal-dual gap at most $8.2 \times 10^{-16}$). Against these optima, our SW reconstruction with ViaLegal's default configuration is on average 0.011% above optimal (worst 0.119%), while on the 16 contended instances BTAssign-style, a density-guided heuristic (DGHL) and greedy are 3.0%, 6.9% and 64.5% above. Headroom does appear outside this regime: with 2x2 windows SW reaches 34.1%, and above about 58% utilization, on three of six swept designs, it reaches 21.1% on bp, 27.5% on swerv and 8.9% on jpeg (217 further certified instances, and ten bp points checked against an independent exact solver). If the published 19-22% gain were measured on instances like ours, SW would have to be at least 24-28% above optimal on average, far more than any SW variant we tested at nominal pitch; the RL paper's own comparison with the Hungarian optimum puts its SW 3.05% above optimal on average. GEO-Exact is also about an order of magnitude faster than DGHL and SW in our harness (geometric means 8.1x and 40x; a compiled core against Python baselines on a shared server). Code, optimal values and certificates are released.
  </details>

- **[GenPFN: An In-Context Tabular Foundation Model Approach for Augmenting Small Datasets in Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14804)**  `arXiv:2611.14804`  `cs.AI`  
  _Noah J. Bagazinski, Cyril Picard, Faez Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Costly simulations and experiments limit the data available for engineering design. This paper proposes GenPFN, a training-free candidate generator guided by a pretrained tabular Prior-Data Fitted Network (TabPFN) classifier with fixed weights. The context contains evaluated designs labeled desired (Class 1) or undesired (Class 0). GenPFN interpolates between desired designs and refines candidates using gated classifier gradients and Gaussian noise. Each candidate is evaluated using the problem's constraints and objective functions. Evaluated desired designs and their objective values augment the context for regression training.Evaluation spans fourteen selected problems, including a two-parameter benchmark and a 44-parameter hull problem. In paired comparisons, GenPFN with TabPFN v1 achieves higher class satisfaction and lower realism distance than the tested adversarial, variational-autoencoder and diffusion generators. The variational-autoencoder generator provides greater coverage and diversity. Relative to the synthetic minority over-sampling technique (SMOTE), GenPFN provides lower coverage distance and greater diversity. Among observed paired regression fits, augmentation reduces estimated AutoGluon validation mean squared error by 62.1% relative to context-only training. A hull-stern case study yields 99.3% propeller-compatible candidates versus 1.4% among randomly sampled context designs. Generation followed by evaluation produces sample sets that augment limited datasets and support training more accurate predictive models.
  </details>

- **[When Should Semantic Priors Enter Partially-Identified Causal Discovery?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14736)**  `arXiv:2611.14736`  `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Saad Bin Ather, Valentin Six, Lancelot Da Costa, Tommaso Salvatori, Bernhard Scholkopf_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Observational causal discovery often leaves causal di- rections unresolved or unstable. Large language mod- els (LLMs) can provide semantic information to resolve these directions, but incorrect proposals can exclude the true causal graph and invalidate downstream inference. We study three separate decisions: where to allocate semantic queries, which proposals to admit, and which causal graphs to retain. Our framework distinguishes population non-identifiability from finite-sample insta- bility, evaluates semantic orientation against statistical and random controls, and examines calibrated admis- sion and error-tolerant DAG retention. Experiments across benchmark networks show that semantic infor- mation improves recovery over coin-flip orientations, but targeting unstable edges has no established ad- vantage over matched-random allocation. The tested admission certificate cannot support nontrivial admis- sion with the available calibration tasks. Moreover, downstream retention depends on reference adequacy: learned references contain the true DAG in only three of 80 planned tasks. These findings separate the value of semantic information from the reliability of its alloca- tion, admission, and retention, and identify calibration size and reference adequacy as key limitations.
  </details>

- **[Together Compiler: Continual Compilation for AI Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14681)**  `arXiv:2611.14681`  `cs.AI`  
  _Reyna Abhyankar, Dylan Lim, Drew Wadsworth, Venkata Hasith Vattikuti, YuTong Julia Zhang, Jung Hwan Heo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today's AI workloads are rapidly changing: new models are released more often, model architectures are increasingly diverse, and traffic distributions change frequently. The challenge we face at Together AI is continual optimization for these changing workloads. Compiling a deployment into an optimized forward pass is a challenge that is well-suited for agent hill-climbing. Existing systems struggle with continual compilation due to a tradeoff between measurement throughput and fidelity. Many systems use agents to optimize individual kernels from the inference forward pass using offline benchmarks, but timings are low-fidelity because of data-dependent behavior in primitives like mixture-of-experts (MoE). On the other hand, systems use agents to directly optimize end-to-end engines but suffer from slow engine launch times. To address these challenges, we build Together Compiler (TKCC), the first truly continual agent loop designed for AI inference optimization. To address the throughput-fidelity tradeoff, we propose: (1) a Tiered Persistent Engine that decouples engine launch overheads from forward pass computation; (2) a cost model to select the parallelism configurations that seed the optimization process. We also conduct extensive ablations on agent context management and present our insights on deploying TKCC in production. TKCC outperforms highly-optimized hand-written Megakernels on the Llama 1B workhorse workload by up to 1.7x and the best open source engines on the latest frontier models (Kimi K3, GLM 5.3, MiniMax M3.1 Flash, DeepSeek v4.1 Flash) across hardware platforms by up to 2.6x higher TPS on the InferenceX benchmark. TKCC also sets the state-of-the-art performance at batch size 1, with 1111 TPS on M3.1 Flash and 1205 TPS on DeepSeek v4.1 Flash. Finally, TKCC generalizes beyond frontier language model inference. TKCC serves trillions of tokens across our production fleet.
  </details>

- **[DForkTree: Accelerating Speculative Decoding with Fork-Aware Block Diffusion Draft Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12356)**  `arXiv:2611.12356`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhicheng Zheng, Cheng Zhang, Chenkai Tang, Tianxiang Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding speeds up language models by having a lightweight drafter propose future tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Block diffusion drafters such as DFlash predict an entire block in one forward pass, and DDTree builds draft trees directly from their per-position distributions. Each distribution implicitly assumes that the drafter's own greedy continuation is accepted, whereas a draft tree improves over a chain only on branches that leave this greedy path. Once a path forks, the drafter's top-1 accuracy drops from 89% to 18% and its top candidates often miss the true token. Re-running the drafter from the fork token largely restores its accuracy, showing that the errors stem from conditioning on an outdated prefix rather than from limited model capacity. In this paper, we introduce DForkTree, a training-free method that re-anchors each branch on its own tokens. It adjusts the drafter's scores with pointwise mutual information from target-generated n-grams, weighted more heavily after a fork, and a layer-wise GPU search recovers the exact optimal tree under these scores. On Qwen3.5-4B and 9B, DForkTree outperforms all baselines on every benchmark, by up to 41.5% over DFlash and 15.1% over DDTree, with average speedups of up to 4.75x over autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[Don't Blame The Preferences: Examining The Emergence of Sycophancy in LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10045)**  `arXiv:2611.10045`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Peter Yang, Catherine Zehr, Pranav Bijith, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.
  </details>

- **[Axion 1.0: Open Agent Foundation Models, Simplified](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03233)**  `arXiv:2611.03233`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuqi Jia, Yunjie Tian, Jiayu Zheng, Yijun Liang, Jingchen Sun, Mengyu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Axion 1.0, a fully open agent foundation model that combines strong and broad agentic capabilities with a remarkably simple training recipe. Axion 1.0 is trained in only three stages: two lightweight supervised fine-tuning stages followed by reinforcement learning, without dedicated mid-training, direct preference optimization (DPO), or on-policy distillation (OPD). The first SFT stage develops strong self-contained reasoning through model-aware data construction, where we synthesize challenging problems and adversarially strengthen them based on the base model's own successful solution strategies. The second stage extends these capabilities to tool-dependent reasoning through a scalable pipeline for constructing diverse and automatically verifiable tasks grounded in real-world data sources and deterministic computation. Finally, reinforcement learning further strengthens long-horizon reasoning and interaction with external tools and environments. Despite this compact training recipe, Axion 1.0 achieves strong performance across a broad suite of agentic benchmarks and is competitive with leading commercial models on several of them. Equally importantly, Axion 1.0 is open beyond model weights: we release the complete recipe, including data construction, generation, filtering, verification, prompts, and training code, enabling the entire development process to be reproduced and extended. Axion 1.0 demonstrates that strong agent foundation models can be both open and simple, offering a practical and reproducible path toward advancing open agentic intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Data-Scarce, Multi-Granular, Multimodal: Benchmark Design for Biosynthetic Gene Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02275)**  `arXiv:2611.02275`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hanlin Xiao, Eriko Takano, Mauricio A. Álvarez, Rainer Breitling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biosynthetic gene clusters (BGCs) are co-located genes that encode the biosynthesis of specialized metabolites, an important source of antibiotics, anticancer agents, immunosuppressants, and agricultural bioactives. Deep learning, particularly self-supervised foundation models, has spurred interest in BGC sequence modeling, but evaluation has lagged behind: existing benchmarks often rely on coarse classification over small curated datasets, providing limited discrimination among models and insight into downstream utility. We propose a benchmark-design framework in which an informative benchmark should be discriminative, separating models with statistical confidence, and applicable, spanning scientifically meaningful tasks under different levels of generalization. We instantiate this framework as BGC-Bench, which derives five tasks from MIBiG, ranging from A-domain substrate prediction to BGC-molecule retrieval, evaluates them under genotype- and phenotype-shift splits, includes an active-learning setting for label-scarce discovery, and is easily extensible to new tasks and encoders. BGCs further provide a shared testbed across granularities and modalities: BGC-Bench compares twelve encoders on nucleotide, amino-acid, or Pfam-domain sequences, including compositional baselines for each input type, and combines BGC and chemical encoders for cross-modal retrieval. No single representation dominates: the best granularity depends on the biological scale of the task, and pretrained encoders do not consistently outperform other representations across tasks and splits. Active-learning experiments show that most tasks remain data-limited, encoder rankings change with labeling budget, and acquisition strategies yield modest gains. These results show that BGC model evaluation requires task-diverse, shift-aware benchmarks, and the framework can be generalized to benchmark design beyond BGCs.
  </details>

- **[Patient-specific rubric-based evaluation of large language models across longitudinal cancer care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35847)**  `arXiv:2610.35847`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei-Wei Zhang, Pengkai Wang, Yan Li, Zhijie Sang, Guan-Qun Zhou, Jun-Yan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise in specialty medicine, yet existing evaluations largely focus on isolated clinical encounters rather than assessing the distinct clinical responsibilities along longitudinal care pathways. We introduce NPC-RubricBench, a phase-aware benchmark for evaluating multistage clinical reasoning across the cancer care continuum, using nasopharyngeal carcinoma as a defined disease context. Curated from real-world records of 405 patients at two cancer centers, it comprises 941 evaluation units across six sequential care stages, from work-up through follow-up, and 17,688 patient-specific criteria across five dimensions. Each assessment is restricted to clinical evidence available at the corresponding decision point. Across 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs, we find strong diagnostic scores did not ensure reliable downstream care. Decision Quality ranged from 56.8 to 94.7 in diagnosis on a 0-100 scale, but dropped to 8.9-56.0 in staging and spanned 32.4-94.2 in treatment. Crucially, failure modes differed across phases: explicit decision errors predominated in anatomical staging (48.2%), whereas decision omissions were more frequent during initial work-up (33.4%) and post-treatment follow-up (30.4%). Varying query granularity revealed a trade-off between decision quality and other clinical requirements. Broad open-ended requests reduced decision quality across all 30 models, whereas more detailed, checklist-like prompts improved decision quality but were accompanied by lower evidence faithfulness and safety adherence. These findings highlight the need to assess clinical reliability against the distinct clinical requirements of each phase along the longitudinal care pathway. Finally, NPC-RubricBench informs the design of stage-aware clinical guardrails and offers a reusable blueprint for evaluating LLMs across other cancers and chronic diseases.
  </details>

- **[IRON-Meme: A Benchmark for Structured Understanding of Identities and Roles in Online Meme Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33097)**  `arXiv:2610.33097`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriella Bollici, Stefano De Giorgis, Wouter Van Atteveldt, Filip Ilievski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As polarization grows on social media, understanding how political memes shape online narratives has become increasingly important. Previous research has relied on either qualitative frameworks that are hard to scale or computational methods that lack theoretical grounding. Both approaches treat narrative categories in isolation and focus exclusively on moral portrayals of identity. To address these limitations, we introduce a framework grounded in social science theories, encompassing three categories: social identities (such as gender, politics, and ethnicity), viewpoints (ingroup and outgroup perspectives), and narrative roles (moral character, intelligence, appearance, and power). Building on this framework, we present IRON-Meme, a human-annotated benchmark comprising 1,110 political memes across substantially more templates than existing benchmarks. Our analysis of IRON-Meme shows that most memes assign derogatory roles and that identities, roles, and templates are systematically interconnected. Evaluation of nine vision-language models indicates that frontier-scale and reasoning models achieve the highest performance, while accuracy on social identities and narrative roles decreases for less frequent labels. Incorporating template background knowledge does not enhance narrative role classification, suggesting that meme interpretation relies on how memes are adapted for specific communicative purposes. We make IRON-Meme and our code publicly available.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu, Xing Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HarnessNet: Feedback-driven Hierarchical Joint Harness Evolution for Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15221)**  `arXiv:2610.15221`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaoyuan Qiu, Sachith Seneviratne, Yu Xia, Saman Halgamuge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents can evolve by using environment feedback to revise their harnesses, the software managing model inputs, tool use, memory, and execution, while keeping foundation models frozen. Existing studies focus on single agents, leaving joint harness evolution in multi-agent systems (MAS) underexplored. A single global optimizer faces growing context and workload as the system scales, while access restrictions may prevent sharing agent internals or system-wide feedback. We propose HarnessNet, a framework for hierarchical harness evolution in MAS. Its basic unit, Agentron, comprises an evolvable forward harness, a foundation model, and a local optimizer that can observe and modify only that harness. Agentrons form a layered graph through which environment feedback propagates backward, with upstream optimizers receiving node-specific feedback from the downstream layer. This decomposes joint harness evolution into smaller, coordinated tasks with separate contexts, reducing individual optimizers' context and workload while keeping access local. Optimizers need not access all agent trajectories or raw environment feedback. Experiments on AgentBench with multiple foundation models demonstrate the feasibility of training HarnessNet. Compared with a single global optimizer, HarnessNet achieves comparable performance gains while respecting local privacy and requiring fewer tokens and less time per optimizer call. A migration study shows that the evolved harnesses remain jointly effective after replacing the foundation models, suggesting transferability across models.
  </details>

- **[EmbodiedRSI: Active Continual Robot Learning Through Hypothesis-Guided Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10498)**  `arXiv:2610.10498`  `cs.AI`  
  _Python Song, Zhixuan Liang, Kelsey Fu, Mengdi Wang, Junfeng Yang, Shilong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot foundation models provide strong visuomotor control, yet their performance can degrade when object positions or task instructions change. Further improvements often require post-training on substantial robot data, which can be costly to collect through methods such as teleoperation. Agentic harnesses can adapt around the model, but current self-evolving harnesses use robot trials inefficiently when deciding which code and skill changes to pursue. We introduce EmbodiedRSI, a self-evolving agentic harness that autonomously decides where to explore next and turns the resulting physical interaction into improved code and skills. EmbodiedRSI realizes this through a Fast-Slow Dual-System Architecture, in which competing code and skill hypotheses are maintained in a Hypothesis Graph. Value-of-Information Experiment Selection chooses physical experiments that can distinguish these hypotheses. Their outcomes guide Code-Skill Co-Evolution. The Slow System builds Hierarchical Memory, and Reward-Grounded Memory Learning selects effective memory according to their value for later Fast-System improvement. On RoboCasa365, EmbodiedRSI reaches 77.0% overall success and 71.3% on Composite-Unseen, compared with 40.1% for the best baseline. EmbodiedRSI also reaches 86.8% overall success on LIBERO-Pro. Beyond benchmark performance, EmbodiedRSI transfers zero-shot to real-world robot, achieving 71.3% overall success across multiple challenging tasks.
  </details>

- **[Chronocooked: A Benchmark for Interval Timing in Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16666)**  `arXiv:2608.16666`  `cs.AI`  
  _Amrapali Pednekar, Alvaro Garrido-Perez, Yara Khaluf, Pieter Simoens_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interval timing is considered an important aspect of human cognition. As artificial agents increasingly function alongside humans, their interval timing abilities need to be studied. However, research in this area remains limited and scattered. This paper presents Chronocooked, a reinforcement learning (RL) benchmark environment that enables a systematic study of interval timing abilities in RL agents. The suite comprises cooking scenarios involving interval timing tasks inspired by psychology literature. Each task is accompanied by evaluation metrics to study different aspects of interval timing in RL agents, namely, task performance, human-like timing and scalability. We report baselines using a non-recurrent model (CNN), a recurrent model (LSTM), and a biologically inspired recurrent model (CTRNN). The CNN model showed limited ability to perform the basic timing tasks. In contrast, both recurrent models with sufficient memory successfully performed the timing tasks, exhibited temporal control in the Fixed Interval (FI) task, and qualitatively reproduced human-like psychometric curves in the Temporal Bisection task. However, no quantitative evidence of human-like timing behavior was found. A systematic study of interval timing in artificial agents may offer complementary perspectives for improving task performance in fields like human-robot interactions (HRI), while also contributing to broader research on time perception.
  </details>

- **[PAL-Bench: Evidence-Grounded Profile Reconstruction from Longitudinal Personal Albums](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16175)**  `arXiv:2606.16175`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qiwei Yan, Zhiqiang Yuan, Zexi Jia, Nanxing Hu, Kailin Lyu, Jie Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Longitudinal personal albums are weak-schema multimodal databases: noisy perceptual records whose key facts require joins across faces, text, timestamps, locations, and repeated events. Existing visual, video, document, and lifelog benchmarks test sub-problems, but not album-scale profile reconstruction with social identity binding and evidence citation. Benchmarking this task is difficult because the ground truth needed for evaluation--owner profiles, social graphs, face-name maps, and evidence provenance--is private state that real albums cannot safely release. We introduce PAL-Bench, a controlled benchmark for evidence-grounded reconstruction under a public-record contract. Its Evidence Compiler builds latent private worlds, programs target-level evidence paths, renders album pixels, re-measures them through perception pipelines, and exports audited public/private views. Agents receive only perception-derived public records; targets, identifier maps, and evidence paths remain hidden. PAL-Bench contains 50 synthetic users, 36,659 public photo records, and 2,799 targets over owner facts, identities, and relations. A privacy-preserving audit with 10 participants confirms that PAL-Bench evidence structures match real private albums, though equivalent releases remain privacy-prohibitive. Across seven systems and two compute-matched diagnostics, a seven-metric protocol reveals a gap between plausible profile summarization and faithful social reconstruction: systems recover some owner facts but struggle with recurring identities and evidence citation. PAL-TRACE, a reference framework that freezes identity bindings before owner-fact mining, performs best but leaves hard identity resolution far from solved. PAL-Bench provides a testbed for perceptual entity resolution, multimodal data integration, temporal evidence aggregation, and provenance-aware structured prediction.
  </details>

- **[Synthesizing Human Trajectory Anomalies: An LLM-Driven Framework with Map-Constrained Routing and Sensor Noise Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10314)**  `arXiv:2606.10314`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yueyang Liu, Joon-Seok Kim, Andreas Züfle_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although the study of human trajectory anomalies is vital for spatial data mining, empirical research remains constrained by the lack of ground-truth datasets. Despite the availability of several real-world and simulated human trajectory datasets, most only include normal mobility patterns and lack annotated anomalies. This limitation primarily arises because anomalous events are rare in the real world and difficult to collect. Furthermore, collecting large-scale mobility data is hindered by strict privacy regulations and acquisition costs. To overcome these limitations and establish an explainable human trajectory anomalies dataset with annotated ground truth, we introduce an end-to-end generative framework designed to synthesize context-aware trajectory anomalies at scale. Unlike existing models, our framework applies real-world physical constraints on baseline simulated trajectories. We employ Large Language Model (LLM) agents to systematically inject semantically meaningful behavioral anomalies such as irregular out-of-distribution check-ins and skipped routine visits. To ensure the spatial validity, the system leverages a map-constrained routing module to recalculate the physical transitions between these LLM agent-modified staypoints. Moreover, to narrow the simulation-to-reality gap, we augment the resulting trajectories with a context-aware spatial noise model, parameterized by environmental and location-specific variables, to emulate heterogeneous GPS sensor degradation. Through this pipeline, our framework produces a CTI & EBI formatted mobility dataset with annotated anomalies, providing a benchmark for downstream spatial data mining tasks.
  </details>

- **[LEAP: Supercharging LLMs for Formal Mathematics with Agentic Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03303)**  `arXiv:2606.03303`  `cs.AI`  
  _Po-Nien Kung, Linfeng Song, Dawsen Hwang, Jinsung Yoon, Chun-Liang Li, Simone Severini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit strong informal mathematical reasoning but struggle to generate mechanically verifiable proofs in formal languages like Lean. We present LEAP, an agentic framework that enables general-purpose foundation models to achieve state-of-the-art performance on automated formal theorem proving. LEAP leverages foundation model capabilities, such as informal reasoning, instruction following, and iterative self-refinement. By decomposing complex problems into smaller units, the system bridges formal proof construction with informal blueprints through continuous interaction with the Lean compiler. To provide a rigorous evaluation beyond increasingly saturated benchmarks, we introduce Lean-IMO-Bench, a benchmark of IMO-style problems formalized in Lean, with short statements yet highly non-routine and multi-step proofs across a wide range of difficulty levels. Empirically, on the latest 2025 Putnam Competition, an annual mathematics competition for undergraduate students in North America, LEAP solves all 12 problems, matching recent breakthroughs by frontier formal mathematical models. On Lean-IMO-Bench, LEAP boosts the one-shot formal solve rate of general-purpose LLMs from below 10% to 70%, notably surpassing the 48% benchmark set by a specialized, gold-medal-caliber IMO system. Furthermore, we demonstrate LEAP's research-level utility by autonomously formalizing complex proofs for open combinatorial challenges, including a verified proof for a key subproblem in Knuth's Hamiltonian decomposition of even-order Cayley graphs.
  </details>

- **[POIROT: Agents as their own auditors for failure attribution in large language model multi-agent systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02282)**  `arXiv:2606.02282`  `cs.AI`  
  _Iñaki Dellibarda Varela, R. Sendra-Arranz, Pablo Romero-Sorozabal, J.M. Valverde-García, Annemarie F. Laudanski, Álvaro Gutiérrez, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) are kept out of safety-critical domains by emergent failures that are hard to detect and to attribute. Current evaluation relies on a centralised evaluator, human or LLM judge, that must master the domain and hold the whole interaction history in one context. We propose that the agents executing a task can themselves attribute its failures. We introduce POIROT (Phased Oversight via Introspection, Review and Opinion Tallying), a modular protocol in which each agent casts a private vote within a hazard space derived from the system architecture, and the votes are aggregated into a single attribution. Before the vote, phases are added that progressively increase the interaction between agents, from independent self-assessment to sharing the self-assessments and peer interrogation. We build the protocol phase by phase, measure the contribution of each phase through an ablation, and compare every configuration with a single-LLM evaluator. We evaluate one proprietary and two open-weight LLMs on the Who&When benchmark and on BLAME (Benchmark for Localising Agent Malfunctions Effectively), a new benchmark for fault attribution in clinical rehabilitation and algorithmic trading systems. Agent-based attribution outperforms the single-LLM evaluator, but the benefit comes from independent self-assessment, not from interaction: sharing the assessments makes agents converge on the same, often wrong, culprit, and interrogation does not recover the loss. Without sharing and interrogation, the protocol outperforms the evaluator in all nine model-benchmark configurations (up to +50 percentage points). POIROT and BLAME are released as open source.
  </details>

- **[From XXLTraffic to EvoXXLTraffic: Scaling Traffic Forecasting to Sensor-Evolving Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29768)**  `arXiv:2605.29768`  `cs.AI`  
  _Du Yin, Hao Xue, Arian Prabowo, Shuang Ao, Flora Salim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic forecasting benchmarks commonly fix the sensor set, although deployed sensing networks change their coverage over time. We present EvoXXLTraffic, a sensor-evolving extension of the XXLTraffic dataset family, with yearly sensor catalogs, persistent station IDs, flow matrices, and geographic graph snapshots across nine California PeMS districts, spanning up to 27 years. The benchmark separates all-sensor forecasting from supervised adaptation to sensors absent from the preceding year. We evaluate 13 baseline adaptations on PEMS04 with five seeds and PEMS05 with three seeds, and seven of them on the other seven districts with three seeds. Each yearly task uses 31 stored days, explicit observation masks, and chronological evaluation. Under the frozen configurations, PECPM has the lowest full-horizon mean MAE on both all-sensor and new-node populations on PEMS04, while Online-AN leads both populations on PEMS05. Among the seven methods run on every district, Online-AN has the lowest all-sensor mean MAE in eight of the nine districts, and Online-NN has the lowest new-node mean MAE in six. A matched control on PEMS05 and PEMS06 finds that training on the evolving catalog changes mean MAE on the sensors present in every 2018-2025 catalog by less than 1% in either direction. Method ordering depends on district, evaluation population, horizon, and metric, and seed variation does not establish statistical significance. The benchmark documents implementation adaptations and uses retrospective geographic graphs, limiting claims about original-paper rankings and prospective deployment. Inherited fixed-sensor gap and hourly/daily tasks provide complementary temporal settings. Code and results:this https URL. Data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoScientists: Self-Organizing Agent Teams for Long-Running Scientific Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28655)**  `arXiv:2605.28655`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shanghua Gao, Ada Fang, Marinka Zitnik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific research proceeds through iterative cycles of hypothesis generation, experiment design, execution, and revision. AI agents can automate parts of this process, but existing approaches typically follow a single research trajectory or coordinate through a central planner with fixed objectives. As a result, they struggle to sustain parallel exploration, adapt as experimental evidence changes, or preserve knowledge of failed directions over long-running experiments. We introduce AutoScientists, a decentralized team of AI agents for long-running computational scientific experimentation. Agents interpret a shared experimental state, self-organize into teams around promising hypotheses, critique proposals before using experimental compute, and share successes and failures to reduce redundant exploration. Under matched experimental budgets, AutoScientists improves over prior AI agents across biomedical machine learning, language-model training optimization, and protein fitness prediction. On BioML-Bench, spanning biomedical imaging, protein engineering, single-cell omics, and drug discovery, AutoScientists achieves a mean leaderboard percentile of 74.4% across 24 tasks, improving over the strongest AI agent by +8.33%. On GPT training optimization, AutoScientists reaches a target validation bits-per-byte 1.9x faster than Autoresearch and continues discovering improvements from a starting champion where the single-agent approach finds none (7 vs. 0 accepted improvements). On ProteinGym fitness prediction, AutoScientists discovers a method for ACE2-Spike binding that improves over the current state-of-the-art model by +12.5% in Spearman correlation. Applied without modification across all 217 ProteinGym assays, the same method improves over the prior state of the art by +6.5% (Spearman correlation).
  </details>

- **[FrontierOR: Benchmarking LLMs' Capacity for Efficient Algorithm Design in Large-Scale Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25246)**  `arXiv:2605.25246`  `cs.AI`  
  _Minwei Kong, Chonghe Jiang, Ao Qu, Wenbin Ouyang, Zhaoming Zeng, Xiaotong Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for optimization modeling and solver-code generation, yet practical operations research and optimization problems often require a harder capability: designing scalable algorithms that exploit problem structure and outperform direct formulation-and-solve baselines. Existing benchmarks are limited to small or simplified examples far below real-world scale and complexity. We introduce FrontierOR, the first literature-grounded benchmark targeting LLM-generated algorithm efficiency on realistic large-scale optimization problems. FrontierOR includes 180 diverse tasks, each derived from a paper published in a top-tier operations research venue, with standardized instances and a hidden, expert-verified evaluation suite. We evaluate recent LLMs' one-shot algorithm generation and improvements achieved through several self-evolving frameworks. The results show that current models still struggle to move from executable formulations to efficient optimization algorithms. Even the strongest LLM succeeds on only 39% of Full-set task instances, where success is defined as returning a feasible solution with an objective gap of at most 1% relative to Gurobi within its runtime. On the Hard subset, test-time evolution raises this proportion from 51% for the strongest one-shot model to 78%. FrontierOR provides a continuously maintained testbed for evaluating LLM-designed optimization algorithms under a shared protocol and controlled computational settings. Code and data are publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Formal Conjectures: An Open and Evolving Benchmark for Verified Discovery in Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13171)**  `arXiv:2605.13171`  `cs.AI`  
  _Moritz Firsching, Paul Lezeau, Salvatore Mercuri, Miklós Z. Horváth, Yaël Dillies, Calle Sönne, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As automated reasoning systems advance rapidly, there is a growing need for research-level formal mathematical problems to accurately evaluate their capabilities. To address this, we present Formal Conjectures (this https URL), an evolving benchmark of currently 7559 mathematical problem statements formalized in Lean 4. Sourced from areas of active mathematical research, the dataset features 1642 open research conjectures without known solutions for mathematical proof discovery, 2923 solved problems for proof auto-formalization, and 2994 supporting statements. Notably, the repository provides a structured interface connecting mathematicians who formalize and clarify problems with the AI systems and humans attempting to solve them. Demonstrating its immediate utility, the benchmark has already been leveraged for new mathematical discoveries, including the resolution of open research conjectures, and for the formal confirmation of major newly announced results. We describe our approach to ensuring the correctness of these formalizations in a collaborative open-source project where contributions stem from an active community. In this framework, AI-generated proofs and disproofs serve as a valuable auditing mechanism to iteratively improve the fidelity of the benchmark. Finally, we provide a standardized evaluation setup and report baseline results on frozen evaluation subsets, demonstrating a climbable signal that measures the current frontier of automated reasoning on research-level mathematics.
  </details>

- **[From Noise to Diversity: Random Embedding Injection in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11936)**  `arXiv:2605.11936`  `cs.AI`  
  _Heejun Kim, Seungpil Lee, Jewon Yeom, Jaewon Sok, Seonghyeon Park, Jeongjae Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent soft prompt research has tried to improve reasoning by inserting trained vectors into LLM inputs, yet whether the gain comes from the learned content or from the act of injection itself has not been carefully separated. We study Random Soft Prompts (RSPs), which drop the training step entirely and append a freshly drawn sequence of random embedding vectors to the input. Each RSP vector is sampled from an isotropic Gaussian fitted to the entrywise mean and variance of the pretrained embedding table; the sequence carries no learned content, and yet reaches accuracy comparable to optimized soft prompts on math reasoning benchmarks in several settings. The mechanism unfolds in two stages: because attention has to absorb a never-seen-before random position, the distribution over the first few generated tokens flattens and reasoning trajectories branch, and as generation continues this influence dilutes naturally so the response commits to a single completion. We show that during inference RSPs lift early-stage token diversity and, combined with temperature sampling, widen Pass@N, the probability that at least one out of N attempts is correct. Beyond inference, we carry the same effect into DAPO training and demonstrate practical gains. Our contributions are: (i) RSP isolates the simplest form of soft prompt -- training-free, freshly resampled -- providing a unified lens for the structural effect of injection that variants otherwise differing in training and form all share; (ii) a theoretical and empirical validation of the underlying mechanism; and (iii) an extension from inference to training.
  </details>

- **[MonitorBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Chain-of-Thought Monitorability in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28590)**  `arXiv:2603.28590`  `cs.AI`  
  _Han Wang, Yifan Sun, Brian Ko, Mann Talati, Jiawen Gong, Zimeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate chains of thought (CoTs) that are not always causally responsible for their final outputs. When such a mismatch occurs, the CoT no longer faithfully reflects the actual reasons (i.e., decision-critical factors) driving the model's behavior, leading to the reduced CoT monitorability problem. This limits the use of CoTs for reliable oversight. However, a comprehensive and fully open-source benchmark for thoroughly evaluating CoT monitorability remains lacking. To address this gap, we propose MonitorBench, a systematic benchmark for evaluating CoT monitorability in LLMs. MonitorBench provides: (1) a diverse set of 1,540 test instances with carefully designed decision-critical factors across 19 tasks spanning 7 categories to characterize when CoTs can be used to monitor the factors driving LLM behavior; and (2) two prompting stress-test settings to quantify the extent to which CoT monitorability can be degraded. Extensive experiments show that CoT monitorability is a conditional property affected by the evaluated LLM, monitor LLM, and task characteristics. Across these factors, monitorability is higher when decision-critical factors shape the intermediate reasoning process, rather than merely influencing the final answer. Under stress-test prompting, most evaluated LLMs can intentionally reduce monitorability, mainly on tasks where decision-critical factors are not structurally required by the reasoning process. Overall, MonitorBench provides a basis for further research on AI control, reasoning faithfulness, stress-test monitorability, and monitoring scaffords. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>
